6                                                                                      Frank Vermeulen




    1. General introduction
    Frank Vermeulen



    T     he idea of looking at a valley as a microcosm suitable to study long term processes of settlement
          dynamics and landscape change produced by human occupation is not new in field archaeology.
    Such settlement studies in the ‘longue durée’, as were long since advocated for Mediterranean
    historical studies by the Annales historian Braudel (Braudel 1949), and since the early 1990s by many
    followers within the traditions of archaeological survey in the Mediterranean (e.g. Bintliff 1991;
    Barker 1995), have by now fully proven their ‘raison d’être’. Even if the application of Braudel’s
    vision on historical change in three timescales – long-term change of ‘structures’ and landscapes,
    medium-term change of demographic and economic ‘conjonctures’ and cycles, and short-term
    change of human actions and ‘événements’ – needs some adaptation when applied to archaeological
    investigations (Fletcher 1992, Bintliff 2004), this framework has its full merits when looking at the
    development of human settlement and the impact this has on the moulding by man of an economically
    viable and sustainable landscape. Many questions concerning (de-)urbanisation, regional building
    traditions, the colonisation of newly conquered land, the rural infill of the countryside in times of
    need or prosperity, the change in economic production patterns, the impact of local or international
    commerce, the acculturation of communities living in the valley, etc. can only be tackled if a wider
    spatial approach to the archaeology of a region is being chosen. Only when such spatial data for a
    sufficiently large area are available can crucial understanding of past demographic evolutions, of
    changing patterns in human subsistence, and of the nature of the relationship between increasing or
    deflating social complexity and regional development be understood.
      Regions constantly change through time, in a dialectic relationship with both wider and local
    political, cultural, socio-economical, demographic and environmental contexts. This constant
    formation process of the landscape can be steered by brusque human intervention in the short
    term, such as with colonial foundations, but can also result from medium-term economic and
    demographic developments, or even from very gradual evolutions which seem to be part of ‘normal’
    aggregations within ‘human’ landscape dynamics. Generally the effect of rather brusque events can
    also be measured over the medium-term timescale, while the gradual effect of certain processes
    like urbanisation can become part of very long-term cycles involving the development of powerful
    population centres mastering the surrounding rural territories, but also town disintegration in times
    of instability and insufficient management of the land.
      When in 2000 the Potenza Valley Survey project, of which this book is a reflection, started, the aims
    for looking at such settlement dynamics in the ‘longue durée’, with the protohistoric and Roman
    periods as chronological focus, were already defined and pursued in several regions of Italy. The
    increasing use of landscape survey archaeology in the Italian peninsula, as in some other parts of
    the Mediterranean, had already enabled scholars to study diachronic patterns of rural habitation and
    land use with much greater precision than before. All kinds of processes of regional transformation
    from a relatively undifferentiated landscape of farms, hamlets and possibly villages into a settlement
    network dominated by large and central population agglomerations supported by their agricultural
    hinterlands had been analysed and explained, leading also to some interesting and much needed
    intra-regional comparisons (e.g. Witcher 2006; Attema et al. 2010). The study of the increasing
    complexity of settlements and the underlying transformation of both society and landscape that
    can be observed by way of a systematic survey approach was however still at its beginning in the
    central Adriatic region of Italy. As in other regions of this remarkable Mediterranean peninsula, the
    modern region of Marche had known several survey projects in the past decades, but the activities of
    archaeological prospections achieved here were still too diverse in strategy, intensity and resolution
    for a good understanding of the mechanisms and character of settlement and landscape dynamics
1. General introduction                                                                                     7




throughout the late prehistoric and early historic periods of change concerned here. The first
archaeological survey projects started in the late 1970s, within the territories of the Roman towns
of Cingulum (Percossi, Silvestrini 1986), Cupra Maritima (Ciarrocchi 1999) and Asculum (Conta
1982), but they covered very large areas and were of a most extensive nature. Later field survey
work, finalised in good cartography, was often initiated at the archaeological departments of the
universities of Bologna, Urbino and Macerata. Surveys to be mentioned here, were mostly based
on bibliographical research, but were later followed up in the field, such as in the valleys of Misa,
Nevola and Cesano (Dall’Aglio et al. 1991, 2007, 2012; Giorgi, Lepore 2010), and work published
within the framework of the Forma Italiae series around the Roman town of Trea (Moscatelli
1988). More intensive prospections were conducted in the 1980s and early 1990s in the valleys
of the rivers Metauro (Luni 1993; Ermeti 1993), Marecchia (Monacchi 1996, 1999) and Foglia
(Monacchi 1987), and in the territories of the Roman towns of Urbs Salvia, Pausulae and Cluana
(Vettorazzi 1987; Moscatelli, Vettorazzi 1988). In the 1990s also fairly intensive prospections
were achieved in the ager Pisaurensis (Campagnoli 1999) and in the Esino valley (Campagnoli et al.
2009). From around 2000 onwards – shortly after the start of our own program in the Potenza valley
– survey projects became even more systematic, with more careful sampling strategies and often
with an eye on ‘offsite’ phenomena. Among these projects of other university teams we mention
in particular the Pisa surveys between the rivers Tenna and Aso near the Roman town of Firmum
(Pasquinucci, Menchelli 2004; Menchelli 2012; Ciuccarelli 2012), and an Oxford-based survey
in the upper Esino valley (Pearce et al. 2009).
 When developing the Potenza Valley Survey project (PVS) in 1999 it was my aim to contribute to
these and other ongoing field surveys in a still under-studied range of Adriatic landscapes by enhancing
earlier approaches with two specific perspectives to archaeological landscape survey in the region.
One was to side the traditional artefact survey approaches, that generally concentrate efforts on surface
artefact collections, by a coherent geoarchaeological perspective. This would involve geographers and
geologists in almost all terrain operations and would imply a field strategy that focuses on a better
understanding of the geomorphologic context and dynamics of archaeological sites and landscapes.
The second perspective was linked to the introduction from the start of the project, and in an intensive
way, of technologies of remote sensing. As is now understood, more than a decade and a half after
the beginning of our work in Marche, the use of aerial photography and large scale geophysical
prospections is indeed crucial for a structurally more revealing survey approach to past landscapes in
the Mediterranean, in particular concerning later prehistory and the early historical periods.
 In this volume, prepared by a full team of researchers involved in the PVS project, we wish to present
a first synthesis of the results from our surveys undertaken in a specific valley of central Marche
since 2000. In full understanding of the explanatory power of large windows in time for identifying
regional patterns, as well as for assessing change, the team proposes to focus on the results of more
than 15 years of fieldwork, desktop analysis and material studies, concerning the period from the
early first millennium before our era (c. 900 BC) to the end of Antiquity (c. AD 600). This long and
crucial phase of regional history, which roughly encompasses the Iron Age, the Hellenistic to Late
Republican period, Roman Imperial times, and the Late Roman/Late Antiquity era, has first of all the
merit to allow us to grasp a long but crucial series of fundamental transformations of Italian society.
But at the same time it is a period that in general can be approached by a coherent methodology
of archaeological landscape research. Such a survey strategy needs necessarily to be very different
for approaching the immediately preceding and subsequent periods of prehistory and early Middle
Ages, which both are notoriously difficult to become tangible via the widely applicable survey
methods that were chosen for this and many other fieldwork projects. While the specific reasons for
choosing this particular valley and for adopting a certain strategy of research are made fully clear
in the course of this volume (see chapter 2), I would like to point out that even if some results were
obtained (and briefly discussed here) concerning Stone and Bronze Age occupation of the valley,
8                                                                                       Frank Vermeulen




    as well as for habitation in the early or high Middle Ages, a more specific research strategy would
    have been necessary to really understand something meaningful of the occupation of the landscape
    in these periods of more ephemeral or archaeologically less visible activity, than the one which was
    chosen for the long era on which we focus here.
      Even if already quite some of the data presented here have been the subject of many preliminary
    presentations elsewhere (see chapter 2), this volume is the first to bring all relevant data together
    about the discovered and systematically surveyed sites in the PVS project. In particular, it is the very
    first time that archaeological finds from our prospections are presented in an extensive way, together
    with a more final presentation of all survey sites in the sample transects chosen for this diachronic
    landscape study of the valley of the River Potenza. The many new and valuable data from both the
    urban centres and protohistoric agglomerations surveyed intensely throughout these past years, and
    from a multitude of sites found in their rural hinterlands, that is now available for wider analysis,
    provides a crucial understanding of an ideal section through the middle part of the wider central
    Adriatic region, linking the Apennine mountain landscapes with the coastal plain of the Adriatic Sea.
    In this way the survey project reveals a spectrum of settlement situations, ranging from a Roman
    colony on the coast and a series of pre-Roman Iron age inland centres, to the smallest dwelling
    places of indigenous and immigrant communities living in this specific settlement chamber of the
    Mediterranean World between the early Iron Age and the end of Antiquity.
      This book is divided in chapters that well reflect some of the most important activities by the team
    of Ghent University, responsible from the start for this project. After a presentation and discussion
    of the chosen methods and approaches (Ch. 2) and of the landscape background and the specific
    geoarchaeological analysis of this valley (Ch. 3), we deal first with the very intensive studies
    concerning centralised settlements in the Potenza corridor (Ch. 4), before tackling rural settlement
    dynamics in the researched transects and beyond (Ch. 5). This is followed by an elaborate study
    of the main finds categories collected and studied via our artefact surveys, illustrating parts of the
    changing material culture in the valley and its wider region (Ch. 6). All these synthetic chapters
    are based on the work presented in two systematic catalogues: a gazetteer of the survey sites (Ch.
    7) and a catalogue of diagnostic surface finds (Ch. 8). Finally, the volume closes with a reappraisal
1. General introduction                                                                                                       9




of some of the main contributions this project proposes to understand settlement dynamics in this
Mediterranean valley (Ch. 9).
 The basic archaeological field data collection and study in the Potenza valley, between 2000 and
2015, would not have been possible without an enthusiastic team of researchers and collaborators
from Ghent University, whom I warmly thank for their support. Thanks are due to all the student
participants, who are too numerous to be mentioned here by name as more than 150 of them
enthusiastically took part in field campaigns over the years, as part of their academic training.
Very special thanks go to the core group of archaeologists and geographers of the team’s staff who
participated in many field survey and excavation campaigns, some of which over more than ten
years. In particular I thank the colleagues from the universities of Ghent, Bologna and Groningen
who co-authored this book and whose names are on the cover, as well as those who participated
intensively in the field and material studies during the first part of the project. Among the latter I
particularly thank Tanja Goethals, Paul De Paepe, Joris Angenon, Catharina Boullart, and Hélène
Verreyke. I include here in my thanks the active participation in the field by partner teams from the
universities of Southampton (e.g. Sophie Hay), Macerata (e.g. Umberto Moscatelli), Cassino (e.g.
Cristina Corsi), Ljubljana (e.g. Bozidar Slapsak), the British School at Rome (Rose Ferraby) and
Eastern Atlas (e.g. Burkhart Ullrich). All this fieldwork was only possible thanks to the financial
support by Ghent University, Belgian Science Policy (IUAP-actions) and the Fund for Scientific
Research Flanders, through a consecutive number of project funds obtained during these years. As
no foreign archaeological mission in Italy could be successful without the help of Italian colleagues
active in the study and management of the regional archaeological heritage, we happily acknowledge
the contributions of the collaborators of the Soprintendenza per I Beni Archeologiche delle Marche
(Ancona) and especially its former director Adriano de Marinis (†) and the archaeologists formerly
responsible for different parts of our study area, in particular Edvige Percossi and Mara Silvestrini.
Finally, a special thanks goes to all inhabitants of this beautiful part of Marche, who in many ways
helped us logistically, and often with a great sense of hospitality. I particularly would like to mention
the municipality of Porto Recanati that for many years hosted and supported our team for fieldwork
and material studies in the area.

                       2. Methodological approach
                       Dimitri Van Limbergen, Frank Vermeulen, Geert Verhoeven, Lieven Verdonck


                         2.1 An interdisciplinary open-air laboratory


                         W        hen the PVS project was initiated in early 2000, one of the major aims was to serve as a test-
                                  case for developing multi-disciplinary approaches to the study of landscape dynamics and
                       settlement patterns over time. To this end, a wide range of complementary techniques was employed
                       from the start in order to fully exploit the archaeo-historical potential of both the Potenza corridor in
                       its totality and the sample zones in the upper, middle and lower valley sections. Geoarchaeological
                       field based tools include: systematic field walking, aerial photography, geophysical survey,
                       topographic measurement, augerings and geomorphological study. These were complemented
                       by the analysis of old and new cartographic material, archived vertical aerial photographs, and
                       recently satellite imagery and Lidar data. This intensive archaeological scanning approach was fully
                       integrated with wider research of toponymical and selected historical written information, the study
                       of existing archaeological collections and finds, the re-analysis of older excavation data, small-scale
                       test excavation work and material studies on the newly acquired finds. All data were assembled
                       in a Microsoft Access database and linked within a Geographic Information System (GIS), both
                       developed especially for this project.
                          Long-running interdisciplinary research projects such as the PVS typically evolve in both research
                       questions and research methods. In this introductory chapter, we would like to provide a brief
                       overview of some of the field methodologies used in the course of 15 years of fieldwork. Its purpose
                       is to give some insight into the various interdisciplinary ways in which the data presented in this
                       book have come into being. For detailed descriptions of our changing field methods and tools – as
                       well as some standard and innovative accounts of some specific techniques – we refer to our earlier
                       published work and other methodological articles and books. These can be found in the notes and
                       further reading sections in this chapter.

  2.2 Systematic artefact survey

   From the start of the project, three large sample zones (each c. 15-30 km²) were selected for
exploration through systematic artefact survey. These areas were chosen strategically at regular
intervals along the c. 80 km long course of the Potenza River, roughly coinciding with the upper,
middle and lower sections of the valley. The selection took into account both geographical parameters
– that is, aiming at full coverage of the main landscape types in the region – and cultural-historical
ones, as these zones all lay in the vicinity of Roman towns and/or protohistoric hilltop centres. In this
way, this strategy allowed us to analyse both the land-use history and the long-term development of
settlement in these sub-regional landscapes.
   The first sample zone (extensive survey area: 17.0 km²; intensive survey area: 3.15 km²) is located
within the intra-montane basin of the Umbria-Marche Apennines. Being a fertile hilly plain within
the upper, narrow Potenza corridor, this area was dominated in the Bronze and Iron Age by the
hilltop site of Monte Primo (1300 m), while in Roman times it proved to be an essential and much-
frequented north-south hub between the towns of Camerinum (Camerino) and Matilica (Matelica),
located respectively in the valleys of the nearby rivers Chienti and Esino (Vermeulen 2016, p. 9).
The second sample zone (extensive survey area: 18.8 km²; intensive survey area: 3.69 km²) consists
of the undulating landscape of the middle valley environment; in an area where the Potenza river
flows between two important Iron Age hilltop sites – the Monte Franco and the Monte Pitino – and a
dominant plateau system on which the Roman town of Trea and its medieval successor Montecchio
(today: Treia) developed. A bit further downstream, near the Roman town of Ricina (Villa Potenza),
the landscape of rolling hills opens up into a wide plain flanked by more gradually sloping hillsides.
The third and largest sampling area (extensive survey area: 32.7 km²; intensive survey area: 3.88
km²) is situated in the coastal plain, at the mouth of the Potenza, and is centred on the territory of
12                                                                                              Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




                        the Roman colony of Potentia (Porto Recanati). To the north, this zone is bordered by a key Bronze/
                        Iron Age coastal control post on the Montarice/Colle Burchio hill ridge, while the southern limit is
                        formed by the plateau on which modern-day Potenza Picena is located.
                           As stated, the initial aim of the project was to document long-term settlement patterns from
                        prehistory up to the Middle Ages within the chosen study areas. To this end, within each of the
                        sample zones, a number of fields were selected for intensive artefact survey between 2000 and 2003.
                        For practical reasons, we chose to use the modern agricultural fields as the units for our surveys. The
                        fields were subdivided in smaller units only in a few occasions when they were too large. Preference
                        was hereby given to ploughed fields, as these ensured homogeneous surface visibility and land use.
                        In order to explore these fields, a high-resolution line-walking technique in combination with on-site

   Fig. 4 The upper
   Potenza transect
   with indications
   of sites and their
catalogue numbers.
2. Methodological approach                                                                                                             13




                                                                                                                        Fig. 5 The middle
                                                                                                                        Potenza transect
                                                                                                                        with indications
                                                                                                                        of sites and their
                                                                                                                        catalogue numbers.


random walking was chosen. Each field was surveyed by a team of five to twelve persons. Standard
intervals between walkers were 15 m, but occasionally ranged from 5 m to 10 m due to decreasing
visibility conditions. With the field of vision of individual walkers being around 2 m wide, this
resulted in some 15% coverage for most of the field units. Such distances can be considered effective
for obtaining a representative sample of what is on the ground, especially when aiming to cover
large areas rapidly and systematically in an intensive way. In addition, the consistent application of
this technique in all surveyed fields made later comparisons within and between the three sample
areas in the valley possible, after corrections for high, medium or low visibility were done.1
  These first surveys of the PVS project were so-called ‘site-oriented surveys’; that is, focused in
particular on detecting and delimiting increased artefact densities in the field. In practice, this meant that
each line within a certain field was walked until the surveyor noticed an apparent change in the number
of pottery sherds, pieces of building material or other artefacts. He or she then marked the beginning and
1
  For some key considerations on field survey techniques and their problems, see Mattingly 2000 and Given 2004. Great
insights can also be found in Bintliff, Snodgrass 1988a; 1988b and Bintliff 2000; 2013.
14                                                                                                   Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




   Fig. 6 The lower
   Potenza transect
   with indications
   of sites and their
catalogue numbers.




                        the end of this ‘concentration’ or ‘site’ with small flags. This concentration was left undisturbed until the
                        whole field had been walked. Afterwards, the team returned to the marked areas and performed a detailed
                        random survey. This approach also determined our on- and off-site collection strategies. With regard to
                        the latter, the smallest registration unit became the field, with the collected artefacts from each individual
                        line amalgamated in bags labelled with the year and the field number. For the sites, all artefacts were
                        picked up, collected and bagged with the year and the concentration number marked on the plastic bags.
                        On a series of relevant - mostly Roman - sites, the bulk of the ceramic building material was counted
                        and weighed on-site, but only a random sample of forms and fabrics was collected. Whenever different
                        nuclei of finds within a concentration were noticed, this material was collected and bagged separately.
                        As such, the detailed recording of both intra-site and site densities allowed us to analyse site occupation
                        history, while the collecting of off-site density data at an acceptable, but cost- and time-effective level
                        still allowed tracking changes in the use patterns of different landscape units over time.
                           Accurate localisation and site mapping was achieved for all fields, concentrations and important
                        isolated finds through GNSS (Global Navigation Satellite System) technology, modern cadastral
2. Methodological approach                                                                                  15




maps and topographic maps of the Istituto Geografico Militare. All field data were recorded digitally
in a database form (called Form A in our database), including elements such as topography, soil,
weather conditions, visibility and land use. For each concentration within a certain field, information
was inserted on extent, artefact density and chronology. Data on the artefacts per field were assembled
in another form (Form B), including all potential chronologically diagnostic finds, such as feature
sherds (rims, bases, handles), lithics, glass and building materials. A third form (Form C) comprised
detailed descriptions of prime diagnostic material.
   It is widely known that surface pottery often has a low chronological and functional resolution;
a defect that significantly hinders precise site identification and dating. To this respect, the material
from the PVS surveys was no different and between 2000 and 2015 regular site revisits in the form
of random sampling took place in order to collect additional diagnostic artefacts. In total, some 20%
of all sites initially found by the team through fieldwalking, or with the help of remote sensing, were
revisited. Although such revisits created a limited bias in our record, they allowed to much enhance
the interpretative value of the first visit, and as sometimes many years had passed, to evaluate the
erosion factor in the valley. The latter should not be ignored, as especially deep mechanical ploughing
continues to decimate the archaeological record of this part of Italy. Nevertheless, of all sites where
visibility allowed a decent revisit and the collection of a grab sample of artefacts was possible, we
estimate that 95% were confirmed. While not quantifiable in any way, these judgement samples have
in many cases provided additional dating evidence and allowed for fine-tuning our interpretations.
Then again, pottery analysis needs to be based on datable pottery sequences, meaning that well-
dated regional stratified deposits were crucial for the identification, typology and chronology of our
material. This is why we actively included the study of the pre-existing archaeological record in the
area – such as some good stratigraphic reference contexts excavated in the recent past at Roman
sites in the valley – into our research program. In particular contexts from the older excavations in
the Roman town sites of the valley (e.g. the coastal colony of Potentia) – chronologically spread
over the total length of the life span of these cities – were used as a regional reference base for this
exercise. In addition, many new and crucial data were gathered by complementing our surveys with
the Ghent University excavations at this Roman urban site of Potentia (2007-2010) and the Late
Republican and Early Imperial pottery production site of Casa Valentini/Acquabona (2007-2008),
both located in the lower coastal plain of the Potenza valley. Another initial barrier was a limited
familiarity with the local and regional pottery of some periods, especially of some transitional phases
like the Iron Age to the Republican period, or of Late Antiquity to the Early Middle Ages. This issue
was remedied by building up research programs for the Late Antique and early medieval pottery,
while several temporary visiting experts were asked for their help in the identification of Pre- and
Protohistoric artefacts and the specialised study of specific material categories.
   Since 2002, in addition to our line-walking activities, short campaigns of intensive grid surveys
were undertaken at a series of larger, nucleated settlements (see chapter 4). These include the
Protohistoric centre of Montarice and the Roman towns of Potentia, Trea and Ricina. The
incorporation of these village- and town-sites into our rural survey program aimed at addressing the
issue of long-term settlement history in the region. Indeed, such an approach would not only allow
us to understand better the evolving diachronic relationship between nucleated and dispersed sites
in general, but also the intertwined processes of Roman colonisation and urbanisation in particular
(see chapter 6). Intensive grid survey can be considered the most appropriate strategy to analyse the
occupation history and the functional organisation of such complex multi-period sites. In the course
of the years, we essentially experimented with a series of varying grid systems and different pick-up
and sampling strategies, depending on our specific research questions and taking into account the
circumstances in the field and the existing knowledge on the site. The artefact recording methods
were similar to those used for the line-walking data, with individual grids inserted as separate fields
into the database.
16                                                                            Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




       The first of these systematic prospections was the grid survey of Montarice in 2002. A rectilinear
     grid of 30 m x 30 m blocks was imposed on the hill ridge of some 5 hectares, with each square
     being walked by three to four persons for half an hour maximum. As aerial surveys and subsequent
     preliminary fieldwalking activities in 2000 had revealed traces of several ancient enclosures and
     intra-mural buildings on this promontory (see chapter 4), the grid survey was carried out specifically
     with the intention of recording full phasing and internal structuring of the settlement. A total
     collection strategy was thus applied, with all materials being collected and bagged according to grid
     number, and inserted as such into the database. This resulted in the recording of more than 9000
     artefacts, of which almost 2000 were diagnostic. As the latter could be linked to each walked square
     within the grid, this approach allowed for some insights into temporal and/or functional shifts within
     the settlement (see chapter 4).
       Similar intentions lay behind the two short campaigns of intensive and systematic grid walking
     in and around the town site of Potentia in 2002 and 2003. With the broad infrastructure of the town
     being known thanks to earlier excavations and our own aerial photography work, the part of the wide
     urban area most suited for artefact survey – that is, the area which is currently used as arable land
     (some 10 hectares) – was subdivided into regular units. In 2002, blocks of 40 m x 40 m were walked,
     but in 2003 a system of 19 m x 19 m blocks was used. Both surveys were timed grid walks, with each
     40 m x 40 m square being walked by three to four persons in 20 minutes, and the 19 m x 19 m grids
     in a somewhat shorter time span with only 2 persons per block. Once again, walkers were presumed
     to pick-up all materials. Time restrictions and the huge amount of material that comes with Roman
     urban sites like Potentia, however, make that the collected materials should be considered a small
     sample of the potential surface finds. Still, homogenous visibility conditions in all prospected fields
     and the deployment of equally skilled walkers assure that what was collected can be considered a
     valid representation of the situation in the field.
       In 2005, a grid survey was achieved at the Roman urban site of Ricina in the middle Potenza
     valley. The main aim was to seek additional contextual information and confirmation for aerially
     detected traces in the northern section of the settlement zone. A grid of 160 blocks, each of them
     measuring 10 m x 10 m was laid out, but in the end only 132 blocks could actually be surveyed. As
     we aimed at improving our knowledge about previously detected traces in a certain zone, our pick-
     up strategy was limited this time to the collection of artefacts with diagnostic and/or chronological
     value (e.g. feature sherds, tesserae, crustae, glass and some specific building materials) within a
     short time span. In practice, each square was surveyed by one person for about 4 minutes.
       Finally, between 2007 and 2010, two intra-site grid surveys were carried out at the town site of
     Roman Trea. The 2007 survey covered the forum area (140 m x 90 m) and was intended to further
     characterise the type, function and chronology of the structures detected earlier in this central public
     zone of the settlement through aerial photography and geophysical survey (see infra). In order to
     facilitate comparison between the results of our non-invasive surveys and those of the fieldwalking,
     the grid system of the former with 16 units of 30 m x 20 m was reproduced. Each rectangle was
     walked by a group of seven persons for 30 minutes. As with Ricina, only diagnostic samples of
     datable pottery and other artefacts were collected.
       The 2009-2010 survey was conducted on the southern slope of the town site, in a zone where
     aerial photographs had revealed cropmarks that may be interpreted as the remains of a town
     wall. Based on these results, we decided to turn our second survey of the town into an exercise
     in interpreting intra- and extra-muros surface distributions on complex Roman sites, and
     understanding displacement of artefacts on a slope. In this particular case, the aim of the survey
     was to assess the role of the wall in the formation of the surface record, and to reflect on the nature
     of city limits in antiquity and the various processes that operated within this kind of activity areas.
     To this purpose, a 2.2 hectares large grid, subdivided into 220 blocks of 10 m x 10 m, was imposed
     on the slope. Because of the enormous amount of material on the surface, sampling units of 4 m
2. Methodological approach                                                                                            17




x 4 m were created. This was the only way in which we could reconcile our desire to achieve
total collection with time and logistical restrictions. The surveyors were instructed to collect all
materials larger than 1 cm within the nested sampling units. The collection time was not limited,
as we believed that this would avoid walkers to produce biased assemblages with only the larger
and more striking pieces. The material was bagged according to each square, marked with the
initials of the field walker.
   By 2012, one of the project’s principal research interests had become the diachronic interplay of
Roman urban and rural settlement in this part of central Adriatic Italy. Therefore, in the summer
of 2012, a series of intensive grid surveys were also carried out on some of the more important
Roman rural sites in the lower valley area that had been previously identified through line-
walking (see chapter 3). The main aim of these surveys was to better sample several rural sites
in the surroundings of the town of Potentia; this in order to study more thoroughly the Roman
colonisation of the landscape in later Republican times. Furthermore, from a methodological point
of view, by resurveying some of these sites with a different walking technique, we wanted to test
the representativeness of our initial surveys. On the basis of the latter’s results, four sites were
selected for gridding. The areas covered by these grids were somewhat larger than those where the
previous surveys had registered higher concentrations of archaeological material; this for better
understanding the relationship between the sites and their immediate environment. The survey areas
were subdivided into sampling units of 10 m x 10 m. Once again, the field walkers were instructed
to collect all artefacts larger than 1 cm within these units in the course of a 10-minute time span. The
material was then bagged accordingly and labelled with the initials of the surveyor. The ceramic
building material was counted and weighed on site, and afterwards discarded. The application of
this strategy proved particularly successful in defining more accurately site boundaries and intra-
site activity clusters for these settlements, even if in general the originally detected positions of the
surface scatters were confirmed.

   Further reading: Vermeulen, Boullart 2001, pp. 8-9; Vermeulen, Monsieur, Boullart 2002, pp. 53-54; Vermeulen
et al. 2003, pp. 79-84; Vermeulen, Verhoeven 2004, pp. 66-67; Vermeulen et al. 2005, p. 53; Vermeulen 2006, pp. 67-
68; Vermeulen, Hay, Verhoeven 2006, pp. 213-219; Vermeulen 2008, pp. 234-236; Vermeulen et al.2009, pp. 101-102;
Verreyke, Vermeulen 2009, pp. 104-106; Vermeulen 2012, pp. 43-46; Vermeulen & Mlekuz 2012, pp. 207-208; Vermeulen,
Slapšak, Mlekuz 2012, pp. 270-271.



  2.3 Aerial photography

  While obviously a key-tool in investigating ancient human landscapes, it has been acknowledged
in the past that pedestrian field survey methods such as line- and grid-walking have their limitations.
These are mostly related to limited insights into the detailed spatial organisation of the archaeological
features, their relationship with other features or the surrounding environment, and the preservation
of potential subsoil structural remains. Other prime limitations for a meaningful interpretation of
the subsoil archaeological record are connected with finding the older (deeper lying) phases of
multi-period sites, measuring the impact of earlier (sometimes clandestine) excavations that may
have distorted the surface record, problems of fine dating the bulk of impasto pottery on pre-Roman
and early Roman sites, and the problem of dealing with the bias produced by working with a
heterogeneous group of field walkers over the long life span of a project as this one. In this regard,
remote sensing techniques – that is, all methods that collect information from the surface of the earth
without actually touching it – have repeatedly proven to be an invaluable addition to artefact survey.
One of the most long-standing and effective approaches has been the use of aerial photography.
The capability of this technique to deliver comprehensive, high-resolution and interpretable images
of archaeological traces has made it into an ideal non-invasive tool for detecting, identifying,
18                                                                                                             Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




 Fig. 7 Monitoring
       of the site of
   Montarice (024)
       with oblique
 photography from
 the air in different
            seasons.




                        interpreting and documenting ancient sites. Buried archaeological features are not captured directly
                        through the photographic process, but reveal themselves through the effects they have on their
                        surrounding environment. These effects usually take the form of soil-, crop- or shadow-marks. Soil-
                        marks are discolorations in ploughed, harrowed or naked fields that often reflect the presence of
                        underlying materials or structures through their influence on the surface texture of the soil. Crop
                        (or more generally vegetation) marks only appear in fields that are covered by plants – mostly
                        cereals such as wheat and barley – and relate to the relationship between the sub-surface remains or
                        anomalies and the soil hydrology. Finally, under low-sun conditions, archaeological shadow-marks
                        may emerge in the field as a result of slight variations in the surface topography or vegetation height
                        differences that are caused by manmade structures underneath.2
                        2
                          For a recent overview of the principles of aerial photography, with key references, see Ceraudo 2013; for the use of this
                        technique in Roman urban survey, see Vermeulen 2013b.
2. Methodological approach                                                                                               19




  A wide range of aerial reconnaissance applications were incorporated into the PVS research
program from the beginning. In the early stages of the project, this work started from the study of
archived aerial imagery such as vertical aerial photographs and satellite data. We hereby mostly
examined photographs made by the I.G.M.I. (Istituto Geografico Militare Italiano) in 1972 and
1974, and the excellent vertical post-war pictures from a R.A.F. flight over the area in 1947. The
use of historical aerial datasets has certain advantages. For example, they often refer to landscapes
before they were impacted by modern urbanisation and industrialisation processes. Also, their more
systematic and extensive character makes them a good source for tracing archaeological features like
roads and field systems in the landscape, or for detecting potential relationships between settlements
and wider human adaptations of the landscape.3 In later years – and up till today – our attention
shifted to the analysis of timescale images from open-access applications such as Google Earth and
3
    For an introduction to the use of historical aerial and satellite imagery in archaeology, see Hanson, Oltean 2013.
20                                                                                              Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




                        Bing Maps. This regularly updated aerial and satellite imagery, of increasingly higher resolution, has
                        particularly proven to be of great use in the regular and cost-effective monitoring of known sites, as
                        well as the fine-tuning of our knowledge on their layout and context.
                           It has, however, always been our intention to supplement this static remote sensing material with
                        new, self-made air images taken specifically for archaeological purposes. In the course of more than
                        15 years of fieldwork, various means and strategies have been put to use. A first approach consisted
                        of a program of regular flights over the entire Potenza valley with a small low-flying aircraft. Our
                        strategy involved the use of a 180 horse power 4-seater from the Aeroclub of Ancona (Cessna 172
                        Skyhawk), flown by a local pilot and manned with one or two trained photographers. Flights were
                        mostly performed in the spring and summer months, but occasionally also during autumn or winter.
                        The general observation altitude was about 1000 feet (c. 300 m), while many oblique pictures were
                        made at 300-500 feet. Between 2000 and 2003, standard 35 mm reflex cameras with zoom lenses
                        were used, but from 2004 onwards the transition was made to 35-mm like digital photographic
                        cameras. Flights were carried out following two envisioned strategic patterns: a first covered the
                        entire valley area with a series of random crossing flights; a second focused specifically on the three
                        chosen target areas with intensive circling flights aimed at taking pictures from different angles.
                           This commonly applied technique of oblique air photography was brought into play for three
                        purposes. In the early years of the project, between 2000 and 2001, a first goal was to obtain data
                        from the whole valley area for geomorphologic analysis and landscape reconstruction. During these
                        flights, our attention was geared mostly towards documenting erosive and (paleo)fluvial processes,
                        as well as other hydrology phenomena (e.g. river terraces), in the Potenza basin, and in particular in
                        the floodplain environments and the river mouth area. These observed geotraces were subsequently
                        integrated with the data from our geoarchaeological augerings in the lower and middle valley
                        areas (see infra). This information has proven to be of the utmost importance in reconstructing the
                        historical morphology of the Potenza River. This in turn was crucial for locating and contextualising

ancient roads, river ports, land divisions, rural settlements and other infrastructure around the Iron
Age and Roman centres.
   Between 2001 and 2005, a second category of flights was launched specifically for accompanying
our extensive and intensive fieldwalking activities in the three chosen sample zones in the upper, middle
and lower valley areas. A first objective of these flights was to investigate the surface for previously
unknown archaeological sites. Once detected, potential archaeological traces were verified on the
ground and – if confirmed – subjected to systematic mapping, intensive field study and historical
interpretation. In the following years, the recorded archaeological anomalies were further monitored
through flights in different seasons and under various weather circumstances. Another target was
the coverage of already identified sites in the valley that had never been subjected to aerial imaging;
this mainly in order to improve our topographical and contextual information on them. Here, aerial
detection has proven to be very complementary to historical and field-based archaeological research,
as was demonstrated in particular by our images of some known Protohistoric and medieval hilltop
sites, or the revelation of the complete plan of a small villa-like structure on the place where our
intensive line-walking had identified a small Roman settlement.
   From 2002 onwards, a third set of flights was inserted into our wider research program on the
large Protohistoric and Roman nucleated settlements in the valley. The idea was to develop an
integrated survey approach – in addition to our systematic intra-site fieldwalking surveys and small-
scale excavations, but with the complementary use of geomorphological survey and geophysical
techniques such as magnetic prospection and ground-penetrating radar (see infra) – for examining
complex (proto-)urban sites. This approach was designed specifically with the intention of
improving our understanding of their topography (e.g. size, lay-out, location of walls, streets) and
position within the wider landscape (e.g. roads, field systems). In this way, we aimed at deepening
our comprehension of the evolution of both the urban and suburban landscape, and thus at gaining
insight into the processes of early nucleation and urbanisation in this area of Marche. Systematic
22                                                                                                                       Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.

                        and full coverage of these sites was achieved by almost continuously monitoring them from the air
                        between 2002 and 2010. The sites of Roman Potentia, Trea and Ricina, for example, were surveyed
                        with some 25 individual flights, many of them delivering additional pieces of information on their
                        urban and suburban topography. Intense flying activities in the dry spring seasons of 2003 and 2009
                        have proven to be particularly informative.
                          In the course of 15 years of air reconnaissance, new instruments have been introduced and
                        more analytical techniques have become available. In 2007, in addition to our conventional aerial
                        photography work from a manned aircraft, the team began experimenting with a range of unmanned
                        low-altitude aerial photography devices.4 Our motivation came from a twofold wish: on the one hand,
                        we wanted to increase the spatial resolution of our aerial imagery, as such detecting smaller features
                        and allowing for a more accurate mapping of them. One the other hand, these platforms should allow
                        4
                            For overviews of the principles and applications of LAAP (low-altitude aerial photography), see e.g. Schlitz 2004 and Verhoeven 2009.
2. Methodological approach                                                                                             23




us to experiment with image acquisition in the invisible near-ultraviolet and near-infrared domains.
One of these devices was a so-called ‘Helikite’, a 7 m³ helium balloon with attached kite wings that
could take a camera-supporting and remotely-controlled cradle aloft. Using a big-game fishing lever
drag reel and accompanying fishing rod, the tractive forces exerted by the Helikite could be handled
and allowed for convenient position of this platform.
   With operational heights up to 250 m and being easily employable under different weather
conditions, the use of HAP (Helikite Aerial Photography) on the four Roman towns and one
Bronze and Iron Age centre (Montarice) in the valley has indeed procured a collection of high
quality imagery, in which we were able to discern several previously unnoticed details. In addition,
HAP photography was also used for reconnaissance purposes in the valley and for documenting the
different stages of our excavations at Potentia between 2007 and 2010.
   In addition to the Helikite, we also carried out experiments with a radio-controlled microdrones
md4-1000 quadrocopter equipped with a mirrorless digital camera. The application of this unmanned
platform in the Potenza valley was meant to generate a series of images for documentation and
illustration purposes, 3D modelling, DSM (Digital Surface Model) or DTM (Digital Terrain Model)
extraction and ortophoto production. Targeted sites were the Roman towns of Potentia and Trea, and
the Iron Age and medieval hilltop site of Monte Pitino. Apart from a more random flying strategy
at the latter, the former sites have been covered by vertical imagery acquired during a series of
overlapping strips. For further archaeological and topographical analysis, the collected imagery was

                       put through a series of image-based modelling procedures. These produced – amongst other things –
                       a set of highly detailed and precise DSM’s, indispensable for correctly georeferencing both old and
                       new spatial datasets of hill- and plateau sites with undulating reliefs. Besides, the creation of true
                       orthophotographs – that is, geometrically corrected photographs in which the position of all natural
                       and human objects is accurately represented on the Earth’s surface – has many potential uses in
                       archaeology, like e.g. the exact photographic documentation of ancient monuments and sites through
                       amalgamating partially overlapping photos (Verhoeven et al. 2013).
                          While the majority of the PVS aerial imagery has been captured with cameras sensitive only to
                       visible radiation, we have on occasion also explored the possibilities of invisible near-infrared (NIR)
                       and near-ultraviolet (NUV) imaging. The NIR approach has proven particularly promising with
                       regard to the detection and visualisation of certain crop marks, especially when applied to the Roman
                       urban contexts in the valley. In the visible spectrum, the amount of vegetation chlorophyll – that is,
                       the pigment responsible for the plant’s green-colouring – determines the spectral behaviour of any
                       plant. This behaviour changes completely in the NIR spectral band, where absorption by pigments
                       is extremely low and the leaf’s internal cellular structure affects a very high and diffuse reflectance.
                       Under healthy, vigorous circumstances, vegetation will therefore appear as green in the visible
                       spectrum and very bright in pure NIR photographs. When under stress, its NIR tone becomes much
                       darker (due to a decreased NIR reflectance) while the visible colour changes to a brighter yellow (due
                       to an increased reflectance in the green to red spectrum). In certain conditions, the reflectance drop
                       in the NIR spectrum is so drastic that it becomes easier to spot various plant stresses in NIR images
                       than in conventional photographs. Under the right conditions, the tests in the Potenza valley have

                        proven NIR imaging to either reveal previously unseen cropmarks, or to expose them more clearly.

                        specific wavelengths certain soil-related differences may become more discernible.5
                          Since 2000, the aerial image database of the PVS project has been continually expanding. It now
                        contains about 10,000 vertical and oblique aerial pictures, taken by both manned and unmanned
                        photography platforms. All these visual data have been subjected to systematic recording and archiving
                        with the help of a Microsoft Access database linked to a GIS. Localising was in most cases accomplished
                        through simultaneously recorded GNSS measurements and topographical work conducted with a total
                        station. Many of the pictures underwent further manipulation in the form of contrast enhancement; this
                        in order to expose archaeological traces more clearly and to enhance the possibilities for detailed site
                        mapping, interpretation and spatial analysis (Verhoeven et al. 2012). Finally, our aerial photography
                        was particularly useful for the production of several photogrammetric Digital Elevation Models (DEM)
                        of the Roman urban sites. These DEMs (both DSMs and DTMs) have proven to be of major use in
                        better contextualising these towns within both the modern and ancient landscape, as well as improving
                        our insight into their phenomenology and post-antique evolution.6

  2.4 Geophysical survey

  Following the excellent results of our aerial reconnaissance work between 2000 and 2003
– in particular with regard to the Roman town sites – a multi-method geophysical research
strategy was conceptualised from 2004 onwards in collaboration with several external partners.
Archaeological geophysics is a comprehensive term for denoting a series of non-invasive,
ground-based techniques that are able to detect buried archaeological features on the basis of
their contrasting physical properties in relation to the surrounding soil. The incorporation of
different geophysical technologies into our survey program was specifically aimed at producing
multiple spatial data layers of certain subsurface features – in particular Roman urban sites – that
could be integrated with the results from our artefact surveys and oblique aerial photography.
As such, a more detailed and accurate vision – and thus a more reliable interpretation – of these
contexts would be allowed. To this end, our approach comprised the use of three complementary
geophysical methods: magnetometry, earth resistance and ground-penetrating radar (GPR).
Excellent accounts of how these different methods work in detail can be found elsewhere; here
we confine ourselves to a brief overview of their basic principles and applications within the
PVS program.7

                                                                                                                              Geomagnetic
                                                                                                                              survey by the team
                                                                                                                              of Eastern Atlas on
                                                                                                                              the site of Ricina.

and Linford 2006. For specific applications on large and complex sites see Corsi, Slapsak, Vermeulen 2013b.

        coastal area.




                          Between 2004 and 2013, the bulk of our geophysical data was gathered through magnetometer
                        survey. In essence, when surveying the surface, this technique measures small and local variations
                        or ‘anomalies’ in the magnetic field of the earth. These are caused by the presence of buried features
                        that have different magnetic properties in comparison with the surrounding soil. This contrast in
                        magnetisation can be acquired by two processes: remanent magnetisation and induced magnetisation.
                        As an example of the former variant, thermoremanent magnetisation refers to structures and/or
2. Methodological approach                                                                                                      31




materials that have been magnetised permanently through their exposure to very high temperatures
(>~650°C). Axiomatic examples of Roman Age features include pottery kilns, ovens and furnaces,
or walls built with fired materials such as tiles and bricks. The presence of igneous rocks, e.g. basalt
in Roman road constructions, produces the same effects. In contrast, induced magnetisation, which
shows in pit-, ditch- and building fills, refers to materials in which magnetisation is only created
when an external magnetic field (e.g. that of the earth) is applied. This induction locally enhances
the external field. The magnetic susceptibility of a material determines how strong the enhancement
will be.8 Because magnetometry is able to detect such a wide range of anthropomorphic features,
the technique was adopted as a dominant scanning method within the PVS project. Furthermore,
by mounting multiple scanners onto mobile carts, magnetometry is a cost-effective method for
surveying large areas quickly.
   Magnetometer surveys were undertaken at the Roman town sites of Potentia (2004, 2005,
2007, 2010, 2012), Ricina (2013), Trea (2007) and Septempeda (2010); the Roman rural sites of
Colle Burchio (2009, 2010) and San Girio (2013); and the Protohistoric nucleated settlement of
Montarice (2013). For the majority of our surveys, magnetic anomalies were recorded by using
fluxgate gradiometers. These measure the vertical component of the earth’s magnetic field. In
contrast, a caesium magnetometer, which measures the total intensity of the earth’s magnetic field,
was used at Trea. Both instruments were used in gradiometer mode (two sensors are placed above
each other with a distance of 0.5 m or 1 m between them, and the two readings are subtracted,
so that diurnal changes in the earth’s magnetic field can be removed). Readings were always
performed along fixed transects within a laid out grid system. With both instruments it was
possible to generally detect buried features up to a depth of 0.5-1m, and in some circumstances
a little more. Between 2004 and 2007 (Potentia), the measurements were carried out by Sophie
Hay of the Archaeological Prospection Services of Southampton (APSS) and the British School
at Rome. For the 2009-2013 campaigns, the surveys were performed by Burkart Ullrich and his
Berlin-based company Eastern Atlas (Potentia, Ricina, Colle Burchio, San Girio) and by a team
from the Department of Archaeology of the University of Ljubljana (Trea, Septempeda, Colle
Burchio) directed by Bozidar Slapšak.
   In the course of five field campaigns, the magnetic measurements at Potentia were able to cover
almost the entire intramural area of the Roman colony, as well as substantial parts of the western,
southern and eastern extramural zone. A single campaign at Trea managed to do the same for the
enclosed area of this small Roman municipium. These surveys were all very successful, as they both
confirmed the results from our aerial photography work and revealed many new elements regarding
the internal lay-out of these towns and their immediate surroundings. In contrast, at Septempeda
and Ricina, the circumstances only allowed for subjecting restricted parts of the urban and suburban
areas to magnetic coverage. Still, for both towns, magnetometry helped to locate and map several
key features of their topography; in this way significantly complementing our aerial and field survey
work on these sites. The results of our selected rural magnetic surveys were equally rewarding.
At Colle Burchio, the registration of very strong positive anomalies substantiated the presence of
large thermoremanent structures on the site; that is, in this case probably pottery kilns for amphora
production. At San Girio, magnetometry revealed the complete plan of a large and rich Roman villa,
making it one of the few rural sites in Italy effectively explored by a combination of artefact survey,
geophysics and aerial photography. Finally, the main benefit from applying magnetometry on the
Bronze and Iron Age site of Montarice was a clearer insight into the organisation and evolution of
the hilltop’s defence system and main internal housing structures.
   In a few cases, these magnetic readings were complemented with earth resistance survey
in selected test zones. Targeted sites include Potentia (2004, 2005, 2012, 2013), Trea (2007),
8
 Recent accounts of magnetometry in archaeology, with references to relevant literature, are Aspinall et al. 2008; Armstrong,
Kalayci 2015 and Fassbinder 2016.
32                      Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




       Fig. 22 Fused
       images of the
   geomagnetic and
 the earth resistance
      surveys by the
Ljubljana team over
    the central town
        area of Trea.
2. Methodological approach                                                                                                             33




Septempeda (2010) and Montarice (2012). This method involves creating an electrical current
(direct current) in the ground using two electrodes. This current then travels through the soil
following a trajectory that is affected by the presence of features that resist the current or conduct
it. The resistivity of a material primarily depends on its capacity to retain moisture. Examples
of buried archaeological features with a high resistivity are walls, metalled roads, pavements or
voids. Ditches or pits generally have a low resistivity. The trajectory of the current, as affected by
features with a high or low resistivity, causes local distortions in the electrical potential (voltage),
which can be measured at the ground surface using two additional electrodes, and provide the
basis for mapping archaeological structures in the subsoil. The electrical resistance is obtained
by dividing the voltage by the current. Whereas the resistance is influenced by the volume of
soil traversed by the current, the apparent resistivity concept accounts for the soil volume and
can be considered as a complicated average of the true resistivity distribution in the soil, which
is different to determine. The four electrodes can be deployed in many different ways. For our
surveys, use was made of the common ‘twin-probe array’ arrangement, whereby a mobile frame
with one current electrode and one voltage electrode is connected to the second, fixed pair of
electrodes positioned further away. This method was chosen specifically for its capability of
producing good results under favourable conditions of soil humidity.9 And indeed, in all cases
these control surveys proved useful, as they further clarified some of the magnetometry results
and thus increased our sectorial knowledge of all four sites, in particular with regard to high
resistance structures like streets, monumental buildings, town walls (Potentia, Trea, Septempeda)
and other defence systems (Montarice).
   By increasing the electrode separation, the measured electrical resistance is affected by features
at greater depth, and the change of the apparent resistivity with depth can be investigated (vertical
electrical sounding, VES). When combining a series of VESs along a line using a large number
of electrodes (electrical resistivity imaging, ERI), a resistivity pseudo section can be generated
(‘pseudo’ refers to the fact that the apparent resistivity values are assigned to a particular depth,
whereas in reality there is no direct relationship between a resistance measurement and one single
depth). Within the PVS project, ERI measurements were carried out in 2012 and 2013 at Montarice
and Potentia.
   Despite the advantages and widespread application possibilities of magnetometry and earth
resistance, both techniques also have their drawbacks. Magnetometry, for example, is highly
susceptible to modern magnetic disturbances and therefore less effective in areas adjacent to
modern buildings and other constructions with large amounts of metal in them (e.g. transmission
towers, pipes, cables). On fields close to modern roads it is affected by magnetic effects of passing
traffic. Reliable interpretations of earth resistance measurements require some prior knowledge
about the expected subsurface structures; this in order to rightly disentangle the complex pattern
of electrical current pathways in the ground.10 Being a geophysical technique able to provide high-
resolution information on the stratigraphy, ground-penetrating radar or GPR was incorporated into
our survey routine from 2007 onwards. As the method is known for providing particularly good
results in detecting stone buildings and other stone structures, our prime objective was the gathering
of complementary information in addition to the magnetometry and resistivity information.
Furthermore, we also wanted to test the effectiveness of the GPR in the different landscape zones
in the valley.
   In short, a GPR is an instrument that transmits radio-frequency electromagnetic pulses (generated
by a transmitter antenna) into the ground. These pulses travel through the soil are reflected off
interfaces between layers or structures with contrasting physical properties, and received back at the
surface by a receiver antenna. The energy that is not reflected, penetrates further into the soil, and
9
    For recent discussions of electrical resistance techniques for archaeological purposes, see Tsokas et al. 2009 and Schmidt 2013.
10
     Schmidt 2009, pp. 73-75.
34                                                                                                  Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




Fig. 23 GPR survey
 with quad traction
       by the Ghent
  team in the lower
     Potenza valley.




                       can be reflected by deeper discontinuities, until all energy is absorbed by the soil. So the presence of
                       buried features or void spaces with different properties than the surrounding soil will cause sequential
                       reflections whose amplitude and time delay can be measured by the instrument. In order to map the
                       subsurface, vertical sections, obtained by conducting measurements along straight transects, can be
                       merged into 3D data volumes from which horizontal slices can be extracted. The most important
                       factor that governs the occurrence of reflections, is the varying degree of water content between the
                       different layers and structures in the ground.11
                         As this technique is slower, costlier and more demanding in data processing compared
                       to magnetometry, the GPR was only used in specific target areas; that is, in zones where we
                       considered it useful to acquire additional phasing information. GPR scanning was carried out at
                       the town sites of Potentia (2007, 2012, 2013), Trea (2007), Ricina (2013) and Septempeda (2013);
                       at the rural sites of Casa Piacampo (2012), Colle Burchio (2012) and San Girio (2013); and at
                       the hilltop site of Montarice (2013). Most of them were performed by PVS team member Lieven
                       Verdonck of Ghent University (Potentia, Ricina, Septempeda, Casa Piacampo, Colle Burchio,
                       San Girio, Montarice), while the survey at Trea was accomplished by the University of Ljubljana.
                       The instruments used in our surveys were Sensors & Software pulseEKKO PRO antennas with
                       centre frequencies of 250 and 500 MHz, integrated with Network Interface Controllers connected
                       to a portable computer. All surveys were executed according to a grid system, with the antennas
                       moved first manually and later with the aid of a wooden cart pulled by an ATV in order to speed
                       up recording.
                        Good and recent explanations of the principles of GPR prospection are Leckebusch 2003; Conyers 2012; 2013 and
                       11

                       Manataki et al. 2015.
2. Methodological approach                                                                                             35




   Unfortunately, the amount of complementary information gathered by this technique has been
rather limited. On the whole, only few of the structures detected by previous survey work were visible
on the GPR imagery, and the traces were almost never as clearly visible as on the magnetometry and
earth resistance maps. Occasionally (e.g. at Ricina and Colle Burchio), new elements were detected,
but generally the weakness and fuzziness of the GPR results hindered conclusive interpretations.
Reasons probably include a mixture of constraints, such as a poor state of preservation (e.g. Colle
Burchio), a too small contrast in moisture content between the archaeological features and the
surrounding soil (e.g. Potentia), or the presence of conductive soils (with a high (>50%) clay content,
e.g. at Potentia, Septempeda, San Girio), an element that significantly reduces the penetration range
of the radio waves and thus the depth up to which buried structures can be detected (under favourable
soil conditions features may be registered up to a depth of ~3 m with a 250 MHz antenna). The use
of GPR in the Potenza valley can thus be considered less effective.
   In the end, however, the results of our integrated geophysical approach in the Potenza valley can
be considered very successful. Indeed, on all surveyed sites, this approach has helped us in locating
many new architectural and topographical elements, as well as in elucidating various site- or sector-
based interpretative issues. In particular, through the overlaying of images resulting from the
different survey techniques, it has enabled us to provide more reliable and complete interpretations
with regard to the layout of the Roman town sites and some smaller Protohistoric and Roman rural
sites. We may thus conclude that our work in the Potenza valley represents an excellent example of
how the integration of multiple datasets from a series of complementary remote sensing operations
such as aerial photography and geophysics can significantly contribute to our understanding of
ancient sites in a systematic and non-destructive way.


   Further reading: Verdonck 2012; Vermeulen, Hay, Verhoeven 2004, pp. 211-212; Vermeulen 2006, pp. 65-67; Vermeulen
et al. 2009, pp. 87-90, 95-101; Vermeulen, Slapšak, Mlekuz 2012, pp. 268-270.




  2.5 Geomorphological study

  A last but essential part of our multi-method field survey approach consisted of the integration of
geomorphological studies (Rap, Hill 1998). The application of this particular branch of research
mainly served three purposes: to trace and map the paleo-landscape (1), to enhance our understanding
of the relationship between this landscape and the archaeological situation (2), and to gain
additional information on some of the more deeply buried archaeological structures known through
geoarchaeological survey (3). In addition, we also wanted to construct a detailed geomorphological
map of the study area. The applied methodology comprised both a passive and an active element. The
former included the study of all relevant existing literary, cartographic, historic and archaeological
material, as well as the processing and analysis of the PVS-produced remote sensing imagery. The
latter consisted first of all of a series of geomorphological field operations in the form of geo-electric
survey, terrain observation and manual augering in selected on-site target zones and anomalies in
the landscape. Furthermore, a series of intensive topographic measurements were taken in the field,
using total station equipment or differential GNSS instruments, in order to obtain higher resolution
maps or DTM’s of the current micro topography of sites and their environments (e.g. the Roman
urban sites of Potentia and Trea), and to facilitate the mapping of geophysical prospection data or
of structures revealed by oblique aerial photography. All these geomorphological operations were
coordinated by Morgan De Dapper of the Department of Geography at Ghent University.
  Hand augering is a relatively simple and cost-effective method for analysing the geomorphological
evolution of an area and with only a few well-chosen drillings a lot of information on the character
36                                                                                             Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.

     bank, C. paleo-

                       and the evolution of the landscape can be retrieved. For all augerings, hand auger equipment of
                       Eijkelkamp was used. Our set included an Edelman auger, a stony soil auger, a spiral auger, a soft
                       soil auger, a riverside auger, and a stone catcher; all this complemented by handles, extensions rods
                       and bayonet connections. The choice for a specific auger mainly depended on the dominant texture
                       of the sediment. Under perfect soil conditions, these augers can achieve a maximum penetration
                       depth of 15 m (Berendsen 2010).
                          With regard to the Protohistoric and rural Roman sites in the valley, our attention was mostly aimed
                       at clarifying the correlation between their location and certain features in the landscape, such as the
                       presence of favourable soils (e.g. arable land) and topographic situations (e.g. elevated plateaus or
                       hillslopes), or the proximity of construction materials and water sources. We also aimed at exploring
                       how processes of alluviation and colluviation may have influenced the preservation, location and
                       diffusion of these sites. In the lower valley area, specific focus was put on the Holocene evolution
                       of the coastal plain and the Potenza River, and their diachronic interaction with the anthropogenic
                       landscape. In this area, our fieldwork essentially consisted of geo-electric survey in combination
                       with hand augering. In order to strengthen our interpretations, many of the sediments retrieved
                       by augering were subjected to radiocarbon and Optically Stimulated Luminescence (OSL) dating,
                       as well as mollusc analysis. Five rural sites in the hinterland of Potentia were studied in detail:
                       Casa dell ’Arco (2003); San Girio, Montarice Villa, Casa Apis and Casa Cingolani (2012). After
                       processing, the results of these surveys were integrated into GIS together with the information from
                       our artefact surveys in order to create detailed geo-archaeological maps.
