                          The same strategy was applied to some of the Protohistoric nucleated sites and the Roman urban
                       sites in the valley. Here, our goal was mainly to understand the ecological and geomorphological
                       history of these sites in relation with human interventions, and to clarify and confirm the data from
                       our artefact and remote sensing work. As such, in the lower valley area, we performed augerings
                       in the western extra-mural area of Potentia (2012) and on top of the Montarice hill (2003; 2013) in
                       order to control some of the aerial photography, magnetometry and GPR results. On-site augerings
                       at Potentia (2003) were aimed specifically at analysing the relationship of the coastal town with the
                       underlying beach ridge. In the middle valley area, augering was done on the urban sites of Ricina
                       (2003, in combination with earth resistance) and Trea (2013). The fieldwork around Ricina was
                       concentrated on elucidating the water supply of the Roman town. At Trea, we wanted to obtain
                       an insight into the geomorphology of the town, in particular with regard to hypothetical landscape
                       modifications and plateau levelling operations in relation with its foundation. Furthermore, the
                       augering surveys also aimed at contextualising the results from our earlier geophysical work and to
                       obtain information on the conservation status of the buried remains.

  2.6 Artefact studies and additional desktop operations

  In concordance with our field survey work, several research programs for artefact studies were
conducted over the years. Our first concern was hereby to establish the chronology of the rural
and urban sites in the valley with as much detail as possible. A second aim was to identify – and
build up timelines for – local and regional ceramic assemblages, thus shedding more light on the
diachronic importance of these materials both in- and outside the area. A third focus of study was
the presence of extra-regional pottery in the valley, and the individuation of major import routes and
(inter)national commercial contacts. In practice, at the end of each survey campaign, the collected
materials were cleaned, processed and stored at the project’s central deposit in Porto Recanati. Over
the years, several targeted campaigns were organised in order to study these materials in more
detail, and for allowing both internal and external experts to have a closer look at some of the key-
artefact categories. These included e.g. Roman amphorae and other categories of Roman pottery (P.
Monsieur, Ghent University), Black Gloss Ware (J. Van Kerckhove, Ghent University), African Red
Slip Ware (H. Verreyke, Ghent University), Terra Sigillata (F. Biondani, Università di Bologna),
Roman lamps (L. Wilmet, Université de Namur), Protohistoric and diagnostic pre-Roman wares (W.
De Neef, Groningen University, K. Boullart, Ghent University and A. Gamberini, and S. Morsiani,
Università di Bologna), coins (J. Van Heesch, KULeuven and R. Villicich, Università di Bologna),
Roman glass (M. Ganio, KULeuven) and also stone materials (P. De Paepe and D. Taelman, Ghent
University). Most of these studies concerned classic typo-chronological approaches, while for
the less well known or less widely dispersed categories of pottery (e.g. local and regional coarse
wares) the identification and formation of ceramic fabric groups was achieved. For certain specific
categories of materials extra analytical approaches and wider area studies were initiated or are
ongoing, sometimes involving more science-based archaeometric approaches. This is particularly
the case for the Roman amphorae studied by P. Monsieur within the wider Adriatic context and
involving petrographic and chemical analysis of sampled sherds from well-defined sites in the
territory of Potentia. Much work has also been done by H. Verreyke on late Roman fine and coarse
wares, with the petrographic analyses of a series of imported and local fabric groups. Also worth
mentioning is the doctoral study by M. Ganio on the exploration of the provenance of raw materials
for Roman glass found in the valley, again based on Potentia contexts, using a combined approach
based on alumina contents, trace elements patterns and Sr-Nd isotopic signatures. And finally, as
part of a wider research project by PVS team member Devi Taelman, all decorative stone materials
collected during the artefact surveys were first studied typo-chronologically (counting, weighing
and typological classification), while archaeometric analyses were performed on a number of them.
Particular focus was hereby put on the analysis of marble use and marble provenance in both Roman
urban and rural contexts. Specific attention was devoted to the materials collected at Trea (2007,
2009, 2010) and Ricina (2005), as these had been important commercial hubs in the valley. The
polychrome marbles could generally be identified macroscopically by comparisons with reference
samples and available image databases (Borghini 2004; Price 2007). The final aim of this study was
40                      Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.

to gain insight into the supply patterns and modes of distribution of marble in the area, and to unravel
the socio-economic mechanisms behind their diachronic use. Some of the main results of all these
artefact studies are inserted below in this book.
   Finally, a series of desktop operations deserve mentioning here. In all stages of the project, in
order to enhance the interpretative value of our data, we aimed at integrating them with the analysis
of historical cartography and classical sources (mainly the Liber Coloniarum), archive research and
GIS processing operations. This approach has proven particularly rewarding for the reconstruction
by C. Corsi (University of Cassino) of the Roman centuriated landscape around Potentia (Corsi
2008; Corsi et al. 2009; Corsi,, Vermeulen 2010) and the related evolution of the surrounding
coastal plain of the Potenza river (Goethals et al. 2006). It further contributed significantly to our
efforts in rebuilding the schematic and diachronic evolution of all four Roman towns in the valley,
and in particular of the townscape of Trea. Also, landscape reconstructions and DTMs obtained in
this way were particularly useful for fully grasping the history of rural settlement in the area, as
well as retrieving the Roman road network that connected the Potenza valley with the wider central
Adriatic area and Rome.
   Outside the use of GIS for the storage, management, integration, analysis and visualisation of
the PVS data, the technique was also put to use for the study of legacy data (Gillings 2001). This
worked on two levels. On the one hand, our project provided the opportunity to include a large
amount of non-systematic excavation and surface operations in the valley of both the very recent
and more distant past for renewed and/or deeper analysis (Vermeulen 2012). The documentation
and digitisation of these data were facilitated by our recent access to – and active collaboration
with – the archaeological database of the Marche region, set-up by the University of Macerata
and the Soprintendenza Archeologica delle Marche, and coordinated for the PVS-project by F.
Carboni. On the other hand, the regular revisiting of certain sites since our original publication in
2006 (Percossi et al. 2006) allowed not only for obtaining higher resolutions with regard to site
definition, classification and chronology; but also for assessing processes of modern agricultural
transformation and urbanisation, and their impact on the nature of the surface archaeological record
(Witcher 2008).
42                                                                                          Devi Taelman et al.




     3. Landscape background and geoarchaeology in the PVS project
     Devi Taelman, Morgan De Dapper, Lara Weekers, Possum Pincé


       3.1. Introduction


       T    he research presented in this chapter describes the landscape environment and the relation
            of this environment with the human occupation in the Potenza valley basin. The focus is
     on the geomorphologic and quaternary geologic aspects of the area, which are then brought in a
     geoarchaeological perspective by addressing questions and topics that were inserted in the aims of
     Potenza Valley Survey project since its start in 2000.
       Although the general genesis and evolution of the Marche landscape has already been studied
     by other scholars (see, for example, Cilla, Dramis 1999; Coltorti 1989, 1991; Coltorti 1997;
     Coltorti, Farabollini 2008; Luni et al. 2009; Nesci et al. 1995; Ortolani, Alfieri 1978), a more
     detailed knowledge of the landscape formation processes in the Potenza Valley Survey study area is
     needed to understand the human-environment relationships along the river Potenza from the Final
     Bronze Age to the early medieval period (c. 1000 BC to AD 1000).
       For the understanding of evolutions and changes in the physical environment, it is essential to
     work in a sufficiently large time window and at the same time to be able to include the effects of
     faster, short-term processes. The temporal frame of the late Pleistocene and Holocene allows for
     doing this while focussing on the aforementioned period of two millennia.
       Furthermore, the processes studied do not only act on different temporal scales, but also on different
     spatial scales. The general study is to be done on the regional scale of the Potenza and the Marche
     region, within the framework of the Italian peninsula; while the application of field techniques and
     observations is situated on a smaller scale of four selected pilot areas, with a particular focus on a series
     of geoarchaeological case studies, placing the detected human occupation into its landscape context.


       3.2. Landscape setting of the Potenza Valley Survey project


        3.2.1. Tectonic, geologic and geomorphologic setting of central Adriatic Italy

         The Potenza Valley Survey study area is centred on the valley of the river Potenza in central
     Adriatic Italy (Marche region). From a geologic point of view, the study area covers the transition
     of the Umbria-Marche Apennine mountain chain to the Periadriatic Foredeep. The Umbria-Marche
     Apennines are a northwest-southeast, arcuate fold-and-thrust belt developed since the late Oligocene
     as a result of the westward subduction of the Adria Plate below the Eurasian plate. This event is
     accompanied by an east- and northeast-vergent thrusting and uplifting since the middle/late Pliocene
     (Calamita, Deiana 1988, 139; Coward et al. 1999, p. 560). East of the Apennine chain, this event
     gave rise to the Periadriatic Foredeep, which marks the transition from the Apennine fold-and-thrust
     belt to the Adriatic Foreland.
         In central Adriatic Italy, the Apennines are made up of two anticline ridges separated by a syncline
     depression; from west to east, the Umbro-Marchean Ridge, the Inner Marche Basin or Matelica-
     Camerino synclinorium, and the Marchean Ridge (Fig. 29, 30A) (Coltorti et al. 1996, 34) South
     of the Potenza basin, both Apennine ridges join to form the Sibylline Mountains, whose maximum
     elevation exceeds 2450 m AMSL (Coltorti et al. 1996, 34). Locally, the Umbro-Marchean Ridge
     and the Marche Ridge reach elevations between 1000 m AMSL and 1500 m AMSL while the Inner
     Marche Basin has an average elevation between 300 m AMSL and 500 m AMSL. In the Periadriatic
3. Landscape background and geoarchaeology in the PVS project                                                                                 43




Foredeep, out-of-sequence thrust fronts crop out at the Dei Fiori Mountains, the Cingoli Ridge, the
Della Cesana Mountains, and the Monte Conero (Fig. 30B)(Coltorti et al. 1996, p. 34).




                                                                                                                             Fig. 29 Structural
                                                                                                                             geology of the
                                                                                                                             Marche region
                                                                                                                             (after Coltorti
                                                                                                                             et al. 1996, 35),
                                                                                                                             with indication
                                                                                                                             of the four PVS
                                                                                                                             pilot zones for
                                                                                                                             geomorphological
                                                                                                                             and
                                                                                                                             geoarchaeological
                                                                                                                             fieldwork.


    The Apennines are made up of a phyllitic basement of late Palaeozoic age, followed by Mesozoic
to Miocene carbonatic, marly and marly-carbonatic deposits.12 In the Inner Marche Basin, these
formations are covered by Miocene – Pliocene turbiditic and evaporitic deposits (sandstones and
marls), and heterogeneous terrigenous clastic sediments (Calamita et al. 1994, 873; Cantalamessa
et al. 1986; Centamore 1986; Scarselli et al. 2007, p. 74).13 The geology of the Periadriatic
Foredeep basin is characterised by upper Oligocene to Quaternary clastic sediments of several
sedimentary sequences deposited at different times in different sectors of the basin, overlying the
foredeep substratum of carbonate and marly sediments of the Adria Foreland ramp (Carruba et al.
2006, 667). Quaternary continental deposits are found as slope deposits, travertines, and fluvial and
alluvial fan deposits (Della Seta et al. 2008, 121).14
    From the middle/late Pliocene onwards, the Umbria-Marche Apennine fold-and-thrust belt
underwent a northeast-verging tectonic uplift that maximised at the end of the early Pleistocene
(Ambrosetti et al. 1982; Calamita, Deiana 1988, p. 139). Contemporary to the upheaval, a subparallel
drainage network developed with a main SW–NE to WSW–ENE orientation, perpendicular to the
main relief units (the Umbro-Marchean Ridge, the Inner Marche Basin or Matelica-Camerino
12

Rossa, Scaglia Cinerea, Bisciaro and Schlier formations.

                          synclinorium, the Marchean Ridge and the Periadriatic Foredeep) (Della Seta et al. 2008, p. 128;
                          Mayer et al. 2003, p. 157). The major rivers start in the Apennine area and end in the Adriatic Sea,
                          on their way they crosscut the Apennine anticlinal ridges in deep and narrow gorges, for example the
                          Furlo and Pioraco gorges (Fig. 30A).

    Regional uplift, combined with Quaternary climate oscillations and anthropogenic factors further
gave rise to several river terrace sequences. Three Pleistocene terrace levels were formed during the
Elsterian, the Salian and the Weichselian glacial periods, and resulted from a combination of tectonic
activity and climatic changes, while a complex succession of Holocene river terraces resulted from
a combination of climatic and anthropogenic events, such as deforestation (Coltorti 1989, 1997;
Coltorti, Farabollini 2008; Nesci et al. 1995). A clearly visible aspect of the Marche river terraces
is their asymmetry: the right banks are relatively steep and support few river terraces, while on the
gentler left banks well-developed terrace staircases prevail. With some anthropogenic exceptions, all
rivers flow in the south of their floodplains, near the base of the hills (Goethals et al. 2005, p. 35).
    Between the early Holocene and 4000 BP, at earliest, the Flandrian transgression forced the
coast inland. An Atlantic-type coast (ria) formed (Coltorti 1991), characterised by the alternation
of promontories (ending in cliffs) and pocket beaches near the debouchments of the rivers (Coltorti
1997, p. 324). The maximal ingression of the Adriatic Sea is dated around 5000 BP (Correggiari
et al. 1996, 699). Afterwards the coast started to prograde, and before the 3rd century BC, a
rectilinear coast with beach ridges in line with the still active cliffs had developed (Coltorti 1997;
Ortolani, Alfieri 1978, p. 326). Behind the beach ridges, existing lagoons evolved in marshlands
by sedimentary infilling because of slope erosion following deforestation during the Bronze and
Iron ages. The sediment amount supplied by rivers would have diminished during the Roman and
early medieval periods, because of the adoption of soil protection measures by the Romans and the
decline in population and natural reforestation afterwards. Erosion takes the upper hand again from
the 12th century onwards, when population numbers start increasing again at a significant rate. The
sediments would have been redistributed along the Adriatic coast, so that the entire coast prograded,
also at the base of the cliffs. The coastal wetlands were gradually infilled, forests were cut down
and many rivers were diverted, straightened or embanked near their debouchment. From the end of
the 16th century to the end of the 19th century, the progradation of the coast accelerated, according
to literature due to erosion after deforestation and land reclamation in the river valleys, resulting in
aggrading braided rivers (Coltorti 1997, 329-331). These events result in a present-day coastline
with inactive cliffs of 100 m to 200 m high and with low sandy beaches, corresponding with the river
mouths. The cliffs are covered with Pleistocene marine terraces (Massari et al. 1986). At the base
of the cliffs, a strip of gravely sand occurs, to 100 m to 150 m wide and to 5 m to 10 m above sea
level, which is only interrupted by river mouths, but varies in width according to former migrations
of the river mouths (Coltorti 1997, pp. 324-326).


   3.2.2. Climate, vegetation and land use since the Holocene in the Marche region

    During the Pleistocene and the onset of the Holocene, climatic oscillations were responsible for
terrace formation in central and southern Italy. The late Pleistocene phase of the Younger Dryas
was marked by a dry and cold climate with (wooded) steppe vegetation (Lowe et al. 1996; Watts et
al. 1996). The fluvial landscape was characterised by river aggradation resulting from an increased
sediment supply from slope erosion. The so-called Holocene Climatic Optimum (HCO) of the early
Holocene marks a period with a warmer and humid climate with typical Mediterranean thermophile
species in broadleaf forests with thick brown-calcic soils. Vegetation cover and reduced slope
erosion caused river incision during the early Holocene (Calderoni et al. 2007; Cilla, Dramis
1999, p. 78).
    Around 6000 BP to 5000 BP, the mid-Holocene climatic transition marks the start towards
a colder and dryer climate that lasted until c. 3500 BP. This period showed a halt in travertine
depositions along the upper courses of the rivers of Marche. This change can be related to a removal
of forest vegetation so that soil erosion was enhanced and the amount of CO2 in the groundwater
46                                                                                   Devi Taelman et al.




     decreased to below the level needed for travertine formation. A decline in travertine deposition
     in entire central Adriatic Italy, locally since 6000 BP, suggests a wider-scale environmental
     phenomenon, such as climatic change, as the main reason for the reduced vegetation cover and
     river aggradation (Calderoni et al. 2007; Cilla, Dramis 1999, p. 81). It is difficult to determine to
     what extent Bronze Age human impact (progressive occupation of larger areas of land, increasing
     intensity of agricultural and grazing activity, and woodland exploitation) influenced these changes
     in vegetation and land use. River aggradation was further enhanced by sea level rise that maximised
     around 5000 BP (Elmi et al. 1994).
         Despite more intensive agricultural-pastoral activities and woodland exploitation, and
     increasing slope sediment supply during the Iron Age and Roman period, fluvial erosion seems
     to have again dominated (Coltorti 1991, 1997). A shift to warmer and wetter climatic conditions
     after 3500 BP (Giraudi 1998; Giraudi et al. 2009) suggests that the human impact on erosion and
     depositional processes was still subordinated to that of climate, especially in the upper and middle
     courses of the rivers of Marche. The impact of man-made forest clearance and agriculture, which
     has progressively spread all over the area since the Iron Age, does not seem to have significantly
     affected fluvial erosion-deposition processes, as suggested by the generalised deepening trend of
     river channels up to late medieval and Renaissance times. For the coastal areas, evidence seems to
     suggest an increase in sediment supply that is probably related to intensified agriculture and land
     exploitation in the Bronze and Iron Ages. For the Potenza valley, the formation of beach ridges
     must have been the result of higher sediment loads for the river Potenza caused by population
     growth, increased agricultural pressure on the lands (Coltorti 1997). Despite increasing population
     in the Roman period, no major changes seem to have affected the coast and the channel patterns,
     which can be attributed to a better knowledge of soil conservation techniques (see above) (Coltorti
     1997; Goethals et al. 2009). An expansion of population and of agricultural land, at the expense
     of woodland, after AD 1100, with major reclamation works since the 15th century, marked the
     start of renewed river aggradation. The reduction of slope erosion in more recent historical times
     has resulted in recent deeply incised rivers (Coltorti 1997, pp. 329-331). Palaeobotanic studies
     have shown that the open agrarian landscapes of the central and coastal Marche region, with their
     Mediterranean and sub-Mediterranean climates, were largely covered by woodland during the pre-
     Roman Iron Age, and to a lesser extent even during the Roman period. Apart from cereals, large-
     scale wine and olive production, especially during the Late Republican and Early Imperial period
     has been reported for the Marche region (Naso 2003, pp. 20-26; Van Limbergen 2011, pp. 85-86;
     Vermeulen 2016).
         The diversified topography of the Marche region, with inland mountainous areas, a central hilly
     landscape and a flat coastal and alluvial region in the eastern area of the Marche region, resulted
     in microclimates, from west to east, almost oceanic, sub-Mediterranean and finally Mediterranean.
     Inland, where higher relief prevails, the climate has high rainfall and cold winters (average January
     temperature 3° C) with woodland and mountain pastures dominating the landscape. Towards the
     east, the climate has more temperate and dryer conditions with dry and hot summers (average
     July temperature 26° C). Rainfall is most abundant in autumn (Bisci et al. 1992, pp. 84-86). This
     micro-regional diversity of the landscape favoured the creation of real settlement chambers, each
     offering different opportunities for human societies in the exploitation of the natural resources
     (presence of different raw material (building stones, minerals, etc.), different agricultural or
     pastoral opportunities, etc.) (Vermeulen 2016).
3. Landscape background and geoarchaeology in the PVS project                                           47




   3.2.3 The valley of the river Potenza

    The river Potenza flows centrally in the Marche region. Like most major rivers in the Marche
region, it drains ENE-wards from the Apennines, near Monte Pennino (1571 m AMSL), to the
Adriatic Sea, just south of the town of Porto Recanati. It is bordered to the north by the river
Musone and to the south by the river Chienti, from which it is separated by two parallel interfluve
hills. The Potenza has an elongated drainage basin of about 775 km2 (Goethals et al. 2005, p. 35).
On its course of approximately 80 km, the Potenza cuts through three ridges creating steep gorges,
from west to east: the Umbro-Marchean Ridge, near Pioraco (+ 481 m AMSL); the Marchean
Ridge, near San Severino Marche (+ 238 m AMSL); and the Treia-Pollenza Ridge, near Passo di
Treia (+ 146 m AMSL).
    In its upper valley, the Potenza is characterised by high slope gradients, with a single river
channel in a narrow valley. In the middle valley, the slope gradients drop and the river takes a
meandering river channel with a valley width between 10 m and 100 m. In the lower valley, the
river changes from a meandering to a braided regime. Near the coast, the river valley floor reaches
about 4 km in width.
    The river Potenza has a mean annual discharge lower than 20 m3/s (Appiotti et al. 2014, 2018-
2019). Like for most rivers in the Marche region, this low mean flow inhibits water transport on
most of their courses, especially during the dry season and in the more torrential middle and upper
courses. The same must have been the case in the protohistoric, Roman and medieval periods,
periods in which the rivers were meandering over their alluvial plain (Coltorti 1997, pp. 328-
331; Goethals et al. 2009). Nevertheless, the Potenza river valley and the other main Marche
river valleys, certainly acted as important arteries linking the coastal area with the Apennine
mountainous area throughout history (Vermeulen 2017).
    Like the other rivers in the Marche region, the Potenza river valley is characterised by three
orders of terraces dated to the Pleistocene and that can be related to tectonic activity and climatic
oscillations. The Holocene terrace sequence results from a combination of climatological and
anthropogenic influences. Like the other rivers, the terraces are not symmetrically divided along
both sides of the river valley. The right valley slope generally does not show the full terrace
sequence (Goethals et al. 2005, p. 34; 2009, p. 439).


   3.2.4 The Potenza Valley Survey project – A geomorphologic approach

    To understand and explain the Final Bronze Age to early medieval occupation patterns within
the Potenza river basin, intensive archaeological and geomorphologic fieldwork was organised in
four pilot areas (Fig. 29, Table 1) (Vermeulen et al. 2003). The zones were located along the course
of the Potenza, so as to assess the variance provoked by geographical factors representing the most
important landscape types of the valley of the river Potenza.
    • Zone 1: upper valley, near the former Roman settlement of Prolaqueum (now: Pioraco);
    • Zone 2a: middle valley, near the medieval towns of Montecchio (now: Treia) and Monte Milone
    (now: Pollenza);
    • Zone 2b: middle valley, annex of zone 2a, centred on the former Roman town of Ricina (now:
    Villa Potenza);
    • Zone 3: lower valley, around the site of the abandoned Roman town of Potentia (near
    Porto Recanati).
48                                                                                         Devi Taelman et al.




        Table 1 – Summary statistics for geoarchaeological pilot zones.


                                     Zone 1                  Zone 2a         Zone 2b             Zone 3
     Location                     upper valley            middle valley    middle valley       lower valley

              2

     Max Elevation                   1285 m                    488 m          325 m              298 m
     Min Elevation                   281 m                     105 m           77 m                0m
     Mean Elevation                  593 m                     216 m          148 m               66 m


         Lower valley – Zone 1
         Pilot zone 1 is located along the upper course of the river Potenza, between the modern towns
     of Pioraco, Castelraimondo and Camerino. The main archaeological sites are the protohistoric
     sanctuary of Monte Primo and the Roman settlement of Prolaquaeum, a road station along the
     diverticulum connecting the Potenza valley with the via Flaminia, the primary Roman road between
     Rome and the Po Plain.
         The zone measures 99 km2. Elevation of the area ranges from 281 m to 1285 m AMSL. The main
     geomorphic features of zone 1 are the Umbro-Marchean Ridge in the west and the Inner Marche
     Basin in the east. In zone 1, the mountainous area of the Umbro-Marchean Ridge displays gorges
     with steep to subvertical slopes formed by tectonic activity and river incision, the most important of
     which is the Pioraco Gorge. After passing the Pioraco Gorge, the Potenza enters the Inner Marche
     Basin characterised by a lower-lying hilly landscape with some plains and tablelands. East of the
     study area, the river cuts through the Marchean Ridge, again in the form of a narrow and steep gorge,
     marking the transition from the river’s upper course to the middle course.
         The geology of the Umbro-Marchean Ridge is made up of Mesozoic pelagic and hemipelagic
     limestones, marly limestones and marls of the Schlier, Bisciaro, Scaglia cinerea, Scaglia rossa,
     Marne a Fucoidi, Maiolica, Bugarone, Corniola, and Calcare massiccio formations (Centamore
     1986). In the Inner Marche Basin, the geology consists of Neogene and Quaternary turbidites and
     pelites, covered by Upper Pleistocene and Holocene alluvial and colluvial deposits. Middle and
     lower Pleistocene river terraces are locally preserved (Centamore 1986).

         Middle valley – Zone 2a
         Pilot zone 2a is a located in the hilly landscape of the middle valley of the Potenza, about 30 km
     from the river mouth. The zone is centred on the village of Passo di Treia, where the river Potenza
     breaks through a small sandstone ridge. On the northern part of the ridge, the town of Treia and
     its nearby Roman precursor Trea were built; on the southern part lies the town of Pollenza and the
     protohistoric site of Monte Franco.
         The zone measures 120 km2, with elevations between 105 m AMSL and 488 m AMSL. The hilly
     landscape consists of small ridges alternating with small and sometimes deeply incised secondary
     valleys, which are connected to the SW-NE oriented Potenza valley. To the northwest, the study
     area encompasses the south-eastern limit of the Cingoli Ridge. Downstream from the Treia-Pollenza
     sandstone ridge, the Potenza develops a wider alluvial plain. The SW–NE oriented interfluves that
     delimit the Potenza drainage basin are made up essentially of upper Miocene and Pliocene pelitic-
     arenitic deposits, with Middle Pleistocene to Holocene alluvial deposits in the valley of the Potenza
     (Centamore 1986).
3. Landscape background and geoarchaeology in the PVS project                                                49




    Middle valley – Zone 2b
    Pilot zone 2b is an extension of zone 2a located immediately downstream, and is centred on the
Roman town site of Ricina (Villa Potenza, Macerata). It encompasses the transition from the middle
valley to the lower valley landscape, and is situated about 15 km from the river mouth. This study
zone differs from the other pilot areas in that it has only been studied by extensive field observations
and not by systematic line walking in the frame of the PVS project.
    Zone 2b measures 30 km2, with elevations ranging between 77 m and 325 m AMSL. The landscape
is dominated by the plain formed by the valley of the Potenza with Holocene and Pleistocene alluvial
deposits (Centamore 1986). Towards the south and north, the Potenza valley gradually evolves
into moderate to high hills oriented parallel to the river valley that are composed of pelitic deposits
(Centamore 1986). South of the Potenza, two ancient terrace levels are preserved. On the northern bank,
five levels can be seen. Roman Ricina was built on an Upper Pleistocene river terrace on the left bank
of the river Potenza (Goethals et al. 2005, p. 44). Apart from the river Potenza, the Monocchietta, the
Cimarella and the Fosso di Cascia are important streams in this zone; all are tributaries of the Potenza.

     Lower valley – Zone 3
     The third pilot area of the PVS project (zone 3) is situated near the mouth of the river Potenza
and measures 112 km2. Elevation in the area ranges from 0 m to 298 m AMSL. The most important
archaeological sites are the Roman colonial town Potentia, lying centrally in the alluvial plain, and
the protohistoric hilltop settlement of Montarice on the northern Potenza interfluve. Other important
centres are the medieval towns of Monte Santo (now: Potenza Picena), Loreto and Recanati.
     Within the coastal area, three main landscapes can be distinguished: the alluvial plains with
Pleistocene and Holocene river terraces, the coastal plain and a landscape of low hills. The lower valley
is, in contrast to the middle and upper valley, very broad. It is delineated by two hilly ridges north and
south of the valley: the hills of Montarice and Colle Burchio in the north, and the Monte dei Priori and
the Monte Maggio, where the present-day town of Potenza Picena is located, in the south. The hills end
in inactive cliffs, and have abrasion platforms at the base, now between 5 m and 6 m AMSL.
     The hills are made up of upper Pliocene to lower Pleistocene blue clays and sandy clays of the
Formazione delle Argille Azzurre (FAA) on top of which lower Pleistocene sandy and clayey loams
and loamy sands of the Formazione di Fermo (FEM) are deposited. Both formations are deposited
in layers that dip in ESE-ward direction as a result of regional uplift. Although the top of these
layers is often consolidated, mass movements and Badlands formation occur frequently. Remnants
of lower Pleistocene marine terraces – gravel deposits of up to more than 5 m thick, sometimes
cemented – are preserved on top of the FAA and FEM formations near the coast (Massari et al.
1986). The alluvial plain has level or sub horizontal soils of Holocene clastic sediments, up to 40 m
thick (Calderoni et al. 2007; Centamore 1986; Cilla, Dramis 1999). Near the coastline, elongated
elevations parallel to the coastline represent ancient beach ridges that are now buried under clayey
alluvial deposits. Behind the beach ridges, loam and clay deposits witness the former presence of
coastal lagoons (Goethals et al. 2009, 432; Vermeulen et al. in press).


  3.3 Geoarchaeological observations in the PVS project

  Apart from the general geomorphologic studies and fieldwork in the four pilot zones, targeted
geoarchaeological research was carried out as well in the PVS project. Methods used in this context
include remote sensing (use of satellite imagery, oblique and vertical aerial photography), field
observations (augering, geophysical survey, topographic survey), soil sample analysis (OSL and
radiocarbon dating (Table 2), mollusc analysis, pollen and macro botanical analysis), and historic-
geographic research (study of ancient maps and documents).
50                                                                                                  Devi Taelman et al.




       The geoarchaeological work was centred on a series of questions related to the archaeological sites
     studied in the valley during the past 15 years. The six most important field observations, relevant for
     the considered time frame of two millennia, are selected here as case studies:
       (1) Preservation of the archaeological record at the Roman site of Trea;
       (2) Study of the water supply of the Roman towns in the valley;
       (3) Assessment of the location in the landscape of the Protohistoric site of Montarice;
       (4) Study of the suburban occupation and landscape context of Potentia in the Roman period;
       (5) River and coastal dynamics at the mouth of the Potenza; from the protohistoric period to the
            present-day;
       (6) Human-landscape relation of the Roman rural settlements in the hinterland of the colony
            of Potentia.

   3.3.1. Archaeological preservation of Roman Trea

    The Roman town site of Trea is situated on a hilly plateau on the left interfluve of the Potenza (c.
308 m AMSL and 70m – 80 m above the present-day valley bottom), which corresponds to a Middle
Pleistocene alluvial terrace composed of poorly sorted sandy-gravel sediments with alternating thin
layers of silt and clay representing alluvial sediments that were deposited in later flooding events.
Archaeological interest in the Roman site started in the 16th century, but it was only in the early
19th century with the first excavations and in the 1970s and 1980s with topographic work that a
basic understanding of the town location was provided (Moscatelli 1988). Since 2003, a more
detailed plan of the urban centre was elaborated by the PVS project resulting from an intensive
multimethod survey approach combining remote sensing techniques (oblique and vertical aerial
photography), geophysical measurements (electrical resistivity and magnetometer survey) and
intensive artefact surveys (cf. Vermeulen et al. 2012 and infra). In the 2013 field campaign, an
evaluation of the subsoil evidence and conservations was achieved (Vermeulen et al. in press).
This systematic augering campaign provided useful information for controlling and confirming the

                          structural evidence provided by the remote sensing operations on the site, mainly aerial photography
                          and geophysical prospections.
                              Today, most of the site area is used for agriculture. Except for some houses and a monastery, the
                          site is free of modern disturbances. Unfortunately, the amplification of agriculture and the lack of
                          awareness concerning this ‘hidden’ archaeological patrimony are a direct threat to the archaeological
                          record. The use of modern agricultural techniques can lead to the fading or disappearance of the visible
                          crop marks and the damaging of the archaeological record. The intensification of agriculture will also
                          increase tillage erosion, especially along the edges of the plateau where the topography is steep.
                              For verifying the survey results, collecting information on the state of conservation of the
                          archaeological record and acquiring insight into the on-site geomorphology and post-depositional
                          processes affecting the conservation and preservation of the archaeological record, the original
                          archaeological survey approach at the site was complemented by a geoarchaeological survey.
                              At Trea, fifty-four hand augerings were conducted in six zones of interest of the town’s intramural
                          area (Fig. 31). The archaeological layer has an average thickness of 1 m, with the exception of
                          some particular zones where the archaeological stratigraphy only reaches a depth of 30 cm. This
                          illustrates that the archaeology at Trea is still well preserved but that the remains are very shallow
                          and, certainly in some locations, heavily threatened by ploughing. Moreover, the steep edges of the
                          site are vulnerable to erosion. The northern border has an inclination of 10 %, which clearly provokes
                          slope erosion. The concentration of archaeological material at this edge is remarkably lower than in
                          the centre of the site (approximately 5 % in the middle of the site versus 0.5 % at the edges).
                              In the north-eastern corner of the site (zone 6) (Fig. 31) the aerial photographs show no crop
                          marks and on the geophysical images only some very disordered traces are visible. A detailed study
                          of the soil profiles in this area indicates the presence of a landslide (Fig. 32). The topsoil is directly
                          succeeded by a very clayey, impervious layer. This clayey layer prevents water infiltration, what will
                          ensure topsoil saturation after heavy or prolonged rainfall events. The sequence of the soil layers
                          and the slope (11 %) create the right conditions for landslides. The investigation of the augerings

shows clearly the presence of erosion processes and the accumulation of archaeological material
at the foot of the slope. At the foot of the slope, there is a 2.1 m thick layer of archaeological
material; the colluvial characteristics were confirmed by the low percentage and heavy mixture of
the archaeological material.


   3.3.2. Water supply of the Roman towns in the valley of the river Potenza: Ricina, Trea
   and Septempeda

    The Roman town of Ricina is built on an Upper Pleistocene river terrace, on the transition of
the middle to the lower Potenza valley, about 15 km from the Adriatic coast. Geoarchaeological
work on Roman Ricina centred in particular on two sets of crop marks visible on the oblique
aerial photography related to the water supply of the abandoned Roman town: (1) a meandering
palaeochannel, north of the city; and (2) remains of a possible aqueduct system.
    Electrical resistivity profile measurements were carried out to determine the exact location of the
ancient rivulet and to characterise its nature. Hand augerings at locations with the highest resistivity
values in the electrical resistivity profile revealed upward fining sequences with multiple gravel
layers corresponding to consecutive thalwegs (Goethals et al. 2005, 45). Further, geomorphologic
observations show that that the channel was originally connected to the Fosso Cimarella. At a certain
moment, the palaeochannel was abandoned and redirected to debouche in the Fosso Monocchietta
(Goethals et al. 2005, 45). In the populous post-medieval period, the need for reclamation of
marginal land was high (Coltorti 1997). Thus it is likely that the stream was diverted in this period,
simultaneously with the river diversions in the coastal plain (see case study: River Dynamics at the
mouth of the Potenza).
    Furthermore, oblique aerial photography revealed a series of rectangular anomalies of
approximately 5 m by 2 m, spaced 6 m apart, aligned almost perfectly in north–south direction in
the field between the Fosso Monocchietta and the Potenza. Augerings showed that the crop marks
occur on river terrace gravels covered with a 40 cm thick layer of finer sediments. At the crop marks,
the gravel begins at 55 cm depth, slightly deeper than the natural level, as if a trench was made to
host a pole or pillar. The trench was filled with finer sediments (Goethals et al. 2005, 46). The crop
marks can be interpreted as evidence of a former, spoliated aqueduct. A possible captation point for
the aqueduct was detected near a present-day diversion channel of the Potenza used for producing
hydroelectricity, c. 4 km south of the town. On the 4 km between the diversion of the channel and
the arrival south of the city, the altitude gain of the channel over the Potenza is 20 m (Goethals et
al. 2005, 46). The age of the aqueduct anomalies could not be determined. Despite the absence of
conclusive evidence, a Roman age is considered likely given the vicinity of the Roman town, the
orientation of the structure towards the area of the theatre, and the absence of any major post-Roman
settlement in the area.
    Similar observations were made for the water supply of the Roman towns of Trea and Septempeda.
For Trea, fragments of Roman terracotta hydraulic pipes were found in several locations during
surveys at a distance between c. 1100 m and 1500 m west of the town. The aqueduct system leads to
a water-rich spring area just east of the present-day San Lorenzo sanctuary where similar terracotta
pipe fragments and fragments of calcium carbonate deposits formed on the inside of the pipes were
found. All archaeological and geomorphological observations suggest that the aqueduct followed a
straight line just north of the S.P.N. 169 modern road entering Trea near the presumed western gate
(Moscatelli 1988; Vermeulen et al. 2009, 104). During terrain observations on and near Septempeda,
we were able to locate the most evident source area for water in the form of a still active source,
situated immediately north of the central part of the town, no doubt originally linked with the thermal
installations (Vermeulen et al. 2009, p. 106).

   3.3.3 Natural and anthropogenic defensive system of the protohistoric site of Montarice

    The protohistoric site of Montarice has a long settlement history between the Bronze Age and
early Roman occupation of the Potenza basin, and was particularly active as a settlement area during
the Iron Age (see infra). The site is located near the present-day mouth of the river Potenza, on top of
a hilly plateau with an excellent view of the lower Potenza valley and the Adriatic Sea. The hilltop
and upper slopes measure almost 7 hectares with steep slopes (c. 25° – 35°) on all sides except on




                                                                                                           Fig. 33 Result and
                                                                                                           interpretation of
                                                                                                           the magnetometry
                                                                                                           survey (Eastern
                                                                                                           Atlas) and location
                                                                                                           of the resistivity
                                                                                                           profile at Montarice.


the east, offering natural protection. The hill consists of a substrate of marine sandstones and clays
covered by an eastward-dipping 15 m thick gravel layer, representing a Pleistocene marine terrace
(Goethals et al. 2005, p. 41).
    Geoarchaeological research has only recently started on the site. In 2013, a campaign was aimed
at obtaining a better understanding of the structures along the northern edge of the hilltop plateau
that were detected by the field observations in the PVS project (since 2001) by way of the aerial
photography and geophysical survey. Along this N slope of the plateau, a resistivity profile was laid
out, with the intention of providing information about the structures in this area (Fig. 33). To verify
56                                                                                                           Devi Taelman et al.




                         and enhance the results of the resistivity survey, ten augerings were undertaken along the 100 m long
                         resistivity profile.
                             The resulting 2D section of the resistivity distribution shows a sequence of high and low
                         resistivity layers underneath a topsoil layer (Fig. 34). This sequence contains two zones in the
                         section from 45 m to 65 m and from 77 m to 86 m, respectively, of high resistivity values between
                         100 Ωm and 500 Ωm. Between 65 m to 77 m and 34 m to 45 m layers of low resistivity values of
                         about 30 Ωm appear. In the lowest part of the section from 0 m to 20 m and at the end of the profile
                         on the top of Montarice hill very low resistivity of less than 30 Ωm were modelled. Assuming a
                         direct correlation of resistivity values and grain size, sections with high resistivity values correspond
                         to coarse sediments such as gravel.
                             The results of the augerings were largely consistent with the interpretation of the resistivity
                         survey. The detected resistivity distribution could mostly be explained by the natural stratigraphy
                         except for the high resistivity zones between 77 m and 86 m. Here, a layer of humic, heterogeneous,
                         clayey to silty sediments mixed with ceramic material below topsoil indicates anthropogenic infill
                         material. This anthropogenic horizon likely reflects an artificial fill to level the top of the hill that
                         was deposited against a terrace wall and/or defensive structure. After the abandonment of the site,
                         the artificial fill was covered by colluvium mixed with ceramic building material originating from
                         the top of the hill.


                            3.3.4 Landscape setting of Roman Potentia and its suburban area

                             The Roman town of Potentia lies in the coastal plain of the river Potenza, c. 1 km south of its
                         present mouth and of the modern town of Porto Recanati, about 100 m inland from the Adriatic
                         coastline. The town was founded as a Roman colony in 184 BC and was abandoned in the Early
                         Middle Ages (Alfieri 1981).
                             The site is archaeologically quite well-known thanks to excavations and topographic studies
                         carried out in the second half of the 20th century (cf. Alfieri 1949; Mercando 1979; Mercando et al.
                         1974; Moscatelli 1985; Percossi Serenelli 1990, 1995). Since 2000, intensive field investigations in
                         the frame of the PVS project identified many features of the ancient urban topography. This research
                         included remote sensing analysis (based on oblique aerial, vertical aerial and satellite imagery),
                         artefact survey, and geophysical survey (Vermeulen 2012; Vermeulen et al. 2009; Vermeulen et al.
                         2005; Vermeulen, Verhoeven 2006). Apart from a site-oriented approach and a general landscape

 Fig. 34 Resistivity
              profile,
       interpretation
(Eastern Atlas) and
   augerings along
      the N slope at
          Montarice.
3. Landscape background and geoarchaeology in the PVS project                                            57




approach (see infra), the relation of the town with its immediate environment was examined from
a geoarchaeological perspective. The central questions deal with the town’s position in the coastal
landscape and the extension of the suburban, funerary area immediately east of the town.
    Several field methods were combined. For the detection of micro topographical features, a
high-precision digital elevation model using total station measurements (DEM-1) was generated in
2007 for the whole urban area (Vermeulen, Verhoeven 2006). The results of DEM-1 were recently
compared with the outcome of an airborne LiDAR-based digital elevation model (DEM-2) that was
used to examine the topographical surface of the town’s immediate suburban zones (Vermeulen et al.
in press). The geomorphologic setting of the area was defined by hand augering. Finally, the urban
and immediate suburban areas were subjected to an extensive magnetic gradiometry prospection and
an electrical resistivity survey to map subsurface structures and underlying landscape features. An
overview of the archaeological traces that allowed reconstructing a plan of the colonial settlement
will be discussed in a later chapter. In this part, we focus on the landscape aspects.
    The geoarchaeological observations illustrated that the Roman town was constructed on top of
an NS-oriented, ancient beach ridge offering protection against potential floods of the river Potenza
that flowed immediately south of the city walls in the Roman period (Fig. 35) (Goethals et al. 2009).
The beach ridge is characterised by an asymmetric profile, with a steeper seaward-dipping slope (c.
5°) and a gently landward-dipping surface (c. 2°), and has a maximum width of c. 300 m (Fig. 36).
Following Coltorti (1997), the formation of the beach ridge must have been the result of higher
sediment loads for the river Potenza caused by population growth, increased agricultural pressure
on the lands and deforestation in the Bronze and Iron ages (c. 2200 BC – 300 BC). Radiocarbon and
optically stimulated luminescence (OSL) dating of auger samples, however, place the formation of
the beach ridge somewhat earlier, in the Chalcolithic and Early Bronze Age, between at the earliest
3953 – 3715 cal BC, and 3.5 ± 0.4 ka BP and 3.3 ± 0.5 ka BP (Goethals et al. 2009, p. 432). The wet
depressions that were created behind the beach ridge were gradually infilled and transformed into a
marshy area or an area with seasonal floods as testified by thinly laminated silty sediments. In the
late medieval or post-medieval period, the beach ridge was cut in the southeast and part of the town
was eroded by displacement of the ancient Potenza (see case study: River dynamics at the mouth of
the Potenza) (Goethals et al. 2009, pp. 433-434). Contemporary with the erosion of the southern
part of the Potentia beach ridge and the Potenza displacement, the depression west of the beach
ridge was further filled with floodplain deposits (Coltorti 1991; Goethals et al. 2009).
    To achieve more detailed landscape information about the extension of the funerary area
extending to the west of Potentia and the main road exiting the town in western direction, detected
by magnetic survey, a N–S oriented resistivity transect of 74.25 m long was laid out. In addition, a
NNW–SSE transect of 13 augerings, perpendicular to the Roman road was performed (Fig. 37). The
augering transect largely coincided with the resistivity transect to combine the stratigraphy from the
resistivity imaging with soil samples. The location of some of the hand augerings was also based
on anomalies detected by the geophysical surveys. The relatively high resistivity values, higher
than 20 Ωm, from 39 m to 57 m and from 59 m to 60 m in the resistivity profile correspond with
buried archaeological structures. Underneath the topsoil, a highly conductive layer with a depth of
2 m refers to a clayey soil with highly conductive groundwater such as brackish water or saltwater
intrusions. The presence of the Roman road was verified at a depth of 50 cm to 64 cm below the
present-day surface (Vermeulen et al. in press). By combining the results of resistivity and augering
survey, it became clear that the Roman road and the adjoining funerary zone, which according to the
aerial photography imagery included several funerary monuments (cf. infra), were built on top of an
artificially constructed platform with a width of about 20 m and a maximum thickness of 100 cm, in
order to elevate the area above the wetland area behind the Potentia beach ridge and to protect the
area from floods. The material used for constructing the platform consisted of flattened, elongated
and rounded gravel, corresponding to beach ridge material.

    That the western suburban area of Potentia was part of a wetland during the Late Republic and
Imperial times is also demonstrated in the contemporaneous pollen record obtained from a series of
pollen analyses on soil samples from stratigraphic contexts of the western gate sector excavated by
Ghent University between 2007 and 2010 (Vermeulen et al. 2011). For the Late Republican period,
the pollen record (analysed by C.A.A. Giorgio Nicoli S.r.l.) points to an open landscape with only
limited, essentially deciduous, forest cover with mostly oak (Quercus), hornbeam (Carpino) maple
(Acer campestre), elm (Ulmus), hazel (Corylus) in the wider surroundings of the town. To a lesser
degree hygrophilous forest with poplar (Populus), willow (Salix) and alder (Alnus) were present
in the landscape. Halophytic plants indicate locally brackish conditions with occasional marine
ingression. Human activity around Potentia is visible in the pollen record as cultivated species such
as cereals (wheat (Avena-Triticum), barley (Hordeum) and millet (Panicum miliaceum)) and small-
scale vegetable and fruit horticulture (including chicory (Cichorium cf. intybus), fennel (Foeniculum
vulgare) mustard (Cruciferae), cabbage (Brassica), and stone fruits (Prunus) such as plum and
cherry. The pollen record further shows the presence of grasslands, possibly used for pasture or
from fallow plots. In Imperial times, a shift can be seen towards denser hygrophilous forestation,
with an increase in grassland. Crop, vegetable and fruit cultivation are less important. Herbaceous
taxa typical of wetlands, mainly lesser reedmace (Typha angustifolia) and reed (Phragmites cf.
palustris), document the existence of watercourses or channels with permanent water near the site.
Finally, the medieval vegetation landscape near Potentia is characterised essentially by an open
vegetation of meadows and reduced cultivation, against a background of hygrophilous deciduous
forest cover.


   3.3.5 River dynamics at the mouth of the Potenza

    At present, the river Potenza flows in the northern part of its alluvial plain. However, the river
did not always follow this course, as coastal and fluvial alternations were frequent in this landscape
during the Holocene. Several researchers (cf. Alfieri, Ortolani 1947) had already proposed
multiple hypotheses of ancient riverbed locations for the river Potenza. However, these hypotheses
were based exclusively on historical and/or archaeological information. Since the PVS project
aimed at understanding the location of archaeological sites in their wider landscape environment,
a key target for the coastal area of the project was to reconstruct the Potenza fluvial dynamics.
To elucidate this situation in the lower valley, extensive geoarchaeological fieldwork gathered
all relevant geomorphologic information and information on the physical geography of the area.
Specific techniques employed were aerial photography analysis, geomorphologic observations,
geoelectrical resistivity surveys, hand augerings, and historical and archaeological study (Corsi et
al. 2009; Goethals et al. 2009; Goethals et al. 2006; Vermeulen, Verhoeven 2006).
    Throughout the alluvial plain of the lower Potenza valley, crop and soil marks can be observed
that indicate the presence of previous riverbeds. Near the coastline, two major palaeochannels can
be seen as former Potenza mouths. Both palaeochannels are situated south of the Roman colony of
Potentia, and were observed as crop and/or soil marks on the aerial imagery.
    The most southern palaeochannel (n. 1) displays an anastomosing pattern on vertical and
oblique aerial imagery, corresponding to gravel outcrops in the field. The ancient riverbed was
further confirmed in an electrical resistivity profile and an augering transect. The thalweg is
represented by a gravel bed, bordered on both sides by a c. 10 m wide transition zone, followed
by two sandy riverbanks of 20 m to 30 m wide. The surrounding clays represent floodplain
sediments. Inland, this palaeochannel showed a typical asymmetric profile of a meandering
river channel, with lateral accretion surfaces building up at the inner bend of the meander
and erosion at the outer bend of the meander. The top of its gravel bed was dated 3.6 ± 0.4 ka

                           pp. 434-435).
                               A second, large palaeochannel (n. 2) could be observed on aerial imagery immediately south
                           of Potentia (Fig. 38). The thalweg of this channel runs underneath the Roman bridge that is partly
                           preserved in the foundations of the 18th-century farm of Casa dell’Arco. At this more inland
                           location, a typical fluvial upward fining with a base of gravel and coarse sand, covered by fine sand
                           and silt deposits and topped off by sandy clays, points towards a migrating meandering river with
                           the sedimentary sequence representing respectively the riverbed, the riverbank and the alluvial
                           plain (Fig. 39). Organic material from the top of the gravel bed was radiocarbon dated to 630 ±
                           25 BP (1288 – 1397 cal CE, at 95.4 %). Two additional radiocarbon samples, taken higher in the
                           profile, representing the deposition of the sandy riverbank deposits and of the clayey floodplain
                           deposits, were dated 505 ± 25 BP (1400 – 1445 cal CE, at 95.4 %) and 80 ± 25 BP (1692 – 1920 cal
                           CE, at 95.4 %). These dates show that the Roman channel remained in use until the late medieval
                           period, after which the river gradually started migrating. The final date illustrates that the alluvial
                           plain was still subject to regular floods until the late 19th century or early 20th century (Goethals
                           et al. 2009, p. 436). Downstream from the Casa dell’Arco, the ancient river is visible as a deltaic
                           river channel with bifurcating channels. Topographic correlation of the ancient riverbeds shows

                         obtained more inland, where the bottom of the riverbank and the bottom of a layer of floodplain

                         430-431, 433-434).
                             After 1453–1635 cal CE, the late medieval and post-medieval course was completely
                         abandoned and an artificial course for the Potenza was created along the northern side of the
                         river’s alluvial plain. Evidence for this artificial alteration of the river course has been observed
                         as an abrupt change in the river morphology about 3 km upstream. At this point the river changes
                         from a broad bed with abundant gravel bars to a narrow and deeply cut channel-like bed. At this
                         location, a breakthrough was created in a higher situated alluvial fan of the Fosso Grande (a
                         tributary of the Potenza) (Goethals et al. 2009). This new course certainly existed in the post-
                         medieval period as is evidenced by an inscription on a bridge in Porto Recanati stating (Alfieri,
                         Ortolani 1947):

                                  “Arcus hic est pars pontis Potentiae veteris dictae le Fiumarelle.”
                                  This arch is a part of the bridge over the old Potenza, the so-called Fiumarelle.

                             Ancient maps and illustrations show that this artificial course originally flowed through the town
                         of Porto Recanati, debouching in the northern part of the town, and was only later moved to its
                         present-day mouth just south of the modern town (Corsi et al. 2009). Due to the development of
                         the modern city of Porto Recanati this ancient course cannot be determined. It can be assumed that
                         the main reason for this large-scale human impact on the Potenza course is related to the search for
                         additional agricultural lands to cope with an increased demographic pressure in Renaissance and
                         later periods (Cencini, Varani 1991; Nanni, Vivalda 1997).
3. Landscape background and geoarchaeology in the PVS project                                               63




    Based on the currently available data, it is clear that the river Potenza displaced its course
multiple times since the Bronze Age. An older channel was detected centrally in the alluvial plain
of the Potenza. Through avulsion, the river changed its course abruptly to the north. This channel,
found just south of the city wall of Potentia, was active during the Roman and medieval periods. In
the late medieval and post-medieval periods, the debouchment of the Potenza changed to a deltaic
system that eventually eroded and destroyed the southern part of the Roman town. This event is
probably related to a reduced attention to maintain the river channel. After 1453–1635 cal CE, the
present course of the river in the north of its alluvial plain was artificially created, most likely for
land reclamation for agriculture as a response to population growth.


   3.3.6 Roman rural settlements in the hinterland of Potentia

    Apart from the focus on the landscape setting of the town of Potentia and the evolution of
its river, the environmental setting of smaller Roman sites in the direct rural hinterland of the
Roman colony was geoarchaeologically investigated. The landscape reconstruction is achieved by
comprehensive research of relevant literature, oblique aerial photographs, vertical satellite imagery,
topography, geologic maps, and by geomorphologic field survey and hand augerings. These new
data were finally confronted with the data obtained from archaeological surveys and excavations
and, where available, geophysical surveys, all summarised in the catalogue of sites in this book.
    In the lower Potenza valley, four major landforms can be distinguished that were occupied by
Roman rural sites: ancient beach ridges, the alluvial plain of the Potenza, hill slopes, and hill crests.
For each of these landforms, a settlement site with artefact survey information was selected for
geoarchaeological study:
    • Ancient beach ridges – site of Casa Cingolani. Sites along the beach ridge are connected
    to the Roman coastal road linking all river valleys in central Adriatic Italy (Moscatelli 1985;
    Vermeulen et al. 2013).
    • Alluvial plain – site of Casa Apis. Only few sites have been detected during archaeological
    surveys in the alluvial plain, probably due to a thick sequence of alluvial sedimentation from
    frequent post-Roman floods. The alluvial plain, if sufficiently drained, was undoubtedly very
    suited for agricultural purposes in the Roman period, as is shown by the presence of a centuriation
    system dividing the land (Corsi, Vermeulen 2010).
    • Hill slope – site of San Girio. The hill slopes of the northern and southern interfluve hills mark
    the contact zone between the hillcrest and the alluvial plain so that they could profit from the
    advantages of both landforms. Moreover, the slopes were favourable for agricultural activities,
    such as viniculture that needs sun and a good drainage (Vermeulen et al. 2013).
    • Hillcrest – site of Montarice Villa. On the hillcrests, many Roman and protohistoric sites have
    been found. The general property of these locations is the good overview of the valley plain
    (Vermeulen et al. 2013).

    Ancient beach ridge – Site of Casa Cingolani (site 119)
    The site of Casa Cingolani has recently (2015) been reinterpreted as an area of min 5 hectares
occupied by a Roman hamlet with several house units. The settlement was inhabited only during the
3rd/2nd century BC, with a possible continuity of a limited occupation or some use of the site later
in the Roman period. The site was located along the ancient beach ridge offering good connection to
the town of Potentia, the coastline and the alluvial plain.
    The aim of the geoarchaeological observations was to reconstruct the past environment of the
Roman site, verify the crop marks present on aerial imagery and evaluate the connection between
the beach ridge, the palaeochannel and the archaeological site (Fig. 40). The augerings verified
64                                                                                                        Devi Taelman et al.




                         the pale crop mark as an ancient beach ridge (thick layer of elongated, rounded, fine gravel with
                         coarse sand) and the meandering crop mark as a paleochannel (fluviatile system of coarse and
                         fine sediments) that cuts through the beach ridge in certain periods and therefore developed later
                         than the beach ridge formation, i.e. later than 3.5 ± 0.4 ka BP and 3.3 ± 0.5 ka BP as illustrated
                         by the OSL samples from the top of the beach ridge taken more to the north (Goethals et al.
                         2009). No chronological information is yet available for the abandonment of the palaeochannel
                         making it impossible to confirm that this streamlet was an extra factor for the position of this early
                         (colonist?) hamlet.
