    Alluvial plain – Site of Casa Apis (site 121)
    The site of Casa Apis is a Roman farm with a long occupation span from the 2nd century BC
to the 5th century AD. The site lies in the alluvial plain of the Potenza, a fertile location with good
agricultural properties, but also sensitive to inundations.
    The geoarchaeological focus of the site dealt with the specific location of the Roman farm in
the alluvial plain, near the ancient river bed and within the presumed centuriation system, and
its relation to the palaeolandscape. The augering sequences along the transect perpendicular to a
pale, curvilinear soil mark visible on aerial images show a top layer of fine loam or clayey alluvial
inundation sediments. Sporadic gravel particles in this upper layer are ploughed up material from the
thalweg of a palaeochannel, and explain the pale soil mark on the aerial imagery. Below the alluvial
inundation sediments, the channel gravel bed was observed that seems to be part of a anastomosing
river system. Comparison of the augering transect with the artefact survey results show that the
nucleus of the archaeological site was located exactly on top of the former riverbed. This can be
explained by the slightly higher topographic position of the former riverbed as a result of differential
compaction of gravel versus finer material such as sand, loam and clay. This landscape position
guaranteed the site protection against regular inundations and offered an easy extraction of water
and control of the centuriation area of the town Potentia.

    Hill slope – Site of San Girio (site 124)
    The site of San Girio is marked by the remains of a typical villa rustica that had a long-term
occupation from the 2nd century BC to the 5th century AD. It is located on the northern flank of the
Monte dei Priori, about 40 m above the present-day Potenza valley floor. The gradient of the slope
is around 15°. The site was first detected in the 1970s, and was thoroughly examined by the PVS
project mostly by means of artefact survey, aerial photography and geophysical survey. Important
elements that came to light were structures of the main building and the possible presence of private
bathing facilities.
    The aim of the geoarchaeological work was to evaluate to what extent the owners adapted the
original slope to accommodate an extensive villa compound at this location. A second objective
dealt with the water supply for the bathing facilities.
    The research first revealed that San Girio lies on a slope with two hydrological networks that
each have a concave and a convex part, the shape of which are most likely created by neotectonic
activities. During the Mid-Pliocene there was a period of uplifting allowing for an incision by a
hydrographical system, which led in turn to a concave and convex slope in this area. Two phases of
uplifting of the area must have taken place since there is a second concave and convex part in the
slope present today. The last and lowest concave part of the slope has been filled afterwards with
alluvium. The archaeological site is located at the end of the highest concave part and this for two
reasons. The concave parts of the slopes have a lower inclination which makes it less sensitive to
erosion. Moreover, there is less flood risk on the highest concave part, contrary to the lower concave
slope. However, the concave slope remains relatively steep on macroscale. For this reason, the
Romans probably built an artificial terrace to erect their villa here, a procedure that is still current
practice for the habitants of the region today. This hypothesis was further supported by the hand
augerings that have been carried out around the site in 2012, based on the changing depths and relief
of the substrate in the core profiles. In total ten hand augering have been executed, parallel as well
as perpendicular to the slope. In 2013, a magnetometer survey fully confirmed this presence of an
artificial terracing by locating a substantial, min 125 m long, terrace wall.
    A different but as important environmental aspect for the location and sustainability of large villa
sites is the water supply. A lot of tesserae and some tubuli have been found during field prospection
indicating a possible thermal complex during part of the Roman occupation period. If this hypothesis is
true, then there should be a place where the villa inhabitants could extract water from. Geomorphological
66                                                                                         Devi Taelman et al.




     survey revealed a spring some 200 m upslope, located on the convex and thus erosive part of the slope.
     Hence, the colluvial layer is thinner here and the chance for a disclosure of water is much higher. The
     spring lies ideally between a gravel terrace and a sandy layer. Usually sand is considered to be an
     aquifer, but in this case it serves as an aquitard in comparison with the gravel lying above it. Nowadays
     there is a pond at the spring, which still contains water in the driest period of the year. Therefore, there
     would have been enough water available throughout the year for supplying the Roman villa.

         Hillcrest – Site of Montarice Villa (site 101)
         On the archaeological site of Montarice Villa, surface evidence was found of the presence of
     a Roman farm or villa, inhabited from the 2nd century BC to the 2nd century AD with possible
     continuation in Late Antiquity. The site is located on top of the hilly ridge delimiting the north side
     of the Potenza valley and has great visual control over the valley bottom and the coastline.
         The geoarchaeological aim of this study was the assessment of the specific landscape location
     of the site and its sustainability as good emplacement for Roman farms. Field observations and four
     augerings on the hillcrest and the southern slope showed that the site lies on top of a Pleistocene river
     terrace that is composed of gravel layers with some clayey lenses that hint towards an anastomosing
     river system. The site is located on a gravel layer of this system offering a better drainage than the
     surrounding clayey area.
         The site offers not only good visibility and a safe location away from inundations, but also a
     good potential for wine cultivation on the adjacent slopes, today and in the past. However, a possible
     reason for the earlier abandonment of this site is the absence of flowing water near the site from
     where a local captation or aquaduct could be organised. The area and thickness of the layer upslope
     that can hold the water is too limited, but the farm could possibly have used a well, just like the
     nearby farm houses today. The depth of the water table is c. 21 m below the surface at the point of
     the present day well.


       3.4 Conclusion

       This chapter has presented an overview of the geomorphologic and geoarchaeological research that
     has been carried out in the frame of the Potenza Valley Survey project. The intensive archaeological
     survey work of the PVS project has identified a large number of protohistoric, Roman and early
     medieval sites in the valley of the river Potenza. To better understand the site distribution patterns
     through time in relation to site visibility and preservation and to address the relation of the landscape
     environment and human occupation, it is essential to evaluate the survey results in a geomorphologic
     and geoarchaeological framework.
       In part 1, the tectonic, geologic and geomorphologic setting of the wider Marche region, the
     Potenza basin and the PVS pilot zones are presented. In part 2, specific geoarchaeological case
     studies of the PVS project are presented and their main results are summarised. The results of this
     approach will be used to evaluate the settlement dynamics in the Potenza corridor, and to investigate
     how human occupation responded to changing environmental settings (see chapters 4 and 5).
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                       67




4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley
Frank Vermeulen, Devi Taelman, Francesca Carboni, Wieke de Neef


  4.1 Surveying central settlements and town sites


  F    rom the start of the Potenza Valley Survey project, and in particular from 2005 onwards, when a
       second major phase in the prospection activities throughout this Adriatic valley initiated, much
attention was given to a series of large protohistoric and early historic sites with a probable central
function in the settlement system of their time. To obtain a good understanding of the settlement
nodes in the ancient communications networks, around which most dynamics of human behaviour
in the valley must have pivoted, intensive archaeological intra-site field survey work was carried out
on a series of large and often complex sites. There are four important Bronze and Iron Age centres in
the valley, as well as the four Roman towns that dominated the Potenza region and that were spread
quite evenly along this river corridor. From the mountainous western fringes to the coastal eastern
edge these relatively large complexes are respectively: the protohistoric hilltop sites of Monte Primo,
Monte Pitino, Monte Franco, and Montarice, and the Roman urban centres of Septempeda, Trea,
Ricina, and Potentia. It seems that most protohistoric sites were (almost) totally abandoned in Roman
Imperial times while the Roman towns had gradually disappeared by the early Middle Ages. As a
result, and because most of them contain today only sparse occupation, they all have reasonable to
excellent potential for non-destructive surface survey applications. Therefore, and also because of the
very limited excavation activities carried out on these sites previous to our investigation of the area,
much could be learned from an integrated survey approach (Vermeulen 2013a,b).
   Parts of these ancient settlements were already discovered or noticed in the past. The contribution
of a systematic reconnaissance via remote sensing and via focused field observations and
measurements of their surface and subsurface situation lies, therefore, not so much in their initial
finding, but more in their full comprehension as former agglomerations or focal places. This
includes a first appreciation of their total size and main layout (e.g. the presence of defences or wall
circuits, street network, entrances, main building structures, …), their relation to the surrounding
landscape (roads or paths, the river, field systems, extra-mural areas…), their general chronology
and evolution, etc. The specificity of Roman townscapes, but to a certain degree also of pre-Roman
centres, that often stand out thanks to their position in the landscape, their monumental demarcation,
good architectural visibility, or the homogeneity of the application of certain architectural models
and plans, is of course a great help in this. This applies in particular to the Late Republican and early
Imperial phases of the four fully urbanised sites in the valley, when townscapes were created which
left us good archaeological visibility, at least from a non-invasive survey point of view. The sheer
size of the former population centres and of certain architectural creations, the systematic choice
for durable building materials and the sometimes quite impressive impact of the central place on
the local topography, may all contribute well to the sometimes very revealing present-day visions
from the air or via geophysical prospection in the field, even if very few structural traces of these
sites remain visible above ground today. And even if, after ploughing, the surface of some of these
sites is still littered today with archaeological remains that can be systematically recorded by grid-
based artefact survey and traditional topographic documentation, the application of repeated aerial
survey and in some cases of active multi-method geophysical prospections, as described in an earlier
chapter, has allowed the team to discover and add important new information to our knowledge of
the settlements.
   As these large, but vulnerable sites are currently the landmarks of the regional archaeology,
their study is essential for the comprehension of settlement history in this part of Italy. They not
only behold the key to many answers archaeologists wish to obtain from the buried ‘archive’, but
68                                                                                    Frank Vermeulen et al.




     are also the standard bearers of cultural tourism and have a great potential for divulgation to the
     wider public. Their preservation, adequate study and efficient management are, therefore, of prime
     importance for regional and national archaeological authorities. At the same time it is understood
     that their investigation with a palimpsest of non-invasive survey techniques is not as simple as for
     the many smaller sites encountered in the valley. In particular when these central sites are located
     in active agricultural landscapes it is seldom possible to photograph or scan them totally and ideally
     during one flight or field operation only. Whichever the specific climatic conditions or season, and
     whichever the type of crops or vegetation covering the many different parts of the extensive site,
     regular and numerous flying, as well as repeated field visits for geophysical or artefact prospections,
     or for augerings and topographic measurements, are needed. We are convinced that monitoring of
     these large sites over several campaigns and years is needed for a certain comprehension of the many
     archaeological structures and the complexity present in the soil, and regular visits and scanning are
     the only possible approaches to map and understand the multiple features or the diachronic use of
     the sites. But, despite the current great variety of means to orthorectify the often oblique imagery
     obtained via active remote sensing and to process and integrate within a GIS-based mapping
     environment subtle and high resolution data from artefact pickups or geophysical measurements,
     their archaeological information will not be exploited efficiently as long as the essentially non-
     stratigraphical data is not thoroughly interpreted. A full understanding of these ancient cityscapes or
     large village-like settlements, based on combining the interpreted evidence from various prospection
     methods, does not mean trying to identify only the elements that indicate past human activities
     related to the occupation in antiquity, but involves using all present-day landscape features as
     elements of contrast or site formation, that help to bring out the residual components of the ancient
     landscape. Furthermore, it is of great importance that the sometimes easily recognisable and regular
     Roman features are not the only ones to be filtered out, but also less linear structures and marks that
     might belong to earlier phases of occupation, when organic growth of a settlement and adaptation to
     the local geomorphology were far more decisive characteristics.


       4.2 Protohistoric centres with some continuity


        4.2.1 Monte Primo

         The oldest but also still somewhat enigmatic centralising site studied during the PVS project is
     located on Monte Primo (site 003), in the mountainous Umbrian-Picene contact zone near Camerino.
     The position of this currently still uninhabited central site, located on a hilltop that visually dominates
     the north-south intra-montane basin of the Apennines, was probably crucial for the formation of
     territorial nucleation. The site, situated at an elevation of c. 1300 m and overlooking the narrow
     gorge leading to the upland segment of the Potenza valley, was once probably the location of an
     impressive open-air sanctuary. Very limited excavations and the discovery of a deposit of bronze
     statuettes, including some late Archaic statuettes (Lollini 1979), have yielded mostly Bronze
     Age material with some evidence of Iron Age presence suggested also by our surveys (Boullart
     2003). These recent prospections were limited to three organised visits to the steep and difficult to
     reach hilltop location, including the taking of DGPS measurements, and to regular oblique aerial
     photographic monitoring of the site during different seasons. As LiDAR data are not available for
     this area, a combination of our remote sensing and measurement efforts was needed to get a grip
     on this site. The aerial photographs, enhanced by recent efforts to turn this imagery in a 3D model
     and an orthophoto of the site (Verhoeven 2016), allowed now also a more detailed interpretative
     mapping of the archaeologically significant surface features.
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                                   69




                                                                                                       Fig. 41 Topographic
                                                                                                       setting and
                                                                                                       mapping of the
                                                                                                       archaeological
                                                                                                       features at the
                                                                                                       protohistoric
                                                                                                       mountain-top site of
                                                                                                       Monte Primo.


    Five imposing concentric but rather irregular enclosures incorporate a central activity area of
almost two hectares. To the west, where the steep slope (c. 30°) of the mountain is clearly visible,
these enclosures are much eroded by both wind and water. Elsewhere, the enclosures are still well
preserved as earthen banks with an average height of c. 2 m, covered by short grassland vegetation
typical for the still much used summer grazing lands of present-day cattle farmers. Due to general
erosion processes, it proved impossible to find any convincing traces of ditches, although one can
assume that they must have paralleled the banks and be connected with the material that was taken
to build the rampart. The erosion processes most likely filled them up, leaving only a network of
banks whose broadest sides are located downslope. The small central enclosure of the whole system,
which is linked to a rock outcrop, surrounds the top of the hill. This was no doubt the focus of the
sanctuary, with the best view on the surrounding landscape and closest proximity to the sky. The
70                                                                                   Frank Vermeulen et al.




     obviously manmade enclosures surrounding the central site are connected on their southern side
     with a series of similar, only slightly lower banks, that seem to form almost 500 m long passageways
     – for cattle and people ? – in the direction of the intra-montane basin to the east, and ultimately to
     the much lower lying area of Camerino, where the main centralised settlement of this area between
     the rivers Potenza and Chienti – the Umbrian and later Roman town of Camerinum – ultimately
     arose. A series of narrow trackways cross several of the banks, and it cannot be excluded that these
     are remnants of original paths and entrances. Several gullies mapped on the site allow us to presume
     that water collection was possible here in the past, while at least one big rock outcrop incorporated
     in the earthen banks suggests a function as ancient quarry for the use of limestone in the construction
     of the enclosures of the complex. Permanent thick grassland all over the site prevented us from
     the collection of surface finds, even if a small pit made by illicit grave robbers allowed for the
     recuperation of impasto pottery sherds and burnt and unburnt bone fragments of sheep/goat, cattle
     and pork. Only regular and well-focused excavations can reveal the exact purpose and chronology
     of the site, but a tentative first interpretation of the prospection data can be proposed here.
         The hilltop site at Monte Primo is clearly the odd one out in our list of central sites, as it is not
     likely that this mountain top site was ever inhabited permanently. The still limited data allow us at
     present to possibly interpret it as a periodical aggregation site for special activities (feasting, cattle
     mustering etc.) and as a communal sanctuary (Bonomi Ponzi 1992). Such mountain-top sanctuaries,
     often connected with seasonal transhumance activities, are typical for the central Apennines,
     especially in Samnium and Umbria (Letta 1992; Conati Barbaro et al. 2003; Malone, Stoddart
     1994) and they generally have a long duration of use. Archaeological data are currently insufficient
     to understand how long Monte Primo was used as sacred site for this community, but the place was
     surely very suited for the ritual observation of the flight of birds and the auspicio, one of the crucial
     elements of ancient Umbrian religion, as is essentially known from the Iguvine Tablets (3rd-1st
     century BC) found at Gubbio. According to sources other than the Tables, the adoration of Hercules,
     as also evidenced by the discovery of late Archaic bronze statuettes found elsewhere in the central
     Adriatic region (e.g. at Rimini and Isola di Fano), was much followed in eastern peripheral parts of
     the Umbrian territories. The attraction of the ‘Hercules function’ within a more Martian sphere is
     also attested at Gubbio, where the main sacrifices were to be executed on the top of the two local
     arces at the moment of shearing the sheep, probably in order to support the summer transhumance
     (Sisani 2009, p. 109). The climb to the top seems to have been part of the ritual. Such ‘archaic’
     traditions, still much alive during the Iron Age, must, however, have gradually declined in the period
     leading up to the Roman conquest.


        4.2.2 Monte Pitino

         Immediately west of the traditional ethnic contact zone between Umbrians and northern Picene
     communities (Vermeulen 2016, p. 36), and situated just east of the entrance to the intra-montane
     basin, we find Monte Pitino (site 217), east of San Severino Marche and north of the river. With its
     impressive location, visually controlling the Potenza valley, from the Apennines to the Adriatic, this
     600 m-high hilltop site was between the 7th and the 3rd centuries BC probably the potent central
     place of a vast territory.
         The survey conducted here in 2007 by PVS-team member Martin Sterry, was restricted to
     preliminary observations and did not include extensive artefact collections, nor any gridded
     approaches. More intensive fieldwork is certainly needed here and regular remote sensing operations,
     with traditional aerial photography and recently also with the use of a drone, can at present only
     provide a very provisional picture of the archaeological situation (Vermeulen et al. 2009, pp.
     106-108). The vegetation cover of much of the hilltop, mostly ranging from dense brambles and
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                                       71




woodland to thick grasses, hinders artefact collection activities, although some results, mostly on
the few ploughed fields near the top could already be obtained. These prospections have established
that during most of the Iron Age there was extensive settlement activity over the terraces and plateau
that surrounds the summit of Monte Pitino, and a first model of the probable ancient terraces can be
proposed now. Medieval material, connected with the 13th century castle built on the very top, and
its associated settlement, is currently overlying the earlier phases, so it is difficult to estimate the
density and full extent of pre-Roman habitation. Nevertheless, it seems likely that already in the Iron
Age the settlement encompassed two distinct areas of enclosed settlement: the highest western part
of the hilltop now taken in by the medieval structure and the large, slightly eastwards sloping plateau
located east of the top. Contrary to the enclosure banks of Monte Primo, the original defences of
Monte Pitino cannot be observed and mapped with the same ease, even if a future LiDAR survey
would be most helpful here.
     The abundant presence of Iron Age roof tiles within the assemblages observed through this
archaeological survey, together with the indications for the important enclosure system, suggest a
permanent nucleated settlement rather than a hillfort periodically visited for security (Vermeulen,
Mlekuz 2012). The surveys show that the site has an artefact cover over an area of almost 10 hectares,
and older excavations on the summit have also produced evidence of structures and ceramics from
the seventh to the 4th centuries BC, but these excavations are poorly recorded (Lollini 1958). There
also appears to be a high quantity of dolium sherds across the protohistoric areas within the terrace
walls that must indicate a high degree of storage activity and perhaps some control of agricultural
activity, but further data is needed to support this hypothesis. The discrete concentrations discovered
in fields on the slopes of the hill (Fig. 43, MPS008 and MPS012) as well as sites known from surveys
in the area during the 1980’s (Moscatelli 1988, p. 38) also show that the central settlement on top of
Monte Pitino was not isolated, but the focal point of a range of different protohistoric sites clustered
around the hilltop. Some of these are cemeteries, and were partly excavated in the 19th and early 20th




                                                                                                           Fig. 42 The hill-top
                                                                                                           site of Monte Pitino
                                                                                                           and its wide view
                                                                                                           over the Potenza
                                                                                                           valley.
72                                                                                                       Frank Vermeulen et al.




      Fig. 43 Survey
     results at Monte
                Pitino.




                          centuries. They have yielded some very wealthy graves – especially the cemetery on Monte Penna
                          with its opulent orientalising and Archaic tombs – demonstrating that the settlement of Monte Pitino
                          had a pivotal role in the control and organisation of transport, through the strategic corridor of the
                          Potenza valley, of luxury goods to and from Etruria and Latium, as well as from Greece and the eastern
                          Mediterranean (Baldelli et al. 1991; Percossi et al. 2006, 21-22). It is at present not clear how and if,
                          the settlement site survived the transition into the Roman period. The presence on the smooth eastern
                          slopes, just outside the presumed widest protohistoric enclosure, of a few fragments of black gloss
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                       73




pottery and pieces of Roman tegulae, found seemingly in association with remains of what could be a
large stone building (Fig. 43, MPS007-B), whose limestone wall or foundation structures were barely
visible at surface level, is indicative of some kind of activity in the later Republican period. This
points at a much smaller occupation (military?; a lookout structure?) than the protohistoric activity,
and its spatial separation is striking. At present there does not seem any material of Augustan or later
date and interestingly; no Republican material was recovered from within the system of terraces, and
it seems unlikely that this is due to preservation as the protohistoric material is so well visible. This
apparent abandonment strongly suggests a link with the establishment of the nearby road settlements
that later became the towns of Trea and Septempeda, and therefore points to a dramatic change in the
settlement system of the middle Potenza area (see below).


   4.2.3 Monte Franco and Passo di Treia

    The next strategic area dominated by a central site is the sector of Passo di Treia, some 7 km east
of Monte Pitino, where the Potenza corridor narrows and control of transported goods, as well as
river crossing, were facilitated. Here, on the opposite side of the river, was located the Bronze and
Iron Age hilltop settlement of Monte Franco (site 012), mostly known in literature for its rich Picene
cemetery of Moie di Pollenza (9th to 5th century BC), located on the north-western slope of the hill
(Lollini 1979; Percossi Serenelli 2003; Percossi 2005).
    As a result of the dense present-day vegetation of trees and high grasses on the hilltop and very
steep hill slopes, where possibly the earliest (MBA?) occupation was located, artefact surveys and
other topographic observations are much hindered. With its limited space available for construction
on the narrow plateau-top – measuring less than three hectares – the hilly part of the site could
only have been one element of the centralising protohistoric settlement. Indeed our systematic
artefact surveys of the ploughed terrain surrounding the hilltop, demonstrated that at the latest in
the Iron Age the main settlement area extended further east and north of the hill, where a kind of
aggregated ‘village’ had developed. Several low-density impasto clusters (sites 011 and 012), some
of which containing decorated MBA fine ware, seem part of a multi-period occupation, near the
general area where also the Moie di Pollenza graves were located. Probably through time some
shifts of settlement grounds occurred here, but the main and more condensed occupation of the
Iron Age Picenes can safely be located on the gradual slopes west of the hill (Boullart 2003, 176;
Percossi et al. 2006, 112-114; Vermeulen, Mlekuz 2012). The density and quality of the collected
materials here suggest a quite rich settlement zone of some 5 hectares, with some dispersed smaller
habitation cores around it. Several concentrations of Iron Age impasto tiles, dolia and remains of
hearths with burned clay and animal bones suggest residential activity, while associated pottery
(e.g. ‘impasto buccheroide’, fragments of bronze objects and Greek ceramic imports and imitation
ware) point at a well-connected place. Diagnostic finds primarily belong to the period between the
late 7th and 5th centuries BC, but also some later Iron Age material (4th/3rd century BC) occurs.
Interestingly, the cluster of Iron Age settlement cores continues on the direct opposite side of the
river, where not only the aggregated habitation extended (site 014), but where at the very border
of the stream our aerial survey detected three circular crop marks (diameter c. 20 m), probably
representing buried ditches of interconnected round barrows with elite Picene burials. Such round
barrows have recently been discovered by aerial sensing in several other valleys of Northern
Marche (Boschi, Silani forthcoming) and together with earlier observations at Passo di Treia of
other funerary Picene presence north of the river (Piangatelli 1970) they confirm the complete
picture of the centralising settlement on and near the hilltop, flanking a strategic river crossing, and
surrounded by several funerary areas close to the river. It is not surprising that the potential of this
location and the long term success of clustered human settlement here, continued somehow in the
74                    Frank Vermeulen et al.

          the east.

                       subsequent Roman period, when some form of centralised settlement remained the norm in this
                       narrow part of the valley corridor. While the artefact surveys proved the presence, at least from
                       the 1st century BC to Late Antiquity, of an important Roman rural settlement or villa at the eastern
                       foot of Monte Franco (site 077), these operations and excellent oblique photography allowed to
                       locate a large roadside settlement on the northern side of the river, replacing or continuing Iron
                       Age clustering in that area (sites 079 and 094). Here the existing settlement gradually acquired
                       more importance due to its position on the route through the valley, which the Romans must have
                       started to develop soon after the creation in the late 3rd century BC of the Via Flaminia, of which
                       this valley road is a diverticulum to Ancona. The oblique aerial imagery from several flights over
                       the area procured us with the clear linear trace of this Roman valley road, observing it over some
                       800 m, interrupted only by modern building structures in this area. Four distinct surface scatters
                       of Roman settlement material, with mostly 1st-2nd century AD finds, were located along this road
                       stretch. Furthermore, it is likely that the modern infrastructures hide (or destroyed) several other
                       nuclei from the settlement area or vicus that in early Imperial times must have measured at least 8
                       hectares in size.

    Because of now aborted plans to expand the modern commercial area, an invasive survey
with systematic trial trenches covering 10% of the terrain, followed by a focused excavation, was
achieved here in 2007 at the bequest of the Soprintendenza per i beni archeologici delle Marche.15
This fortunate ‘ground truthing’ of our aerial and field prospection data, confirmed exactly the
location and presence of the presumed settlement cores in the four areas indicated by the surveys,
and allowed the discovery of remains of four Roman houses, dated roughly between the 1st and
early 3rd centuries AD. Of the simple rectangular houses remained only a series of mostly spoliated
wall structures, made of roughly cut sandstone and large river pebbles, and a few fragments of brick
floors. In the western part of the vicus (site 94) the houses were located near the possible junction
of the mentioned NE-SW oriented gravel road, running parallel with the river, and maybe a second
road or street with a NW-SE orientation. The latter, if confirmed by further fieldwork, might have
been of only local significance or was possibly the road link to the Roman town of Trea lying some
4 km NW. Interestingly, the trial trenches in a plot near the presumed road junction also revealed
15
   We thank Dott. Tommaso Casci Ceccacci, responsible for this area at the Soprintendenza, for providing us information
about these unpublished excavations.
78                                                                                                     Frank Vermeulen et al.




                       a series of simple Roman burials. Although some of these graves were covered with tiles and were
                       partly destroyed by ploughing, the function as local cemetery had not been identified by our artefact
                       surveys. The interpretation of surface finds as ‘funeral’ is always a delicate matter in landscape
                       survey where only scattered artefact concentrations are observed.


                          4.2.4 Montarice

                           Finally, reaching the coast, we can report on the excellent preliminary results obtained by our
                       intensive integrated survey on the site of Montarice (site 024), where the presence of a large multi-
                       period central settlement can be proposed. The site lies about one kilometre north of the presumed Iron
                       Age river mouth, whose stream-bed was recently located via paleo-landscape studies near the centre
                       of the valley floor (Goethals et al. 2009). The site is located on a subcoastal hill, directly north of
                       the river plain, and overlooking both the coastline and the valley. The rather flat and elliptical hilltop
                       plateau of Montarice, sloping only slightly towards the sea, is reasonably well preserved despite
                       agricultural practice. Protohistoric people made good use of this geomorphologic unit, bordered on
                       three sides (N,S,W) by rather steep defendable slopes. The actual plateau measures some 5 hectares,

  Fig. 47 Enhanced

but at least 7 hectares are available for easy permanent settlement if the more gradual upper parts of
the slopes are included. The presence of pre-and protohistoric activity on the site was known from
sporadic earlier finds (Boullart 2003), but our total survey approach since 2000, including aerial
photography, gridded artefact survey, geomatic measurements, geophysical prospections and some
augering operations, have now revealed many unknown and important aspects of the character and
evolution of human occupation in this strategically crucial location of the Potenza valley.
    Most important for the structural characterisation of settlement activity on Montarice are the
aerial photographs acquired between 2000 and 2014 during the regular series of observer-directed
low-altitude flights above the whole Potenza valley (Verhoeven 2009; Vermeulen 2011). Especially
successful flights in 2001 and 2003 that revealed a multitude of cereal crop marks incited for
further monitoring of the site from the sky, taking aerial pictures yearly during different seasons,
thereby documenting various crop and soil regimes and experimenting with special techniques
such as NIR imagery. Recently the most revealing image sets were processed into conventional
orthophotographs, and experiments were done to derive new data products that can be used as
proxies for other types of archaeological prospection (Verhoeven,Vermeulen 2016). In particular,
the extraction of the spectral (i.e., plant colour) and geometrical (i.e., plant height) components of
the Montarice crop canopy from these aerial photographs, allowed to create a canopy height model
80                                                                                                Frank Vermeulen et al.




 Fig. 49 Montarice:
  orthophoto, based
   on oblique aerial

    with soil marks.
       The grey hues
  represent a strong
      archaeological
             impact.




                       using image-based modelling, and provided new archaeological information that could be extracted
                       from this vegetation model by applying relief-enhancing visualisation techniques. Through
                       interpretation of the resulting data, a combination of the co-registered spectral and geometrical
                       vegetation dimensions clearly added new depth to the quite complicated interpretative mapping
                       of the archaeological structures of the site. Soon after the first successful aerial photography
                       operations also a grid-based artefact survey of Montarice was organised. This occurred at the right
                       moment when ploughing had brought up large quantities of artefacts over the whole plateau part
                       of the site, resulting in the collection and fine recording of some 9300 artefacts, of which almost
                       20% were diagnostic. A further phase of intensive prospection on the site happened in 2012-2013
                       when different geophysical operations were achieved, namely an earth resistance survey profile
                       and limited GPR scanning in a sector on the northern edge of the plateau where some of the
                       detected features of possible enclosures were checked. Finally, a total coverage magnetometry
                       survey using fluxgate gradiometers was achieved over the whole plateau area. All these field
                       operations were further enhanced by the application of some limited hand augerings and DGPS
                       geomatic measurements, in order to complement data with information about the basic stratigraphy
                       and microtopography of the site, and also to allow interpretative mapping of all features obtained
                       via the remote sensing operations.
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                                   81




    Even if some Stone Age material (e.g. a Middle Palaeolithic artefact) was found here, permanent
habitation must have started on Montarice only from the Middle Bronze Age (MBA, c. 1700-1350
BC) onwards, and it is attested at least until Late Republican times, essentially the 2nd century BC.
There is also evidence for more reduced Imperial Roman and some high medieval activity on the
site, represented by more than 17 % of all diagnostic potsherds, but the spatial extend and possibly
duration of these later occupations is less pronounced. The many datable impasto fragments found
in the grids indicate a gradual expansion of occupied space during the Bronze and Iron Age, with a
strong focus on the higher westernmost part of the plateau in the MBA and moving towards the east
and a more total plateau occupation in later phases. This protohistoric occupation was however not
always of the same intensity and it is therefore interesting to confront the finer spatial movements
of concentration on the plateau with the possibly corresponding structural traces observed via
remote sensing.
    The first signs for stable occupation date to the early phases of the Middle Bronze Age (c. 1700-
1500 BC), and datable pottery fragments from MBA1-2 are found all over the plateau, albeit in
small densities. Soon the NW corner of the plateau becomes the main focus of this Bronze Age
settlement, and most material datable to the advanced phase of the MBA (MBA3; c. 1500-1350
BC) and of the subsequent Recent Bronze Age (c. 1350-1100 BC) was collected in this part of the
highest elevation of the hilly site. The nearby extreme western part of the plateau also suffered




                                                                                                        Fig. 50 Montarice:
                                                                                                        overlay of the
                                                                                                        Middle Bronze
                                                                                                        Age density grid
                                                                                                        from the artefact
                                                                                                        survey over the
                                                                                                        image produced
                                                                                                        by magnetometer
                                                                                                        survey (see also
                                                                                                        fig. 33).
82                                                                                                       Frank Vermeulen et al.




                          more from post-depositional erosion activity, which explains the void of surface finds in some areas
                          here. All these Bronze Age surface finds suggest a firm embedding in subsequent central-Adriatic
                          material culture groups, supplemented with occasional traces of contacts with areas further away,
                          even if so far no Bronze Age Aegean imports or Italo-Mycenaean wares have been identified in the
                          ceramic assemblage. The transition from Recent to Final Bronze Age (c. 1100-950 BC) marks a
                          significant low in the number of pottery fragments, but the site flourishes again in the subsequent
                          Iron Age, and in particular from the later 7th century BC onwards. In marked contrast to the EIA and
                          first phase of the MIA stands the high number of datable Late Iron Age fragments, which suggests
                          a new development in the settlement history of Montarice. Most diagnostic pottery belongs to
                          the buccheroide impasto wares, influenced by productions from Etruria, even if Etruscan imports
                          seem to lack. From the 6th century BC onwards many Greek imports and wares imported from
                          more northern and southern Adriatic coastal areas help to date the clear expansion of settlement
                          activity, now occurring over the full extent of the hill, including evidence of activity on its northern
                          and especially its southern slopes towards the river plain. Also the central and eastern parts of the
                          plateau are now fully settled, with several hotspots possibly indicating specific habitation units and
                          high activity concentrations most of all in the central segment of the site. The latter observation
                          must be taken with caution as the ongoing erosion of the plateau border may well explain some
                          high densities of archaeological material here, and it is likely that certain concentrations of artefact

scatters represent more actively eroding archaeological deposits. Interestingly, the settlement does
not seem to terminate abruptly with the Roman conquest of the area, in the course of the 3rd century
BC. Republican material shows still quite high densities, with most pottery collected in the central
and eastern part of the plateau, and on the southern slopes. At least until the late 2nd century BC,
several generations after the founding of the Roman colony Potentia (184 BC), only 2 km south
of Montarice, this occupation was very lively and probably still of the centralised or multi-nuclear
type. Diagnostic pottery for this later occupation are fragments of Greco-Italic amphorae, some
local plain wares, black gloss, cooking ware from the Vesuvius region, and Rhodian amphorae. Late
Republican, and Early Imperial to late Roman occupation (1st century BC-5th century AD) becomes
much more sparse and seems essentially restricted to the extreme eastern segment of the plateau,
where perhaps a single farmstead now occupied the site during parts of this period.
    The still preliminary and, because of a lack of excavations, quite difficult interpretative mapping
based on the aerial imagery and the geophysical prospections, nevertheless allows us to propose
that the hilly plateau settlement of Montarice was, probably during most of its main phases of
occupation, surrounded by imposing defences. The positive crop marks, most of which were
confirmed as subsoil anomalies picked up by the geophysics, indicate that these could be evidence
for combinations of earthen banks (with palisades) and ditches, or during certain phases, possibly
even (partly) stone walls and ditches. It is possible to distinguish several diachronic phases in the
84                                                                                   Frank Vermeulen et al.




     enclosure systems, which also have differing widths between c. 6 m and 16 m. What is probably
     the oldest system seems to enclose only the highest western part of the plateau, an area of some 1.3
     hectare. As we have seen, this is also the area with the highest concentration of Bronze Age surface
     materials. A second system encloses a larger and more longitudinal oval, more or less coinciding
     with the edges of the whole plateau. It seems to enclose an area of at least 4 hectares, even if the short
     eastern and western sides are difficult to spot as a result of modern interventions on the topography
     of the site. This enclosure coincides best with the general geomorphology of the terrain, and its
     long northern and southern sides mark the break between flat terrain and the current steep slopes
     of the hill. Crop marks suggest that it was possibly interrupted by a monumental entrance near the
     centre of its southern side. Even if it cannot be excluded that both enclosures are contemporary,
     which would then perhaps suggest a well-defined social structure within the general layout of the
     settlement, it seems likely that they rather show a chronological evolution in the settlement towards
     enlarging the inhabited space. It would be logic that the second and larger system was active when
     the site was at its full expansion, which according to the artefact record was mostly between the 6th
     and the 2nd centuries BC. A series of very wide enclosure traces – suggesting at least three different
     chronological sub-phases of construction and use – seem to cross the eastern half of the plateau and
     to cut through the second enclosure system along the northern hillside. This, therefore definitively
     younger, third main system possibly excludes the eastern extremity of the plateau, but seems to
     include more terrain on the more gradual upper part of the northern hillslope, where even more
     linear traces occur. It is not clear whether these should be interpreted as parts of enclosures or as an
     ancient (?) system of terracing, such as we also spot in the crop marks on the southern slopes. This
     reorganisation of the defences might still belong to the long period of major occupation between
     the Iron Age and Roman Republican times, when the enclosed or defended area did not necessarily
     shrink, as now more occupation was made possible on the slopes of the hill.
         Inside the multi-phased enclosed settlement areas remote sensing operations revealed many
     crop marks of regular anthropogenic anomalies. Many suggest the presence of large and small
     rectangular houses and huts, some seemingly with sunken floors, others with wall alignments made
     out of posts. If these preliminary identifications are correct, at least some 40 to 50 building structures
     of different sizes and shapes can be individualised, fully or partially, but the data are difficult to
     read. This number might even have been higher if our view on these archaeological traces was not
     hindered by the large erosion disturbance near the western edge of the plateau, and by several large
     homogenous zones in the crop marks masking possible building structures in the central-eastern
     part of the plateau. Data from the aerial imagery and from the magnetic surveys are also not always
     complementary, which is often useful, but can also hinder convincing interpretations. Also the lack
     of comparative information on Picene housing from other settlement sites – a specific problem of
     archaeological research in the central Adriatic region (Vermeulen 2016, pp. 58-60) – is not very
     helpful for this interpretative work. Even if the interpretation and mapping of individual structures
     might still change when new survey evidence, or preferably some stratigraphic controls become
     available, it is already clear that the multitude of houses and other buildings with many surrounding
     pits, and their quite regular spacing and orientations, suggest that in certain periods of the life of
     the site a ‘village’ type of settlement with defences and dense habitation existed here. Excavations
     are absolutely needed, however, to interpret further the complex topo-chronological phasing of this
     enduring site, which was ultimately to be overshadowed by the Roman colony of Potentia in the
     nearby valley floor.
         Further archaeological investigation should also include two meaningful discoveries by survey
     near the edges of the plateau: a possible ‘extra-mural’ settlement core immediately west of the site,
     and a presumed monumental tomb on the southern slope of the hill. The former (site 216) needs
     further confirmation in the field, but recent observation on a high resolution satellite image of 2013,
     in a lower lying sector at some 100 m west of the hillsite, allowed to distinguish a whole series of
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                                       85




crop marks similar to those suggesting larger building structures on Montarice. The area, which
includes indications for at least two large rectangular buildings and many pits has also delivered an
artefact scatter nearby, with late Iron Age impasto as well as some black gloss ware and 2nd century
BC amphora sherds. This find suggests that the wider perimeter of the hill was subject to peripheral
settlement cores during late Iron Age and the Republican period.
    The second discovery is even more exciting. Already during the gridded artefact surveys of
the hill a very dense concentration of mostly Iron Age pottery fragments, several Adriatic (e.g. red
painted wares) and Greek imports (e.g. black glazed pottery), and some special finds (e.g. pieces of
worked sandstone, fragment of a Bronze Age handle) had been found in a very restricted area of the
central part of the southern hillslope. This had been preliminary interpreted as a possible ‘extra-mural’
habitation core developed on the terraces towards the river plain (Percossi et al. 2006, p. 124). New
aerial photography analysis, using the technology mentioned above (Verhoeven,Vermeulen 2016)
allows now for a re-interpretation of this important site as the place of a large round burial monument
with a diameter of some 25 m, whose ditch or stone recuperation trench can be well observed in the
crop marks on our aerial photography. The very visible and dominant position of such a funerary
monument, the date of late 6th/early 5th century BC suggested by the most diagnostic associated
surface finds, and its location near the presumed main southern entrance to the site, all point at a very
significant marker in the Iron Age landscape of Montarice and the coastal plain.


  4.3 Roman towns and their medieval extinction

  The four Roman town sites that were studied with a battery of non-invasive survey techniques
are quite evenly spread over the middle and lower Potenza valley. We will discuss them here from
inland to coast, but the bulk of information about their topography and general evolution assembled
by the PVS project through the years (since 2002) is so impressive that, for reasons of space, only
a short synthesis and update of the most important acquisitions will be presented here. Recently,




                                                                                                            Fig. 53 Septempeda:
                                                                                                            location on the
                                                                                                            modern topographic
                                                                                                            map of the
                                                                                                            Roman city wall
                                                                                                            with indication
                                                                                                            of the areas of
                                                                                                            our geophysical
                                                                                                            prospections.
86                                                                                                      Frank Vermeulen et al.




                          one of us has been able to provide a detailed overview of their urban evolution, taking also into
                          consideration all legacy data, and placing them in the wider context of colonisation and urbanisation
                          of the central Adriatic region (Vermeulen 2016). The more intensively surveyed town sites of
                          Potentia (Vermeulen 2012; Vermeulen, Monsieur 2012), Ricina (Vermeulen, Carboni 2012) and
                          Trea (Vermeulen et al. 2012) have also been the subject of recently published monographic papers.
                          Apart from a presentation of the general topographic acquisitions of prospections on the four city
                          sites, we will emphasise here mostly the artefact surveys done on the three town sites studied by
                          way of gridded fieldwalking, as this also provides context for the material studies dealt with later in
                          the book. More complete bibliographic referencing for earlier work on the four sites can be found in
                          the site catalogue (chapter 7). Finally, it must be stressed that some of the sites concerned here knew
                          probably some pre-Roman activity, even if questions about the precise character of this, and about
                          the degree of continuity between the later Iron Age and the Roman period, can only be resolved by
                          stratigraphic excavations reaching their deepest levels. This specific problem of the methodology
                          of surface survey is partly also applicable to the last phases of these towns, which all suffered
                          total abandonment in Late Antiquity and/or the Early Middle Ages. The ephemeral nature of the
                          archaeologically still understudied material culture of the transition period between the late 6th
                          century, with its Lombard invasion of Italy, and the 10th century, when a gradual new centralisation
                          of settlement commenced in this region, prevents at present a full understanding of the end of these
                          settlements as regional centres (Vermeulen, Carboni 2012; Vermeulen 2012).


                             4.3.1 Septempeda

                             The site of Septempeda (site 139), near San Severino Marche, can be defined as the place of an
                          Imperial Roman urban centre, possibly preceded by a late Iron Age settlement or sanctuary and a
                          Roman Republican village. Before being abandoned, and replaced by San Severino, this Roman
                          town probably ended as an early medieval village or hamlet. The site is located for the most part on

Fig. 54 Septempeda:
      location on the
       LiDAR image
 from the Ministero
   dell’Ambiente of
       the interpreted
          traces of the
   Roman city (city
     wall with gates,
       streets, forum,
   public buildings,
    houses…) based
 on our geophysical
   prospections and

    data from earlier
   excavations (e.g.

     gates) and field
        observations.
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                        87




a rather flat river terrace directly north of the river Potenza, east of the modern agglomeration where
the later medieval site was born, but it also encloses a small hill stretching immediately north of this
terrace. Today arable land prevails here, and the small rural church (pieve) and a few houses near
the valley road are the only constructions bothering archaeological visibility. Fieldwork by several
actors during the 20th century led to the excavation of an Imperial Roman bath complex, of parts
of at least two Early Imperial domus, the remains of a street, and several elements of the town wall,
including two monumental gates in the SW and E sides of the wall circuit (Landolfi 2003). Extra-
mural remains of Imperial times include a necropolis (1st/2nd centuries AD) and parts of a pottery
workshop outside the presumed western gate. Our systematic topographical survey approach started
here in 2004 and the main field operations so far include monitoring with oblique and low altitude
aerial photography, geophysical prospections (magnetic, GPR and earth resistance) of large central
and western parts of the site, a small geomorphological augering operation, and very limited artefact
survey on a field west of the town limits where aerial photography discovered a possible sanctuary.
    The origin of permanent and centralising occupation of this site near the river Potenza was possibly
the development of a healing water sanctuary near the spring-line at the foot of the mentioned small
promontory overlooking the valley floor (Vermeulen 2016). While there is no trace yet of its expected
early structures from the times only Picene populations must have used it (e.g. simple unroofed
platforms, votive pits, …), we suggest that the monumentalisation of this sanctuary can be recognised
in the large and rectangular platform type of structure, of some 29 m x 17 m, found between 2005
and 2010 through our aerial photography and subsequent geophysical surveys, on the eastern edge
of the Roman town area. This possible cult-structure, which was maybe at the origin of the urban
development here, was at an undefined moment in time surrounded by a monumental portico, creating
an enormous rectangular space of c. max 128 m x 88 m. Even if this portico was only added later, all
present data indicate that the possible sanctuary was bordered or even cut across by the presumably
late 3rd to 2nd century BC Roman valley road from Rome to Ancona, and that it certainly predates
the building of the Roman city wall in the late Republic. The latter would have excluded the sacred
precinct from the newly created enclosed town area, leaving it outside the walls. The very limited
chronological data for this huge complex come from a hand augering on the so-called platform
and from a simple intra-site surface collection of artefacts. The former provided a most interesting
radiocarbon date of 2150±28BP, taken at the almost 3 m deep base of the platform structure. This
date, probably somewhere in the 3rd century BC, places the beginning of this presumed sanctuary
roughly around the Roman conquest of the area. The intra-site fieldwalking, however, provided no
distinctly pre-Imperial surface finds, but this is possibly due to thick colluvium coverage of the terrain
here. Interestingly the areas of the portico delivered good amounts of painted wall plaster, confirming
the (possibly somewhat later) Roman date and monumental quality of the architecture.
    Even if, without excavation, further chronological or even functional interpretations of this
structure remain at present unsolved, in view of its shape and the location on the route to highland
pastures in the Apennines, it recalls the sacred precincts of Heracles/Hercules on pastoralist routes,
and certain Italic sanctuaries monumentalised in later Roman times under Hellenistic cultural
influence, such as the sanctuary at Monterinaldo at the edge of the territory of Firmum Picenum
(Landolfi et al. 2009). The Hellenistic or Republican development of the sanctuary could well
be connected with some of the oldest finds from excavations in the western part of Septempeda,
namely a dozen of 2nd century BC (?) graves of that period, containing quite rich goods of imported
character, including black gloss pottery, unguentaria, lagynoi and iron strigiles (Landolfi 2003).
The still very limited data suggest that the core of this settlement was possibly a Hellenistic-style
sanctuary in the valley bottom, and perhaps with another religious pole on an arx dominating the
roadside settlement from the nearby hillock, even if no stratigraphic data can confirm this so far
(Vermeulen 2016). This agglomeration, still called oppidum in the Liber Coloniarum (Blume et
al. 1848, p. 253), became a municipium around the mid-1st century BC. This rise in status was
88                                                                                Frank Vermeulen et al.




     preceded or followed by the erection of a monumental sandstone town wall in opus quadratum
     incorporating the habitation nucleus near the river and the possible arx into an enclosed area of max
     15 hectares. It is not yet clear when exactly this wall construction can be dated, but suggestions for
     a date connected with the mid-1st century rise in rank of the town, or with the events shortly before
     or after the Social War, are the most likely options (Perna 2012).
         The results of our surveys have already procured much data about the urbanism of the town,
     which according to all evidence seems to have flourished mostly from the last decades of the
     Republic to the 2nd century AD. The remote sensing information allows to map most clearly a series
     of streets that developed parallel to the main central artery and linked to four or five monumental
     entrance gates. This not very regular street grid might have grown gradually, in association with the
     demographic bloom of the settlement whose living quarters only slowly filled the intra-mural space.
     In order to convey the idea of a Roman town also a forum was developed in the very centre of the
     settlement, along the main so-called decumanus maximus and immediately east from an area where
     excavations have revealed a large bathhouse of Imperial times (Landolfi 2003). Our prospection
     data, which now clearly indicate the location of the forum, show that the rectangular plaza of min 59
     m x 33 m, positioned with its longitudinal axis perpendicular to the main E-W street, was surrounded
     by public buildings and monuments. Even if the interpretation of the sub-surface traces remains
     problematic, we can distinguish several units. On the long east side a probable rich domus with
     several courtyards or peristilia is oriented towards the so-called decumanus maximus. It is flanked
     on its western side by five similar rectangular buildings opening up towards the forum plaza. This
     looks very much like a row of side by side rectangular one-room tabernae with a common and
     continuous back wall and comb-like plan. North of these, but still on the long east side of the forum,
     there is a large terraced structure bearing two identical rectangular buildings. An identification of
     this as a sanctuary with two parallel temples is tempting. On the short side north of the forum our
     latest analysis of the aerial data identify a large building structure facing the plaza with its long
     southern side, most probably a monumental basilica as is also suggested by epigraphic evidence for
     such a building erected in Septempeda before 12 BC (CIL IX, 5576). Also the long western side of
     the plaza is bordered by a few rectangular buildings, whose function remains, however, unknown.
     Beyond the short south side of the forum, on the other side of the main street, we have currently
     no data, but it is in this sector that ill documented old excavations located some domus. Although
     chronological information for this dynamic transformation of the town centre is still poor, proof
     of an important cult place at Septempeda with typical architectural terracotta’s, the discovery of
     an inscription referring to the Feronia cult (CIL XI, 5711) and the construction of a major public
     building, all indicate the last decades of the 1st century BC and the Augustan period as a phase of
     spectacular bloom (Landolfi 2003). Data about the late Roman and early medieval demise of this
     centre are still very poor.


        4.3.2 Trea

         Also the Imperial Roman town of Trea (site 137), located only 11 km northeast of Septempeda,
     was probably preceded by dispersed Iron age settlement and a Roman Republican village, while
     here again the town must have been abandoned only gradually via the intermediate stage of an
     early medieval village. The site of Roman Trea is located c. 1 km northwest of its modern successor
     Treia, both north of the river Potenza. It coincides with the eastern extreme of an elongated plateau
     connected with the Apennine mountain range, and is delimited by steep northern and southern
     slopes. Here too an agrarian landscape still dominates today, archaeologically only disturbed by a
     few dispersed houses and the convent of SS Crocifisso. The only remaining visible ruins are two
     small western sections of the former city walls, partly incorporated in a now abandoned farm house.
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                                     89




                                                                                                        Fig. 55 Trea:
                                                                                                        enhanced
                                                                                                        orthophoto, based

                                                                                                        photography, of the
                                                                                                        full town area with
                                                                                                        many crop marks
                                                                                                        of houses, streets,
                                                                                                        forum, city wall, ...

The first and only major excavations, by Fortunato Benigni in the late 18th century, determined the
exact location of the town and revealed parts of its city wall and a few buildings. In the 1970’s and
the 1980’s the University of Macerata intensified research in this area, with artefact surveys and
topographic studies, and with punctual excavations in the convent area (Moscatelli 1988; Fabrini
1990). A more encompassing topographical approach with mapping of the almost complete town




                                                                                                        Fig. 56 Trea:
                                                                                                        location on the
                                                                                                        modern topographic
                                                                                                        map of the
                                                                                                        Roman city wall
                                                                                                        with indication
                                                                                                        of the areas of
                                                                                                        our geophysical
                                                                                                        prospections.
90                                                                                                    Frank Vermeulen et al.




                        plan was started by the PVS team in 2003 and the main field oriented operations during the past
                        decade were: detection and continuous monitoring with oblique and low altitude aerial photography
                        (airplane, helikite, drone, …), geophysical prospections (including almost full coverage magnetic
                        surveys, GPR and earth resistance), geomorphologic augering operations, and gridded artefact
                        surveys on the central and southern parts of the site (Vermeulen et al. 2012, 2016).
                            The wider artefact surveys in this area have demonstrated the existence of a series of small
                        Picene settlements unevenly scattered over an elongated plateau covering at least 30 hectares,
                        roughly 4 km northeast of Monte Pitino. Only in the eastern part of this plateau, were Republican
                        Roman finds clustered, the settlement developed into a more aggregative centre, congregated around
                        the well observed east-west road running from the upper Potenza Valley in the direction of Auximum
                        (Osimo) and ultimately Ancona. Depending on the date accepted for the colonisation of Auximum,
                        this development of Trea might even be placed more accurately somewhere in the 2nd century
                        BC, as the needs for land transport from Rome, and certain areas of inland Latium and Umbria to
                        its new colony might have necessitated a good halting place and perhaps the seat of an organising
                        praefectura (Vermeulen 2016). Interestingly our campaign of hand augering on the central part of
                        the site procured us with two radiocarbon dates taken from the lowest occupation level that seem
                        to confirm this early chronology. The dates, respectively 2220±29BP and 2125±30BP, suggest a
                        beginning of permanent settlement here between the 3rd and 2nd century BC. This seems more or
                        less confirmed by some of the black gloss pottery and other diagnostic sherds picked up during our
                        artefact survey.
