                            From the combined remote sensing evidence we can now propose that the initial development of
                        this roadside settlement also involved the laying out of plots and houses perpendicular to this ancient
                        road axis. Especially the latest interpretation of several revealing aerial photographs taken between
                        2003 and 2009 allow now to propose that the initial village-type settlement was organised around
                        a road junction, where the plateau road arriving from the west (Rome) splits into two branches:
                        one heading northeast to Ancona and another progressing to the southeast in order to reach the
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                                      91




                                                                                                           Fig. 58 Trea:
                                                                                                           detail of the
                                                                                                           image produced
                                                                                                           by magnetometer
                                                                                                           survey of the forum
                                                                                                           area of the town.
                                                                                                           See also fig. 22.


coastal colony of Potentia. Later, in the course of the second half of the 1st century BC, probably
in the decades after the elevation to the status of municipium in 49 BC, the whole settlement was
restructured and urbanised with the building of a circuit wall and an impressive forum. The oval
plateau area was then enclosed by a town wall in opus quasi-reticulatum, built with local limestone
and according to the new survey data interrupted by a series of rectangular towers, and probably by
four gates providing a direct exit towards the nearby towns of Septempeda, Auximum, Ricina and
Urbs Salvia. The impressive western gate, directed also towards the capital, was like the SW gate
in Septempeda of the monumental semi-circular type, giving extra greatness to this otherwise small
town. It can be noticed that the now well identified intramural area was smaller than previously
thought, probably only c. 11 hectares. The existing Rome-Ancona road remained an important E-W
axis of town circulation, acting as its main so-called decumanus, but as the new municipium needed
a representative administrative centre, a very monumental forum complex was built at its heart.
The survey data suggest that this forum interrupted the original central road that was now partly
moved around the plaza. The whole urbanising operation and restructuration created also a semi-
regular grid of streets and insulae oriented according to the two new axes of the forum. The smaller
western part of the town kept its original orientation with a main E-W street and a few parallel and
perpendicular streets, mostly demarcating plots for houses and probably some spaces for production
(NW sector). In the SW sector a re-interpretation of historical vertical aerial photographs might
suggest also the emplacement of an intra-mural amphitheatre, but this needs further confirmation.
The new, larger and more orderly eastern sector of the town was from now onwards, however, fully
aligned with the newly introduced civic centre, itself lying at the junction of all four incoming roads.
    This forum was a regular and monumental complex grouping mostly public buildings around a huge
longitudinal plaza of approximately 94 m x 33 m (ratio 3:1) that was partly surrounded by tabernae and
a series of porticoes, one of which seems to have been extended as a kind of colonnaded street leading
to the southern part of town. The forum plan well resembles the strong model of tripartite forums that
find their architectural prototype in the closed block forum of Caesar in Rome (Gros, Torelli 1988, p.
342). The symmetry and axiality of the identified main temple on its eastern short side and the basilica
92                    Frank Vermeulen et al.




      Fig. 59 Trea:
 enhanced oblique
 aerial view of the
            forum.
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                                      93




on the western side, show a clear strategy in the planning concept. The interpretation of the remote
sensing data, enhanced by a detailed grid-based artefact survey of the area of the forum (Vermeulen et.
al. 2009), showed that the temple was probably erected on a podium. At the surface of this location we
found large pieces of mortar – possibly of a podium nucleus built of opus caementicium – fragments
of very big roof tiles, a few white marble crustae, a fairly large amount of tesserae, and fragments of
a small marble column and of a slab with remains of an inscription. The temple was possibly flanked
on its northern side by an honorary arch giving access to the plaza immediately north of the temple.
On both the south and north long sides of the forum, porticoes border different buildings, such as
a possible curia in the NW corner, a rich domus with atrium in the SE corner and several rows of
tabernae. The monumental western plaza side is dominated by the large open colonnaded basilica (46
m x 23 m) which was already partly excavated by Benigni (Benigni 1812). The 17 varieties of marble
and other decorative stone, found by us on the surface as pieces of crustae, indicate that the building
with its three naves and a portico opening to the square, had a very rich marble clad floor.
     To the west and north of the civic centre we mapped mostly houses. House plans are still not
very precise, but the registered traces and surface artefacts (e.g. mosaic tesserae) point towards
the presence of many large and richly decorated buildings of the domus type, while also more
simple street side dwellings of the taberna type can be observed. North of the forum area a possible
macellum was identified via the traces of a large square with an internal portico. It is likely that
the tripartite forum was realised over a period of several decades between the mid-1st century BC
promotion as municipium and the reign of Augustus, when a wave of viritane colonists joined the

                         territory of Trea (Grom. Vet. 259 L.; CIL IX, 5654). The accompanying statuary, which finds its
                         reflection in several preserved fragments and some epigraphy found earlier on the site (Moscatelli
                         1988) – mostly dating to the reign of Augustus and his immediate successors – demonstrates a
                         very intensive effort in that period to emulate the tastes and political climate of Rome. Such deeds
                         continued strongly in Trea deep into the 2nd century AD when in the south-eastern part of town,
                         under the current church of SS. Crocifisso, a sanctuary for the Egyptian gods was built (Fabrini
                         1990). Our artefact surveys in the whole intra-mural part of the ancient town, as well as on the
                         southern slopes beyond the circuit wall, have generally confirmed this period of opulence, but still
                         good quantities of late Roman wares and some elements of early medieval pottery suggest that
                         the town did not shrink much in Late Antiquity. However, from that period a certain ruralisation
                         must have initiated, even if some finds suggest that Trea probably survived as a smaller settlement
                         several centuries into the early medieval period, possibly centred around the ‘pieve’ that preceded
                         the present-day SS Crocefisso.

        4.3.3 Ricina

          The Roman urban settlement of Ricina (site 140) must, like Trea and Septempeda, have grown
     out of a Late Republican road village, but here we have no direct evidence for any permanent pre-
     Roman occupation. The site is located on a flat river terrace directly north of the river Potenza, at the
     beginning of its lower valley course, less than 20 km from the coast and directly north of Macerata.
     The terrain is today taken in by some arable land, but also by the small roadside agglomeration of Villa
     Potenza, which is perhaps a remainder of scattered occupation in this area during the medieval period
     and after. Only the vast Roman remains of a large theatre, a cistern and some isolated and anonymous
     fragments of building walls are visible above ground today. Rescue excavations by the regional
     Soprintendenza and other actors during the 20th century revealed parts of a Roman street lined with
     shops, next to and parallel with its present-day successor the valley road, and fragmentary elements
     of several domus (Mercando 1971; Percossi Serenelli 1989). A more systematic topographical
     survey approach with the discovery and subsequent mapping of many new elements (houses, shops,
     amphitheatre, temple, …) was started by the PVS team in 2004 and the main field oriented operations
     during the past decade were: detection and continuous monitoring with oblique and low altitude aerial
     photography, geophysical prospections (GPR and magnetic survey) on part of the site, a few hand
     augering operations, and a grid-based artefact survey of a large northern/central part of the site where
     the geophysics and aerial prospections were most successful (Vermeulen, Carboni 2014).
          The results of this non-invasive fieldwork, together with an incorporation of legacy data and an
     observation in 2004 of the exact location of the Roman bridge over the river Potenza, allow us to
     delineate today the main traits of Roman urbanism in Ricina. The street village that developed along
     the valley road and that, according to the oldest pottery sherds found during our surface surveys must
     have been active at least since the 2nd century BC, remained the organising factor of the urban pattern.
     This so-called decumanus maximus probably became the east-west backbone of a city plan at the latest
     from the mid-1st century BC onwards, when Ricina was upgraded to the status of municipium and
     shortly later received viritane colonists during the second Triumvirate (Grom.Vet. 226 L). The town
     probably had a circuit wall, but the evidence from aerial photography for the erection and chronology
     of a regular city wall, enclosing a more or less rectangular town area of some 22 hectares, is still scanty
     and needs stratigraphic confirmation. Better attested are the only well-preserved remains above ground
     of a very large Roman theatre with a cavea of 71.80 m diameter, in the core of the urban area. Other
     major buildings dominating the ancient city centre were identified through aerial photography and
     afterwards in part confirmed by the geophysical survey. They include an amphitheatre, a large temple
     complex, a possible bathhouse and several domus and smaller buildings (e.g. tabernae). The two large
     buildings for the staging of spectacles in Ricina are especially remarkable and the presence of an
     amphitheatre for gladiatorial games was surely an important focus, providing services for the town and
     its surrounding region. The presumed size of the amphitheatre (c. 80 m x 60 m) also suggests such a
     wider attraction and servicing function. The axial integration of the amphitheatre with the Augustan
     theatre of the town suggests a quite early date for this spectacle building too, maybe connected with the
     first generations of veteran colonists that were settled here. Both buildings were respectively located
     on either side of the second main street of the city, which connected in a perpendicular way, the main
     east-west thoroughfare with a monumental bridge over the Potenza River.
          At the crossroads of both axes, now hidden mostly by the modern agglomeration, we expect
     the emplacement of the forum. North of this presumed rectangular civic centre a large rectangular
     building (min 25 m x 12 m) incorporated in a monumental precinct must have dominated the plaza.
     The structural features of this building observed in the crop marks and by geophysical prospections,
     the remains above surface of a nucleus of opus caementicium faced as opus latericium, and a large
     fragment of a marble cornice found on surface level in this location, all point to an identification
     as a large temple, possibly the main sanctuary of the city placed on a high podium. Indirectly also

Immediately east of the temple, remains of several houses were traced. Indications in the crop marks
of preserved stone floors and concentrations of tesserae collected exactly in these areas during our
grid survey point to the presence of mosaic floors, and together with good quantities of fine pottery,
marble veneer and glass, to domus residences. The surface finds indicate that these houses were most
elaborate in High Imperial times, which coincides well with the period of use of the monumental
cemetery located south-west of the town (Capodaglio, Cipolletta 1996, 26-32), namely between
the reigns of Augustus and the early 3rd century AD when under Septimius Severus the town was
promoted to colonial status and was named Colonia Helvia Recina. From that 3rd century onwards
the town probably started to decline, and even if the existence of houses with mosaic pavements has
been attested archaeologically until the 4th century AD, documentation of two late antique graves on
top of the shops along the main street suggests contraction of the habitation area during these later
phases (Percossi Serenelli 1989). Our surface finds however, seem to indicate that some form of
permanent settlement remained here at least until the 6th century, while there is evidence for a ‘pieve’
as focus for a kind of residual habitation core in the Early Middle Ages (Vermeulen, Carboni 2014).

   4.3.4 Potentia

    Finally, at the site of the Roman citizen colony of Potentia (site 132 and periphery sites 106,
116, 117) we deal not only with a distinct Late Republican and Imperial urban settlement, but also
probably with a still ill-known preceding smaller Iron Age habitation nucleus, as well as with an early
medieval village or hamlet that immediately preceded the total abandonment of this coastal place. The
archaeological site is located in the central-northern part of the valley floor, on the geomorphologically
now well mapped narrow beach ridge, directly north of the former Roman mouth of the river Potenza
(Goethals et al. 2009). This area of mostly arable and pasture land is today diagonally crossed by
the coastal road and railroad, and is only occupied locally by two farm houses. The emplacement of
the famous colony was first located by Alfieri during the late 1940s, and first examined in the field
via rescue-excavations of a northern housing sector and an extra-mural cemetery by Mercando of the
100                      Frank Vermeulen et al.




    Fig. 69 Potentia:
        oblique aerial
  photograph of the
   central part of the
     town area (seen
from the southwest)
      with vegetation
  marks in grassland
        and some soil
  marks in ploughed
     fields of Roman
        street system.




   Fig. 70 Potentia:
      location on the
modern topographic

       city wall with
    indication of the
areas of our gridded
artefact surveys and
  of the geophysical
        prospections.
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                         101




Soprintendenza in the 1960s-70s (Vermeulen 2016, p. 155). Later, main archaeological contributions
to the town topography were achieved by Moscatelli and through regular excavations near its town
centre between 1985 and 2006 by Percossi of the Soprintendenza. A more holistic topographical
approach was started by the PVS team in 2000 and the main field oriented operations during the past
15 years were: continuous monitoring with oblique and low altitude aerial photography (airplane,
helikite, drone and study of other remote sensing data (satellite, Lidar), geophysical approaches (in
2004, 2005, 2007, 2009, 2010, 2013) including full coverage magnetic surveys and partial coverage
with GPR and electric resistivity prospections, geomorphological augering operations and total
station measurements of the microtopography, excavations of the W-gate (2009-2012) and finally
systematic artefact surveys in grids over the current arable fields of the site (2002, 2003).
    The evidence for a substantial Iron Age occupation of this part of the coast is still thin. Our gridded
surface surveys revealed some impasto ware as well as a few fragments of Attic pottery, allowing
for the hypothesis of a small habitation core or landing-stage near the Potenza estuary in the general
period of intense Greek commercial contacts and imports in the area around Monte Conero. This pre-
colonial Picene settlement, possibly developed as a satellite of the nearby village of Montarice, was
stratigraphically confirmed by the excavations in the temple area of Potentia (Percossi Serenelli 2012)
delivering a 5th/4th-century BC occupation layer. This context is situated under a clay deck, about
one metre thick, on which the Roman colony was ultimately laid out during the early 2nd century
BC, suggesting that there was no direct link between this Picene occupation and the later Roman
developments. This also suggests that the area around the original mouth of the river had for a while
been the object of regular alluvial floods. On the same part of the narrow beach ridge, parallel with
the coastline, just north of the ancient river mouth, there is again some activity at the end of the 3rd
century BC. The same excavations by Percossi revealed few traces of habitation and possibly ritual
activity preceding the colonial foundation, and even if the limited extent of the deeper soundings by
the archaeologists have so far prevented building structures being found, it can be hypothesised that a
settlement – maybe a small conciliabulum of viritane colonists or a praefectura to organise the area –
developed here near the strategic contact zone of river and sea (Vermeulen 2016). When in 184 BC the
colony was eventually founded (Livy 39, 44, 10), the whole beach ridge area was reorganised to receive
the town structures we know well today.
    The excavations on the central site of a Late Republican sanctuary and its Imperial successors by
the Soprintendenza (Percossi Serenelli 2001, 2012), and the intensive surveys and excavations in
the area of the western town gate by the Ghent team, have delivered a quite detailed urban plan and
a certain insight into the general phasing of urban development (e.g. Vermeulen 2012; Vermeulen,
Monsieur 2014). The geomorphology of the site clearly dictated the choices of the Roman town
builders. The selected narrow beach was the best location for the realisation of a quite elongated
rectangular town. Its NNW-SSE orientation and putatively elongated shape are perfectly parallel to
the nearby coastline and must have originally consisted of an approximately 525 m x 300 m (16 ha)
defended space. The original defences of the initial period, when the settlement must have looked
more like a camp than a town, are still poorly known. Excavations near the western entrance have
shown that the site was probably surrounded by a V-shaped ditch, delimiting the pomerium. It can
be assumed that this ritually important town limit, which subsequently also received a drainage
function, was flanked by a small agger with palisade, but these did not survive the operations needed
for later wall constructions (Vermeulen et al. 2011; Vermeulen, Monsieur 2012).
    Thanks to central intervention, the settlement was by 174 BC transformed into a real town (Livy 41,
27, 1 and 10-3) probably receiving: a temple for Jupiter, a circuit wall with three arched gates, a regular
street network with sewers, an aqueduct, and a portico with shops to close the forum square. The circuit
wall, a fine ashlar construction of yellowish sandstone built in opus quadratum, and measuring c. 2.4 m
wide, was flanked on the city-side by a massive agger of sand and clay. The whole defensive system was
some 11 m wide and was probably flanked by a parallel interior street. There are some, still unconfirmed,

      indications of rectangular towers at the angles of the wall circuit, and possibly also at certain intervals
      along the length of the wall. Two gates were well located, one by survey only (northern side) and one by
      excavation (western side), while the southern gate could only be located indirectly by its outgoing road,
      as this area of the possible location of a small river port suffered most from post-Roman fluvial erosion.
      The excavated western gate is a construction with an internal courtyard and consecutive double gate
      system comparable to the almost contemporary NW gate at Aquileia (Tiussi 2008), and the associated
      artefacts generally date this city entrance to the first half of the 2nd century BC, and therefore around the
      period of the urbanistic intervention in the year 174 (Vermeulen et al. 2011).
          The intra-mural urban area is regularly subdivided by a network of streets designed parallel with the
      walls, and with the two formative axes of the town: the cardo maximus coinciding with the coastal road
      and the decumanus maximus coinciding with the final stretch of the valley road, leading approximately
      perpendicularly to the town and coast. The streets, with widths between 4 m and 6 m, had hard surfaces of
      mixed clay and river pebbles, and no pavement has been revealed so far, but this might be a result of the very
      thorough medieval spoliation of the ruins of abandoned Potentia. It is likely that at least both main town axes
      would have received a sturdy pavement, and sewer systems have also been recognised under them, but the
      construction of these is probably not much earlier than the Augustan period. At least 50 rectangular insulae
      were projected within this original street grid. The housing blocks are of different sizes, with the longest
      measuring 1 x 3 actus, and all have a longitudinal orientation according to the main decumanus.
          The remote sensing and geophysical operations have allowed us to also locate and study the
      forum plaza of the town, situated directly SW of the crossroads of the two main and central street
      axes (Vermeulen et al. 2006; Vermeulen, Carboni forthcoming). The plaza probably measures
      some 98 m x 29 m (or a ratio of almost 1:3) and was bordered on both long sides by rows of
      tabernae preceded by a portico. Through the years and centuries it was provided with a series of
      public buildings along its sides, some of which can be detected in geophysical imagery. Two major
      buildings were positioned on dominant platforms on the northern short side, with most western
      one displaying grid-based foundations of a probable podium: an identification as main temple –
      for Jupiter as mentioned by Livy – is a serious option. It might have been sided by a curia or
      other important building. A possible three-aisled basilica is presumed on the short southern side of
      the plaza, but this needs also further confirmation. The identification of a temple complex on the
      eastern side, however, is indisputable. According to the full excavation of this Hellenistic inspired
      sanctuary, and the associated study of its finds and architectural terracotta decorations, the complex
      was constructed here in the late 2nd century BC (Percossi Serenelli 2009; 2012). Along the same
      eastern side of the forum, the Percossi excavations have also touched upon some remains of the Late
      Republican tabernae, identified as rectangular shops of c. 5 m x 6 m, built with small sandstone
      blocks and covered by a tile roof. Considering these forum shops, together with the nearby macellum
      – now only known from its Augustan phase, but possibly preceded by an earlier market – and
      perhaps together with another (larger) market square with central fountain (?), determined through
      geophysics as taking in a full insula in the town plan at some 50 m NW of the forum – this town must
      almost from the start have had a very commercial and maritime vocation (Vermeulen 2016, p. 85).
          Although the surveys of Potentia provide some data about intra-mural housing, with structures
      well tied into the symmetry and regularity of the street grid, we need more stratigraphic evidence
      from excavations reaching their oldest phases to understand the way the city plots were being filled
      in. The best evidence comes from the southern sector, where remote sensing showed some well-
      preserved house floors and good concentrations of surface artefacts and building materials were
      noted, as well as from the NE sector where the Mercando excavations have provided partial plans
      of two housing blocks. In most parts of the town the grid survey showed a wide range of building
      materials and household artefacts which can be linked to residential functions that, according to
      their general chronology, were especially flourishing from the end of the Republic into Mid-Imperial
      times. It must be noted that the intra-mural town space of the original 2nd-century BC colony was
4. Intra-site surveys on protohistoric and Roman central places in the Potenza valley                            105




probably enlarged at the end of the Republic with one row of additional insulae, enlarging the
town’s surface area towards the coastline to almost 18 hectares (Vermeulen, Verhoeven 2006). The
town was hit by an earthquake in the year 56 BC (Cicero, De har. Resp. 28, 62) and serious repairs
were needed, and additional veteran colonists joining the territory of Potentia during the second
Triumvirate (Grom. Vet. 259 L.; CIL IX, 5654), probably had to be housed in the urban centre.
Probably the housing space of the town was extended some 50 m towards the sea, possibly erasing
the city wall on its eastern side, and realising the building of what is presumed by magnetometry to
be a theatre, well integrated in the extended town grid. According to the west gate excavations this
town restructuring, implying also a change in gate typology, was finalised during Augustus’ reign.
    Archaeological evidence for extra-mural habitation or town-related economic activity is still limited,
with the exception of the areas directly outside the northern, western and southern town gates where
artefact surveys showed some concentrations of Early Imperial domestic pottery (e.g. dolia) and brick
building materials. An artisanal-industrial zone dating to the Augustan period probably occupied the area
outside the northern gate, as well as a section of the adjoining intra-mural part of the town. The attestation
of a press floor in association with a kiln producing Dressel 6A wine amphorae and Dressel 6B oil
amphorae point towards an important production-related function of the area between the mid-1st century
BC and the mid-1st century AD (Monsieur 2007). The area of the possible river port must have been
located immediately outside the southern gate, where some vague crop marks alongside the identified
Roman paleo-channel suggest the presence of buildings and other harbour related infrastructure. Outside
the western gate a large artificial platform made of packed river gravel, encountered during our excavations
here, suggests early Imperial adaptations to the wetland situation for certain building infrastructure and
the easy movement of people directly outside the gate (Vermeulen et al. 2011).
The absence from the survey of extramural domestic materials in later Roman times suggests that from
the 3rd century AD onwards the habitation definitely contracted again within the walled urban area
(Vermeulen 2008), in the same time when one of the typical phenomena which characterised Roman
cities in Late Antiquity, such as the implantation of craft activities in previous public spaces, have been
documented by the excavations in the macellum, brought to light next to the temple area (Frapiccini
2004). The same surveys have also attested an apparent discontinuity of the habitation within Potentia
from the mid-5th century AD onwards, when mainly the forum area and the northern section of the town
remained in full use, while large parts of the eastern section seem to have been abandoned. These survey
results confirm some of the most significant excavation data for that era, such as the demolishing of the
temple next to the forum at the end of the 4th century AD, the gradual encroachment of that temple area
for private functions (such as storage dolia in the former portico) and the insertion of post-5th-century
tombs in the north-eastern corner of the town, in an zone previously occupied by active housing units.
The remote sensing and associated field operations have also provided clear insights into the zones
immediately outside the walls. All three roads leaving the town gates of Potentia in western, southern
and northern directions, have been detected most clearly as gravel roads. In the wetter and originally
more low lying areas to the west and south of the town, the road decks were bordered by parallel
ditches and were built on slightly elevated banks or low dikes. These roads give access to, and were
surrounded by, large funerary areas. The northern one, located on the dryer beach ridge is probably
the largest and surely the best known cemetery, as over 400 tombs were excavated here in the 1960’s
by the Soprintendenza (Mercando et al. 1974). It was in use from the 2nd century BC until the 4th
century AD. Furthermore, all cemeteries were most clearly observed thanks to the remote sensing
detection of rows of extra-mural funerary monuments positioned along the road sides. According to
the 2004 excavations by the Soprintendenza in the area north of the city (Percossi Serenelli 2007),
which clearly confirmed our earlier aerial photography data of these phenomena by revealing at
least five such monuments, they date mostly from Augustan times and the decades following his
reign, even if some earlier Late Republican ones might have been present here and there. Most of
these monuments seem to belong to tower-shaped constructions with square plans that increased the

      5. Rural settlement dynamics in the Potenza corridor between 900 BC and AD 600
      Dimitri Van Limbergen, Frank Vermeulen, Devi Taelman, Francesca Carboni


           5.1 Introduction


           T his chapter provides a synthetic overview of the contribution of our systematic surface
             observations to the understanding of rural settlement dynamics in the Potenza valley. Since the
      start of our terrain activities, preliminary examinations and interpretations of parts of our datasets have
      appeared at several occasions. These exploratory results have already proven their value in stimulating
      methodological debates in field survey archaeology, as well as in deepening our knowledge on rural
      settlement history in Italy (e.g. Verdonck, Vermeulen 2004; Verreyke, Vermeulen 2009; Vermeulen
      2012; 2014). Still, this synthesising chapter offers the opportunity to fully present for the first time the
      analytical results of our rural fieldwork in all three valley transects, and to link them to the diachronic
      patterns of the larger centralised sites in the area, whose main developments were discussed in chapter
      4. We start this chapter with an argumentation of our main methodological approaches and choices
      with regard to site definition, chronometry and classification; taking into account some potential
      biases in the surface record. This is followed by the presentation of our results in a second part. The
      third and final part then integrates these results within broader territorial and chronological changes in
      the Potenza valley, the wider central Adriatic area and the Italian peninsula as a whole.


           5.2. From survey scatters to ancient realities

         As has been explained earlier in the methodological chapter, the interpretative line between ‘site’ and
      ‘off-site’ concentrations was arbitrarily drawn on the field on the basis of significant apparent changes
      in artefact densities. The site catalogue at the back of this volume – to which we will repeatedly refer
      throughout this chapter – is a good illustration of how this decision-making process was not rigid, but
      rather dependent on field-specific conditions and artefact distributions.16 It follows that, here in the
      Potenza valley, ‘sites’ have been defined as “concentrations where the density of the recorded material
      suggested – at least to us – a human occupation or other sedentary activity in the past, covering either
      a single or multiple historical time periods”. Indeed, a common feature of our material assemblages
      is their identification as so-called ‘chronozones’; that is, areas with a clear diachronic character that
      were occupied over a prolonged period, sometimes with signs of spatial relocation between sub-
      phases. On the other hand, ‘off-sites’ are here considered to represent either site haloes, or the result
      of ancient activities such as rubbish disposal, pasturing or manuring. Especially on the lower ends of
      sloped fields, their distribution may also have been caused by post-depositional process like erosion
      (De Haas 2012, pp. 62-64). While we acknowledge the potential value of these off-site data for
      tracing and understanding human activity in the past, we have decided to focus our interpretative
      efforts in this chapter on the proper site data only because of prevailing uncertainties about the genesis
      processes of the former (e.g. Alcock, Cherry, Davis 1994, pp. 137-170; Fentress 2000).


             5.2.1. Diachronic data distribution and the extraction of chronological patterns

         The translation from recorded artefact scatters to diachronic settlement patterns is a
      complicated matter. It needs to consider several interpretative biases and, therefore, necessarily
      entails several assumptions, making the entire process inevitably a rather subjective enterprise.
      This should not weaken the scientific value of our interpretations, but rather motivate us to
      16
           Cf. the surveys in the Pontine region in southern Lazio by the Groningen Institute of Archaeology (e.g. Attema et al. 2002; 2004).
5. Rural settlement dynamics in the Potenza corridor between 900 BC and AD 600                                                        113




substantiate the chronological evaluation of our sites as much as possible, and to explain the
reasoning behind our methodological choices, even if only for purposes of comparability with
other survey projects.
We have stressed before that the extraction of meaningful chronological patterns from survey
pottery in the Potenza valley is hindered by diachronic differences in the availability of
representative dating material (e.g. Vermeulen 2012); a situation much inherent to Italian survey
archaeology in general. For example, thick and dark-reddish impasto wares dominate the Iron
Age material assemblages and the consistent lack of datable fine wares for this protohistoric
phase means that we can merely attribute these sites broadly to a period somewhere between
900 and 250 BC (Boullart 2003; Riva 2007).17 For the Late Republican period too, the generic
nature of most black gloss finds impedes secure fine-tuned dating within the last two centuries
BC. Regarding the red slip wares of the Imperial period, the chronological link between the end
of the Arretine terra sigillata distribution, its replacement by the northern and central Adriatic
‘medio-adriatica’ terra sigillata, and the start of the African Red Slip (ARS) imports in the 2nd-
3rd century AD is still not entirely clear (Brecciaroli Taborelli 1978; Verreyke, Vermeulen
2009, pp. 103-120). Finally, for the late Roman period, dating problems also arise from the 5th
century AD onwards, when import levels for one of the main chronological markers (ARS) drop
significantly (Verreyke, Vermeulen 2009).
     Over the years, several efforts have been made to increase the diagnostic potential of our
materials and to refine the chronology of some of the key-artefact categories. These include the
study of published excavated contexts and the building up of new ceramic sequences based on our
own excavations at Potentia and Casa Valentini in the lower valley area (Vermeulen et al. 2009;
2011; Vermeulen, Monsieur 2012). We may also mention here the work of external specialists on
local and regional protohistoric pottery, terra sigillata, Roman lamps and coins; as well as some
specific artefact research programs by members of the PVS, such as the Late Republican Black
Gloss studies by J. Van Kerckhove, the central Adriatic amphorae analyses by P. Monsieur and the
work by H. Verreyke on late Roman fine (e.g. ARS) and coarse wares (see chapter 2).
Still, the fact remains that – when comparing protohistoric and Iron Age material with later Roman
Age and late antique artefact collections – there is a rise and fall in the availability of closely datable
diagnostic pottery over time; that is, first an increase from the Iron Age to the Roman period in
general, and in particular from the Late Republican (250-50/25 BC) to the Early Imperial period
(50/25 BC – AD 100), and then a decrease from the Mid-Imperial phase (AD 100-300) onwards.
This means that the period between the mid-1st century BC and the mid-1st century AD will
automatically tend to be overrepresented compared to the preceding and following periods (Witcher
2008, pp. 278-279). For the Iron Age, the effect of underrepresentation is increased by the pottery
being handmade and coarse in texture – and thus more prone to fragmentation and weathering –
causing them to be generally less detectable than Roman Age and later pottery. Besides, simply
because it is older, protohistoric pottery is often buried deeper and has been subjected longer to
erosion. The methodology of line survey is known to be particularly sensitive to these biases, which
is why we have tried to remediate this situation in part through frequent revisits and re-surveys of
representative fields (see chapter 2). Of other contributing factors to artefact visibility – that is,
visibility conditions in the field, post-depositional processes and chosen walking distances – only the
first variable could be systematically accounted for in the chronological grouping of our intra-site
material (cf. Attema et al. 2000).18
17
   Although we have occasionally been able to distinguish between Early (900 BC-600 BC) and Late Iron Age (600 BC – 250 BC)
material in some of our assemblages (as specified in the site catalogue), we have opted in this chapter to not make the distinction
between the two periods and to discuss the survey evidence within a single Iron Age period of between 900 BC and 250 BC.
18
   For earlier discussions of these visibility factors, we refer to our preliminary reports (Vermeulen, Boullart 2001;
Vermeulen, Monsieur, Boullart 2002; Vermeulen et al. 2003; 2005; 2009).
114                                                                                         Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




          In any case, if we are to gain proper insight into the diachronic patterns of the historical entities
      hidden in our survey assemblages, we need to go beyond the merely acknowledgement of the specific
      nature of our data and the circumstances in which they were collected, registered and studied. To
      this purpose, we adopted a rather straightforward methodology that in its organisational set-up
      is reminiscent of the exemplary Boeotia and Ager Tarraconensis surveys, while in its diachronic
      data distribution model tributary to the work of Elizabeth Fentress and Philip Perkins (Bintliff,
      Snodgrass 1985; Carreté et al. 1995, pp. 253-255; Fentress, Perkins 1988, pp. 207-208). The basic
      idea of this approach – which was originally developed by Joseph-Maria Carreté, Simon Keay and
      Martin Millet for comparing and analysing pottery distributions in the Ager Tarraconensis (Spain)
      – is simple and considers each individual site as an assemblage whose values may be compared
      to those of the total assemblage of survey material (in this case the material from a single survey
      transect). As such, this method allows for visualising the relationship between each site and the
      general pattern that emerges from our artefact collections in the lower, middle and upper valley.
      But instead of seeking to clarify patterns of pottery distribution, our goal here was to assess phases
      of ‘certain’, ‘possible’, and ‘residual’ occupation within our multi-period site assemblages; with
      the latter category referring to phases in which the amount of material was considered too low for
      assigning site status in that particular period.
          Within survey archaeology, the thresholds for distinguishing between certain and possible
      phases of occupation are usually defined within a subjective process that is based on informed
      reasoning. For example, based on the registered material counts one can establish informed
      thresholds for total sherd counts, the number of period-specific building materials, or for the
      amounts of fine wares like black gloss and terra sigillata, or also for the number of more closely
      datable pieces within these fine wares. In practice, considering their different levels of visibility and
      representation in field surveys, Roman assemblages should hereby be assigned higher thresholds
      than Iron Age materials.19 Still, if established randomly within individual site concentrations, a
      framework against which to assess our assumptions lacks. Therefore, there is no way to know
      whether a certain arbitrary value for a particular material category actually constitutes a useful
      criterion for discerning between phases of certain and possible occupation in a certain period. This
      becomes even more problematic when – as is the case in the Potenza valley – fine wares are sparse
      or even lack on many rural sites. In other words, we create an impression of objectivity, but in
      origin our values remain subjective.
          In an attempt to overcome this problem, a uniform diachronic data distribution method was
      used for establishing these thresholds within multi-period datasets in a more systematic way. In
      this method described by Martin Millet (2000, p. 56), but going back to the work of Clive Orton
      (1980, pp. 99-100) – the material is distributed in a uniform way over fixed-time intervals. Each
      sherd is hereby considered to have an equal probability of occurrence in every sub-period within
      its established date range. For our model, 50 year intervals were used. Although smaller intervals
      of 25, 5 or even 1 year can be used in diachronic data models derived from survey data, we
      considered this larger interval to be more adapted to the level of chronological accuracy inherent
      to the PVS material categories. Furthermore, this time range facilitated further comparison of
      individual survey assemblages across the Potenza valley within the broader defined time-periods
      of Iron Age (900-250 BC), Late Republican (250-50/25 BC), Early Imperial (50/25 BC-AD 100),
      Mid-Imperial (AD 100-300) and Late Antique (AD 300-600). The formula applied for attributing
      occurrence values to the various artefact categories in each interval was the following (cf. Poblome
      et al. 2013; Willet 2014):

      19
        For example, an exemplary threshold of five pieces can be set as a ‘certain’ marker for the category of ceramic building
      material when analysing the Iron Age period. For the Roman period, we may want to use a higher threshold of 10 pieces. For
      an application, see De Haas 2012, pp. 29-31.
5. Rural settlement dynamics in the Potenza corridor between 900 BC and AD 600                                                   115




                            Time range of category within interval
                 Category = ———————————————— × number of sherds
                                  Total time span of type

    For example, if a particular sherd is dated between AD 20 and 90, then its occurrence within
the interval AD 50-100 is graphed as 0.571; for the interval AD 1-50, its occurrence is 0.429. Two
sherds of this type would be marked as 1.142 and 0.858, respectively. While this method still
gives greater weight to finer datable artefacts, its prime advantage is that it allows for the inclusion
of more broadly datable materials – that is, for example, various categories of Republican and
Imperial coarse and plain wares – into our site analyses. Indeed, when applied as such for all
material categories within a site assemblage, this provided us with dating profiles for individual
sites. Finally, based on the dating profiles of all sites within a particular valley transect (upper,
middle, lower) combined, a mean occurrence rate was derived for each 50 year interval. This
became the threshold for discerning ‘certain’ from ‘possible’ and ‘residual’ occupation phases
within site assemblages. For example, if the total linear data curve for the middle valley transect
indicated a mean occurrence rate of 12.6 for a certain 50-year period, then the data curve from each
individual site in the middle valley needed to attain an equal or higher rate to receive a ‘certain’
status for that period. All site values below this threshold received a ‘possible’ or ‘residual’ status.
In order to establish a lower threshold for the latter category – and to distinguish ‘possible’ from
‘residual’ occupation – we repeated the exercise with the known off-site assemblages. As such,
all site values between the mean occurrence rate of the site data curve and that of the off-site data
curve for a particular interval were marked as ‘possible’. Finally, the values that equalled or fell
below the total off-site mean rate were considered ‘residual’ and did not receive site status for the
interval concerned. An advantage of this method is that it automatically assigns higher thresholds
for better represented periods for which more recognisable and diagnostic data are available. In
practice, this means that for a site in the Early Imperial period to be classified as ‘certain’, a higher
value needs to be reached than for a site in the Iron Age or Late Republican period.20 The entire
procedure is summarised in figure 79. The obtained settlement dynamics models for each valley
transect are visualised in tables 3 to 5.
    As stressed earlier, we are aware that the interpretation of survey assemblages is an inherently
flawed process. We do not claim that the method proposed here is critique-proof.21 However, we
consider the model successful for the purpose of this chapter, as it has almost always confirmed
our initial impressions in the field, as well as our earlier qualitative assessments of the surface
material (e.g. Vermeulen et al. 2006; 2013; Vermeulen 2012; Verreyke, Vermeulen 2009) (see
site catalogue). Especially for the longue-durée sites, it has enabled us to better pinpoint phases of
highlighted occupation within the Roman period. Furthermore, even if doubts about the accuracy
of our surface records remain, we do get the impression that the model has given both the Iron
Age and post-2nd-century AD occupation in the valley a firmer voice. In our opinion, this chapter
can therefore offer a useful step forward in the understanding of rural settlement dynamics in the
Potenza valley.

20
   The results of our data models were further tested in two ways. First, we ‘informed’ them by building diachronic
data distribution curves based only on the fine ware typologies and other finer datable materials. In all cases, these
either confirmed or enhanced the assigned status for a site in a particular period. Second, we alternatively built a
data model in which the values from the individual site assemblages were compared with the total assemblage of
survey material from the whole valley. This gave largely similar results to the ones obtained by the first methodology
– that is, comparing the site values with the total values from a certain valley transect – but displayed lower levels
of periodical site recognition, especially for the Iron Age. As such, this strengthened our belief in the workability of
the method used.
21
   A recent talk at the “Fields, Sherds and Scholars” workshop in Athens (2017) has further explored some of the possibilities
and issues of this method. The proceedings of this conference will be published in 2018.
116                                                                                             Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




           5.2.2. Settlement site classification

           A second issue of site interpretation is that of site classification. For the pre-Roman period, we
      possess too little information for discerning distinct rural site types. While over the years some 16
      sites were encountered in the Potenza valley that contained predominantly Iron Age material, the
      poor and uniform character of these assemblages does not allow for more in-depth classifications or
      interpretations.22 It is thus difficult to evaluate the precise status of these sites, even if an identification
      in all cases as the location of former ‘settlements’ is at least highly likely. Using variations in size,
      we can roughly distinguish three sub-groups in this category: small sites of between 50 and 675 m²,
      medium-sized sites of between 1000 and 2000 m², and larger sites of between 2400-4500 m². For
      reasons cited at the beginning of this chapter, we realise that such a parameter needs to be applied with
      caution, in particular for the sketchy Iron Age surface record (see supra). These scatters are probably
      all to be identified as simple farms or temporary dwellings for which we have no further information.23
      Another feature of the protohistoric landscape in the Potenza valley is the presence of a series of
      larger nucleated settlements.24 These are generally situated on hilltops and hilly plateaus and are
      often of a fortified nature. They vary in size between 2 and 10 hectares and can be found in all
      parts of the valley. In the PVS transects, four sites of this type were studied: Monte Primo, Monte
      Pitino, Monte Franco and Montarice. These will not be discussed here, as they have been treated
      extensively in chapter 4. More such central sites can be presumed to have existed in other dominant
      positions of the valley now taken in by modern towns, such as Montecassiano, Recanati and Potenza
      Picena. Here, however, many traces have probably been erased, while surface survey can no longer
      be used to reveal their potential existence, but occasional in situ finds – often of a funerary nature –
      have at least suggested their presence in the past (Percossi et al. 2006).
           Our picture is clearer for the Roman period. In the past, we have offered preliminary
      classifications of our rural and other non-urban settlement sites in the Potenza valley at several
      occasions (e.g. Verdonck, Vermeulen 2004; Vermeulen et al. 2006; Vermeulen 2012; Vermeulen,
      Mlekuz 2012; Verreyke, Vermeulen 2009). Now, with all materials and sites studied in more detail,
      we can propose a more fine-tuned site classification for these sites. For reasons of compatibility
      and comparability, the framework for this site typology builds further on our earlier efforts. Key-
      criteria for classifying Roman rural settlement in the study area thus remain scatter size, the quantity
      and quality of the artefacts, the nature of building materials, topography and information on intra-
      site structuring. All these data are provided in the site catalogue. These were collected by using a
      wide range of complementary field techniques. The basic data for establishing hypotheses about
      the nature of our rural sites come from the systematic artefact surveys and the subsequent study
      of pottery groups and construction materials. In many cases, regular site revisits in the form of
      random sampling added useful chronological and functional information. For a few selected sites,
      our knowledge on chronology, site size and functional zoning was particularly enhanced through
      detailed grid surveys.25 Data on the presence of building structures or other organisational aspects
      that have much enhanced our field observations are provided by remote sensing operations (e.g.
      aerial photography, geophysical prospection) and by occasional ground-truthing operations (e.g.
      augering, test trenches).26 Finally, contextual information for rural settlement in the central Adriatic
      area was gathered by looking at contemporary survey projects in the Marche region (e.g. the ‘Pisa
      South Picenum Survey’ project around Fermo (Menchelli 2012) and at some of the (limited)
      published excavation data for rural sites in the region (e.g. Mercando 1979, pp. 89-296; Pignocchi
      2001; Dall’aglio, Vergari 2001; Percossi 2005; Frapiccini et al. 2006, pp. 233-310).
      22
         Sites 1, 2, 3, 5, 15, 16, 19, 20, 22, 23, 25, 26, 27, 53, 71, 230 (see site catalogue).
      23
         On our maps, these sites are classified as ‘type 9’ sites (protohistoric rural site).
      24
         These are indicated as ‘type 10’ sites on our maps (protohistoric nucleated site).
      25
         Sites 101, 108, 121, 125 (see site catalogue).
      26
         For some considerations on the use of these criteria for site classification, see e.g. Vermeulen 2012, pp. 47-50.

                          Based on all this information, we can now propose the following site classification for all non-
                       urban Roman settlement in the Potenza valley, comprising both single settlement units (types 1-5)
                       and a form of grouped rural settlement (type 6) 27:
                          • Type 1: very small concentrations up to 300 m² (0.03 ha) in size, characterised by a dominance
                          of simple building materials (e.g. uncut stone blocks, pebbles, roof tiles) and few (coarse) pottery,
                          mostly storage and utility wares. These scatters probably refer to small rural units that represent
                          either temporary (seasonal) dwellings or isolated structures (e.g. storage deposits, secondary
                          buildings, outhouses of larger estates, etc.). Twenty-two sites belong to this category.28
                          • Type 2: small concentrations of between 300 m² and 1200 m² (0.03-0.12 ha). Fairly simple
                          building materials still prevail (e.g. worked stone blocks, bricks, roof tiles), but pottery
                          assemblages are generally of a more varied and sometimes richer nature (coarse and plain wares,
                          amphorae, fine wares). These scatters are probably best described as (single-unit) small farms.
                          Twenty-four sites were classified as such.29
                          • Type 3: medium-sized concentrations of between 1200 m² and 2400 m² (0.12-0.24 ha), where
                          the distributions of common building materials (e.g. sandstone- and limestone blocks, roof
                          tiles, sometimes marble crustae and stucco) and pottery (e.g. mostly common and cooking
                          wares, amphorae, dolia, and occasionally fine wares and lamps) points towards the existence of
                          respectable medium-sized farms. This category includes nineteen sites.30
                       27

       • Type 4: large concentrations of between 2400 m² and 4000 m² (0.24-0.4 ha). The amount and
       the diversity of building materials (e.g. roof- and floor tiles, tubuli, large cut sandstone- and
       limestone blocks, bricks) often point towards the existence of several units (e.g. main building
       with a few outhouses or a large building subdivided into functional zones). The attested pottery
       assemblages contain a wide range of common pottery, fine wares and other (imported) artefacts.
       We therefore consider these scatters as large farms or small/simple villas. In the entire Potenza
       Valley Survey, seventeen sites were labelled as such.31
       • Type 5: very large concentrations of between 4500 m² and 9500 m² (0.45-0.95 ha) with a
       very wide range of building materials (e.g. stone blocks, bricks, tiles, tubuli, column fragments,
       floor slabs, lumps of concrete, mortar) and clear signs of luxury or a certain comfort (e.g.
       marble crustae, mosaic tesserae, painted stucco, bronze artefacts, coins). The layout of these
       concentrations suggests the presence of distinct functional sectors and/or several separate
       buildings, while the pottery is more varied than in all previous types, with a larger share of fine
       wares and imported products (e.g. lamps, amphorae). These wide scatters thus seem to approach
       our idea of a (large) Roman villa. In total, seventeen sites fulfil these criteria (Fig. 80).32
31
     Sites 4, 7, 29, 30, 33, 35, 38, 47, 54, 58, 72, 77, 101, 121, 126, 127, 225 (see site catalogue).
32
     Sites 37, 44, 49, 51, 55, 59, 66, 69, 85, 89, 100, 104, 105, 124, 125, 129, 224 (see site catalogue). Site 89 represents an
122                                                                                                 Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




          • Type 6: medium to large areas with a multitude of nuclei, measuring between 3,000 m² and
          10,000 m² (0.3-1 ha) that are to be associated with vici and other village-like or roadside
          nucleated settlements. Here, a proper internal structuring or spatial clustering may be
          discerned on the basis of the survey results, while the variety and the quality of building
          materials and pottery are mostly in the top segment, but depend on the general chronology
          of these Roman settlements. For the moment, only three such sites are well identified in our
          survey transects of the Potenza valley: a large roadside settlement at Passo di Treia in the
          Middle valley (see also chapter 4)33, and two probable Late Republican hamlet or village-like
          settlements (Montarice and Casa Cingolani) near the coast. As has been stated higher (chapter
          4) it is however likely that also the early stages of the Roman towns in the valley (Septempeda,
          Trea, Ricina and maybe even Potentia) belonged to this category of roadside villages, while
          these sites probably returned for a while to the non-urban stages in the final phases of their
          existence, before becoming extinct34.
          It is presumed that these six types represent the full spectrum of non-urban settlement sites
      in the valley. They do, however, not include a series of distinct Roman site types that were also
      observed during the systematic field surveys and some of the remote sensing operations, mostly
      in the suburban areas of the towns, but not used for our rural habitation dynamics analyses.
      These include road segments, elements of land division and ditches, bridges, aqueducts,
      sanctuaries, funerary areas, and areas for activities such as pottery production (e.g. amphorae)
      and clay extraction.35

  5.3 Characterising the rural settlement history of the Potenza valley


    5.3.1 Iron Age (900-250 BC)

    Despite some of the difficulties surrounding the interpretation of protohistoric field scatters
(see supra), a few main trends for the Iron Age period in the Potenza valley can still be
discussed.36 The overall picture of the rural landscape in all three survey transects seems to be
one of a series of smallish settlements distributed randomly in-between the larger nucleated
hilltop sites discussed in the previous chapter (Fig. 82). The poor representation of these
protohistoric sites in the survey record – sometimes only by a few sherds – is in line with other
surface observations in Italy and Greece, and they are probably best identified as isolated,
36
   For previous considerations on the rural protohistoric landscape of the Potenza valley based on preliminary interpretations
of the PVS data, we refer to Vermeulen, Boullart 2001, pp. 11-12; Vermeulen, Monsieur, Boullart 2002, pp. 54-57;
Vermeulen, Verreyke and Boullart 2006, pp. 77-84 and Vermeulen, Mlekuz 2012, pp. 208-211.
124                                                                                          Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




      often short-lived farmsteads (e.g. Di Gennaro, Stoddard 1982; Bintliff 1998). The frequent
      association of these Iron Age concentrations with areas of darker grey soil (possibly ploughed-
      up pits) and the occasional occurrence of storage vessels could point towards a small-scale
      agricultural or pastoral-like nature for most of these sites.37 The general scarcity of building
      materials like stones and tiles suggests the presence of mostly simple and ephemeral wattle and
      daub huts. In some cases, ceramic tiles indicates the existence of more substantial buildings,
      provided tiled roofs. Good examples of this latter category within the PVS database are site 15
      (Casa Castellano) in the middle valley, and sites 215 (Casa Leopardi) and 216 (Colle Burchio-
      Autostrada) in the lower valley. The multi-core site 219 (Treia-SS. Crucifisso) might represent
      an aggregation of such buildings, possibly forming a sort of semi-centralised village preceding
      the later Roman roadside settlement of Trea (Fig. 81) (see Ch. 4) (Vermeulen et al. 2009;
      Vermeulen 2014, pp. 146-148).38
          The largest concentration of protohistoric sites occurs in the middle valley (Fig. 83; Table 7).
      In terms of site densities, this feature translates itself into a density of 8.4 sites/km²; that is, about
      twice as high as compared to densities of 4.8 and 3.6 sites/km² in the upper and lower valley areas
      respectively.39 This might in part be related to historical events – that is, a higher level of Iron Age
      activity in the middle valley zone due to the central presence in our survey transect of the important
      central place Monte Franco (see chapter 4) – but biases in archaeological visibility may also be at
      play. For the lower valley plain in particular, there is the possibility that the protohistoric sites are
      now buried too deep in order to be detected by field survey in the valley bottom. This is mainly
      because of the influence of a succession of anthropogenic and natural processes in post-antique
      times on the landscape (see chapter 3). In essence, these processes have resulted in a wide array
      of physical transformations that have both eroded and buried past rural occupation under layers of
      sediment, meaning that the survey results may not accurately reflect the situation in the Iron Age.40
      To a certain extent, the same holds for the Roman Age sites and we will come back to this issue
      later in this chapter.




      37
         A number of animal bones (e.g. cow, pig, sheep-goat) have been recovered from some of these sites, but these cannot be
      dated with certainty to the Iron Age.
      38
         Seemingly similar developments towards houses with stone foundations and permanent roofs have been detected on the
      hilltop settlements of Monte Pitino and Montarice (see chapter 4). Just outside the Potenza valley, buildings with wooden
      posts, foundations of river pebbles and tiled roofs seem to have characterised the protohistoric settlement of Matelica from
      the 5th/4th century BC onwards (Biocco 2000, pp. 59-65; De Marinis, Silvestrini 2005).
      39
         These densities are calculated on the basis of the intensively surveyed areas by the PVS within the three chosen transect
      zones: 3.15 km² in the upper valley, 3.69 km² in the middle valley, and 3.88 km² in the lower valley (Vermeulen, Mlekuz
      2012). The same goes for the Roman site densities further in this chapter.
      40
         In the Iron Age and Roman period, the typically hilly Adriatic valley-landscape consisted of meandering rivers that
      entered a series of lagoons and swamps at the junction with the sea (Coltorti 1997, p. 317). However, from c. AD 100
      onwards – and especially between the 15th and the 19th century – an always increasing anthropogenic deforestation for
      purposes of land reclamation caused the degradation of the hill slopes and the filling of the river valleys with sediment
      (Coltorti 1997; Goethals, De Dapper, Vermeulen 2005). At the river mouths, these sediments gradually filled-up the
      swamps and lagoons along the coast (Giarizzo 1963; Ortolani/Alfieri 1978; Jacobelli et al. 1982; Anselmi 1986).
      Especially in the lower reaches of the rivers, the interaction of these two phenomena led to the creation of the so-
      called braided river plain system (Coltorti 1997). In addition, many rivers were artificially diverted or rectified. At the
      beginning of the 20th century, a reduction in erosion and thus in sediment supply occurred. This was mainly because of
      the introduction of the ‘alberata’ system in Italian agriculture and the accompanying anti-erosion measures, like artificial
      drainages for protection against flooding and artificial levees for extending areas suitable for cultivation (Coltorti 1997;
      Goethals, De Dapper, Vermeulen 2005).

    In both the upper and middle valley transects, the location of farmsteads and hamlets often
seem to have been determined by the vicinity of easily accessed water sources, such as natural
springs or the confluence of torrents.41 Other sites coincided clearly with strips of cultivable land
along both sides of the Potenza River. In the lower valley area, the extensions of the hilly ridges
bordering the Potenza plain and the sandy beach ridge along the coast in particular proved to be
preferred topographies for occupation in protohistoric times (Fig. 84). In all three survey transects,
we get the impression that this pattern of dispersed rural settlement was structured around – and
probably located in the territory of – a series of central places; that is, Montarice in the lower valley,
Monte Franco in the middle valley and Monte Primo in the upper valley. This combination of ‘rural
infill’ and the development of more aggregated forms of settlement (cf. also site 219) illustrate well
the unfolding of a proto-urban landscape in full development that likely reflects a socio-economic
 40
settlement   structure with its roots in the later Bronze Age (Vermeulen, Mlekuz 2012).42

               5.3.2 Roman Age and Late Antiquity (250 BC – AD 600)

          With the arrival of the Romans in the Potenza valley, the nature of rural settlement becomes more
      adapt to scanning by systematic artefact surveys. Today, after more than a decade of fieldwalking and
      the reassessment of older survey data, aerial and geophysical survey, augering and test-trenching,
      the PVS site catalogue contains 89 certain and possible sites that may be defined as loci of rural
      habitation in Roman and/or late antique times.43 The dating profiles of these sites as reconstructed
      through the aforementioned methodology are listed in tables 3-5, while the overall changing
      occupation patterns in the three valley transects throughout the main chronological periods are
      visualised in tables 6-8. As mentioned earlier in this chapter, these tables were created on the basis
      of site dating profiles graphed according to intervals of 50-year periods. In practice, one interval of
      certain occupation within a broader period hereby sufficed for assigning a certain status for a site
      in that period. For example, if in the Early Imperial period (50/25 BC-AD 100) two intervals of a
      site were labelled as ‘possible’ and one as ‘certain’, the overall period received a ‘certain’ label for
      that site. A ‘possible’ status was only awarded when all intervals within the period were labelled as
      ‘possible’.44 For the Roman and late antique period, slight fluctuations in site numbers were noticed
      between the sub-intervals of the various main phases; which is why it was deemed useful to include
      here the settlement dynamics patterns based on these 50-years intervals, as they allowed for a higher
      chronological resolution and helped in refining our interpretations. The latter patterns are visualised
      in tables 9-11. The distribution patterns of the sites in the main chronological periods are visualised
      in figures 85-96. These tables and figures form the basis for the discussion of the chronological
      evolution of rural settlements in the Potenza valley that follows.
          In both the lower and upper valley zones, the Late Republican phase (250-50/25 BC) is marked
      in our survey transects by a rise in site numbers compared to the Iron Age. Based on certain and
      possible sites combined, site densities are now 7.6/km² in the upper area, and 5.7/km² in the lower
      area. The middle valley contrasts somewhat with this pattern by displaying a drop in site numbers
      between Iron Age and Roman Age, with a density of 4.9 sites/km² in the Late Republican period
      43
         These concern 35 sites in the upper valley, 24 in the middle, and 30 in the lower valley. Mention must be made here of
      another eleven Roman rural sites that – even if they were included into the site typology (see supra) – were not involved in
      our settlement analysis, as they were classified as ‘residual’ because of their very small amount of material (sites 39, 40, 42,
      43, 72, 73, 81, 103, 118, 122, 130; see site catalogue).
      44
         The same strategy was followed for the Iron Age sites.

     As noticed earlier in this chapter, this apparent downfall may be biased by the pronounced Iron
Age activity in the middle valley area because of the central presence of Monte Franco in our survey
transect (see supra). Based on the more detailed 50-year interval graphs, slight variations within the
Late Republican period may be noticed – there seems, for example, to have been a peak in activity
towards the beginning of the period in the middle valley – but the overall patterns are similar.
     With regard to continuity between protohistoric and Roman times, it is remarkable that in the
lower valley 12 out of the 14 Iron Age sites continue to be occupied into the Late Republican
period (85.7% of all Iron Age sites).45 Another 11 sites are new foundations.46 This high degree of
continuity is absent in the middle valley area, where only 13 out of 31 Iron Age sites show traces
of occupations in the Late Republican period (41.9% of all Iron Age sites).47 With only 5 new sites,
the input of Roman foundations is also less pronounced here.48 Besides, the 50-year graph for this
area indicates that a good number of the continuing sites do so only for a very short time, as such
increasing the contrast in site numbers between the Iron Age and the Roman Age. A lower degree
of continuity is also noticeable in the upper valley, where 7 out of 15 Iron Age sites continue into
Roman Age (46.7%), but there a large input of 17 new sites is visible in the Late Republican period.49
It thus seems that the arrival of the Romans in the Potenza valley in particular gave an impulse to the
dispersed settlement in the countryside in the upper valley, where more advanced agricultural and
pastoral techniques and broader food practices possibly enabled a more efficient exploitation of the
limited land resources in this area.
     When involving site typologies into our analyses, significant differences in settlement
structure become noticeable between the three sub-regions. In the upper area, large farms and
villas dominate the landscape from the start – and would continue to do so in the following
periods – while in the lower area a more balanced pattern between smaller and larger site types
occurs. The middle valley distinguishes itself through the presence of village-type settlements in
this period, such as the one situated on the first flat terrace of the Potenza to the north of the river
(sites 79, 94) (Fig. 100). Spreading out over an area of at least 1.8 hectare – but almost certainly
much larger – this settlement seems to have evolved out of smaller Iron Age concentrations and
shows traces of occupation until late antique times (see site catalogue). A similar process seems
visible at the aforementioned site 219 (see supra), where the Iron Age agglomeration continues
to show signs of occupation in Roman times, but gradually becomes part of the wider western
suburbium of Trea (Fig. 81). Two smaller hamlets also seem to have developed in the lower
valley, where Late Republican multi-core occupation phases have been attested at the sites of
Montarice (24) and Casa Cingolani (119). Several Roman villages are known in Italy, but their
role in the organisation of the Republican landscape is still poorly understood. A recent study has
stressed their potential role in the settlement organisation of newly conquered landscapes, where
the clustering of people in smaller centres in the countryside offered certain advantages with
regard to security and land exploitation (Pelgrom 2008). A similar scenario is not unthinkable
for the Potenza valley.
     With the transition to the Early Imperial phase (50/25 BC-AD 100), Roman site numbers reach
their peak in all three valley transects. While in the middle valley the occupation density never
achieves the levels of the Iron Age period (5.7 sites/km²), site densities now reach their all-time
maximum levels in both the lower (7.2 sites/km²) and the upper valley area (9.8 sites/km²). In the
lower valley, except for two sites (n. 27, 32), all Republican occupations (21 in total, or 91.3%)
