      continue into the Early Imperial period. They are accompanied by 6 new sites.50 A similar pattern
      is noticeable in the middle valley, where 16 out of 18 Republican sites (94.4%) remain occupied in
      Early Imperial times, with 5 new sites appearing in this period.51 In the upper valley too, 23 out of
      24 Republican sites continue (95.8%), while 8 new sites appear in this area too.52 A fine example of
      the latter process in the upper valley is the large and rich Imperial villa at Campo di Concentramento
      (site 100), where our artefact surveys and aerial photography revealed a rectangular building with
      separated residential and productive sectors that would remain occupied until late antique times
      (see site catalogue). The 50-year interval graph indicates how this phase of overall vitality seems to
      be particularly pronounced in the first 50 years of the Early Imperial period in the lower and upper
      valley, while in the middle valley this phase is more balanced. We would like to stress here that,
      although we are aware that the period between the mid-1st century BC and the mid-1st century AD
      in Italy has a higher level of archaeological visibility because of the availability of better datable
      fine wares – and is thus probably biased compared to previous and later periods – our diachronic
      data distribution model was specifically developed to account for this matter, as higher thresholds
      needed to be reached for a site to be classified as such in these 50-year intervals (see supra). It thus
      seems that we are effectively seeing here a flourishing phase of Roman rural settlement in the entire
      Potenza valley.
          On the whole, Early Imperial settlement structures are a continuation of Late Republican patterns.
      Large farms and villas remain a prominent feature of the landscape in the upper valley, while in
      the lower valley a wide range of settlement types have equal importance, ranging from single
      house units over small farms to large villas. Once again, the middle valley displays a somewhat
      different evolution, with small house units in particular becoming an important characteristic of the
      countryside. It is also conspicuous that this latter category remains unattested in the upper valley.
      These different patterns of rural settlement probably reflect in part variations in land organisation
      and agricultural exploitation, but the nature of our evidence makes the linking of detected site
      distributions with specific historical land regimes a rather problematic issue. Nevertheless, in the
      next section of this chapter, some food for thought is offered on this matter (see infra).
          The Mid-Imperial phase (AD 100-300) in the Potenza valley is generally characterised by a
      drop in site numbers. This drop is most noticeable in the lower and middle valley, where 8 out of
      28 sites (28.6%) and 8 out of 21 sites (38.1%) respectively now ceased to be occupied.53 With only
      4 out of 31 sites (12.9%) disappearing from our radar, the drop is less pronounced in the upper
      valley. Furthermore – apart from one site in the upper valley (68) and one site in the middle valley
      (222) – our data indicate that no new sites were established in the valley in this period. We have
      suggested before that this might point towards a diminished level of investment in the countryside
      (e.g. Vermeulen 2012, p. 53). Once again, the 50-year graph tells a somewhat more nuanced version
      of this story. In both the upper and the lower valley, site numbers start to drop already in the second
      half of the 1st century AD and then gradually diminish between the 2nd and 3rd century AD. In the
      upper valley, a small revival is noticeable in the 2nd century AD. Finally, in the middle valley, the
      decline in occupation is more abrupt and occurs entirely after AD 100. In any case, for all survey
      transects, the graph suggests that this decline was mostly a phenomenon of the 3rd century AD.
      Concerning settlement structures in this period, no real changes are visible in the upper valley. In the
      lower valley, the combination of small and large farms becomes the most dominant feature, while in
      the middle valley, these small- and medium sized farms characterise the landscape around the still-
      existing vicus at Passo di Treia (see supra).
      50
         Sites 31, 107, 109, 110, 115, 218 (see site catalogue).
      51
         Sites 15, 17, 95, 217, 225 (see site catalogue).
      52
         Sites 33, 36, 37, 41, 48, 52, 65, 100 (see site catalogue).
      53
         Sites 31, 107, 109, 110, 115, 126, 129, 218 (lower valley) and 15, 17, 82, 85, 91, 95, 99, 225 (middle valley). In the middle
      valley, one Iron Age site becomes active again (222) (see site catalogue).
5. Rural settlement dynamics in the Potenza corridor between 900 BC and AD 600                              147




    Given the uncertainties of the surface record in this period – as was mentioned in the first part of
this chapter, 2nd and 3rd century AD pottery remains difficult to identify and it is therefore entirely
feasible that we ‘missed’ certain site occupation phases during our surveys (see supra) – it is not
possible to determine the precise nature of this apparent decline in site numbers in the Potenza
valley. There is, however, no doubt that the Italian countryside underwent important socio-economic
changes in this period and some connection with these events is likely (e.g. Barker 1995, p. 225;
Christie 2004, p. 11; Wickham 2005, p. 107; see also infra this chapter).
    This downward trend in site numbers continues into the Late Antique phase (AD 300-600). In
the lower valley, only 11 out of 20 Mid-Imperial sites (55%) remain occupied; in the middle valley
this is 42.9% (6/14) and in the upper valley 66.7% (18/27). In the entire Potenza valley, the 50-year
interval graph implies some resilience in the 4th-5th century AD, and a definite drop in site numbers
afterwards, with the exception of the upper valley. Tables 3 to 5 allow for obtaining a long-term
view on settlement occupation in the area. Indeed, one of the advantages of our data distribution
visualisation is that we can easily pick out longue durée sites and pinpoint phases of highlighted
activity within the Roman and late antique period. This enables, for example, to discern sites with a
major Late Republican occupation from those whose material emphasises the Imperial period. The
most remarkable pattern, however, is that the occupation of practically all late antique sites goes
back to at least the Early Imperial period, and often even to the Late Republic. A quick look at the
late antique settlement structure in the Potenza valley learns that these are mostly large farms and
villas, which now dominate the landscape in all parts of the valley.
    We have stressed before that survey evidence alone is not sufficient for adequately grasping
the full evolution in the countryside during this period. This is particularly true for the 5th and
6th centuries AD, when the importance of (easily recognisable) imported goods diminished and
the region fell increasingly back on regional exchange systems and (ill-known) local pottery
distributions; a process that has obvious consequences for the identification of site occupation in
this period. Still, our earlier comparative work has already shown that this era marked a phase of
significant structural changes in the central Adriatic countryside, especially after the 4th century AD
(Verreyke, Vermeulen 2009; Vermeulen, Carboni 2011; Vermeulen 2012; 2014). Some of these
transformations are reflected in the well-known example of the rural dwelling of Pollenza Santa
Lucia, just to the south of the middle Potenza valley, where in the 4th century AD an olive press
and a storage room with dolia were installed within former living quarters, destroying as such the
black-and-white geometric mosaic of Augustan date (Mercando 1980; Percossi 2005; Frapiccini
et al. 2006, pp. 233-310; Van Limbergen 2011, pp. 87-88). This is a part of a wider phenomenon
in late antique Italy, with certain residential areas of villas being reused for agricultural activities
or industrial production; an evolution whose interpretation has now shifted from social decline or
degradation scenarios to one which considers these transformations as the expression of a rural land
class that now lived in a different, non-classical style (Marzano 2007).


  5.4 An evolving Roman landscape

  The multi-disciplinary approach of the PVS has proven particularly successful in improving our
knowledge on rural occupation patterns in the Potenza valley between Late Republican and late
antique times. A full understanding of these patterns, however, can only be achieved by placing them
within the broader rural and urban developments in the central Adriatic area. The confrontation of our
analysed survey data with ancient textual sources and other archaeological information now allows
for better contextualising our results within wider processes of urbanisation, territorial colonisation,
land occupation and agricultural exploitation in the region. Some of our observations here build
further on earlier interpretations; others are presented as preliminary trajectories for future research.
148                                                                                                             Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




    Fig. 97 Roman
  towns, press sites
 and amphora kilns
 in central Adriatic
              Italy.




                         The developments in the Potenza valley from the 2nd century BC onwards finds their roots
                       in Rome’s intense military involvement in the central Adriatic area between the end of the 4th
                       century BC and the mid-3rd century BC. Initially, these contacts were established in the form of
                       treaties, first with the native centre of Camerinum in 310 BC (Livy IX.36) (Fig. 97, n. 29) and
                       later with the Picene population as a whole in 299 BC (Livy X.10.12; XI.7.8). In particular the
                       latter partnership – which probably applied to the territory between the rivers Esino and Salinello
                       – was inspired by Rome’s desire to find allies in their response to the hostile attitude expressed
                       by the Praetutti and the Samnites in the south, and the Celtic people in the north (Laffi 1975;
                       Raggi 2014). The fruits of these alliances were harvested in 295 BC, when the Roman army
                       decided the outcome of the Third Samnite War by defeating a coalition of Samnites, Etruscans,
                       Umbrians and their Celtic allies at Sentinum. Another concrete result was the conquest of the
                       territory of the Praetutti – that is, the Ager Praetutianus – in 290 BC by Manius Curius Dentatus.
                       About a decade later, in 283 BC, these events led to the incorporation of the Ager Gallicus –
                       the territory north of the river Esino taken by Rome from the Celtic Senones – into the Roman
                       ager publicus (Harris 1971; Sisani 2007). Rome’s increasing presence in the Adriatic, however,
                       did not bode well with the Picenian people, who ultimately ended their collaboration with an
                       armed rebellion in 269/268 BC; a conflict that eventually ended in their disadvantage and which
                       resulted in a partial deportation of the Picene population and the full annexation of the Ager
                       Picenus into the Roman state.54 Those who remained were organised in settlements that first had
                       the status of civitas sine suffragio (partial Roman citizenship), and later on civitas pleno iure

                       54
                         Literary sources that refer to these events are Livy (Per. XV); Eutropius (Eutr. II, 9); Florus (Flor. I, 14); Orosius (Oros.
                       IV.4.5-7 and Frontinus (Str. I.12. 3); see also Delplace 1993, 4, note 24; regarding the partial deportation of the Picentes to
                       the region of Salerno, see Strabo (Geogr. 5.4.13) and Pliny (HN 3.5.70).
5. Rural settlement dynamics in the Potenza corridor between 900 BC and AD 600                                                  149




(full citizenship).55 The notable exceptions were the indigenous Italic settlements of Asculum
and Ancona, which acquired the status of civitas foederata or federate town (Fig. 97, n. 13, 33).56
   Amid these tumultuous events, a series of Latin (L) and Roman (R) colonies were founded by
the Romans along the central Adriatic coast: Hatria (L) and Sena Gallica (R) between 290/289
and 285/283 BC; Ariminum (L) more to the north (268 BC); Castrum Novum (R) and Firmum
Picenum (L) around 264 BC, and possibly Aesis (R) in 247 BC (Delplace 1993, pp. 11-13, 22) (Fig.
97). Right from the beginning, this first colonisation wave was accompanied by the distribution
of viritane allotments on the confiscated lands; a process that reached a more systematic character
around 232 BC with the appliance of the Lex Flaminia de agro Gallico et Piceno viritim dividendo
(Moscatelli 1985; Sisani 2007). Together with the development of the Via Flaminia and its side
roads between 223 BC and 220 BC, these initiatives – named after the tribune Gaius Flaminius – led
to a significant reorganisation of the central Adriatic territory. One important consequence was the
gradual establishment of a series of praefecturae or road stations – often sprouted from pre-existing
indigenous settlements, but not necessarily with an urban character – along these routes, such as
Forum Sempronii, Suasa, Ostra, Sentinum, Cingulum, Tolentinum, Falerio and Cupra Montana
(Fig. 97). In the Potenza valley, this process was probably more a phenomenon of the 2nd century
BC, with the road stations of Ricina, Trea and Septempeda along the main Apennine-coastal arteries
(see chapter 4). After the Second Punic War (218 BC-201 BC) the foundation of the colony of
Potentia (184 BC) in the lower Potenza valley formed part of a new colonisation wave that also
included the towns of Pisaurum (184 BC) in the Foglia river valley and Auximum (174 BC-157/156
BC) in the Musone valley (Fig. 97) (see chapter 4).57 Also, in the final decades of the 2nd century
BC, the territories of Ancona and Auximum were touched by the Gracchian land reforms (133-123
BC). This profound reorganisation of the ager publicus probably also led to the foundation of the
Roman colony of Pollentia (later Urbs Salvia) (Fig. 97) (Delplace 1993, pp. 162-163).58
   In the wake of the Social War (91-88 BC) – yet another fruitless attempt of the Italian allies to
separate themselves from the Roman State – the gradual process of transforming the praefecturae
into municipia – that is, autonomous administrative towns – was set in motion. The settlements that
received the status in the first half of the 1st century BC were governed by a college of four magistrates
(quattorviri); those that acquired it after the mid-1st century BC had a college of two magistrates
(duoviri) (Moscatelli 1985; Paci 1998a; Sisani 2007; Marengo 2012). Based on this distinction,
it seems that the towns of Pitinum Pisaurense, Urvinum Mataurense, Fanum Fortunae, Tuficum,
Attidium, and Camerinum belong to the first category; the towns of Forum Sempronii, Suasa, Ostra,
Cupra Montana, Planina, Cingulum, Matilica, and Cupra Marittima would belong to the second
category. The same goes for Ricina, Trea, and Septempeda in the Potenza valley (see chapter 4).59
   Of equal importance for the urban development of the central Adriatic area was the fact that
from the 1st century BC onwards this process was accompanied by a vast program of veteran
resettlement and land distributions. Indeed, the Liber Coloniarum informs us about the assignment
of veteran allotments in the territories of several municipia and older colonies in the area between the
second Triumvirate (43-33/32 BC) and the reign of Augustus (27 BC-AD 14): Sena Gallica, Ostra,
Sentinum, Auximum, Cingulum, Tuficum, Matilica, Attidium, Pausulae, Urbs Salvia, Tolentinum,
55
   The full citizenship was probably awarded as quickly as 241 BC (Bandelli 2002; Paci 2008c, p. 423; 2010b, 15-18 and
Antolini, Marengo 2010, pp. 209-215).
56
   For discussions, see Laffi 1975; Bandelli 2002 and Giorgi 2005.
57
   For Potentia and Pisaurum, see Livy XXXIX.44.10 and Vell. Pat. I.15.2; for Auximum, see again Vell. Pat. I.15.3.
58
   For the veteran allotments under the reign of the Gracchi at Ancona and Auximum, see the Liber Coloniarum (Lib. Colon.
I and II, 227.1-3; 253.1-4). For the possible foundation of the colony of Pollentia near the end of the 2nd century BC, see
Fabrini 2013b, 198-201.
59
   The attribution of municipal status to the towns of Tifernum Mataurense, Sentinum, Ancona, Asculum and Hatria in the
years surrounding the Social War is based on historical grounds. It is currently not clear when precisely the towns of Cluana
and Tolentinum acquired the status.
150                                                                                         Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




      Camerinum, Cupra Marittima and Castrum Truentinum. The Potenza valley forms no exception
      to this case, as Potentia, Ricina, Trea, Septempeda all received veterans in this period (see chapter
      4).60 In some localities, these operations took on the form of proper colonial (re-)foundations,
      such as in Ancona, where the placement of veterans from the battle of Filippi was accompanied
      by the rewarding of colonial status in 42/41 BC. The same goes for the ancient Roman colony of
      Pisaurum, which was now re-founded (Gabba 1953; 1973, pp. 459-471; Keppie 1983, p. 58; Paci
      1998d). The ancient Latin colony of Firmum – which in the meantime had become a municipium
      after the Social War – also saw its receiving of veterans from the troops of Anthony reinforced with
      a colonial deduction in the same period.61 After 31 BC – or even after 27 BC – colonial deductions
      probably also occurred at Fanum Fortunae, Falerio and Asculum (Fig. 97) (Paci 1998d, pp. 211,
      note 7; Branchesi 2007).
        These main trajectories in the processes of colonial expansion and territorial occupation in
      central Adriatic Italy offer a good framework for interpreting the rural survey material of the PVS
      (Vermeulen 2017). Indeed, along with the establishment of an elaborate road network and the
      development of a series of important administrative centres, it seems that the typical Roman pattern
      of dispersed rural settlement started to develop fully in the Potenza valley in the 2nd century BC.
      Especially in the lower valley, the installation of new farms in the coastal zone seems to be a clear
      response to the opportunities offered by the new portal town of Potentia to its hinterland, together
      with the gateway it provided to wider overseas trade networks. Emblematic for this process is
      the site of Colle Burchio (site 105), a large Late Republican wine-producing estate that started
      manufacturing late Greco-Italic wine amphorae in the first half of the 2nd century BC.62 Through
      a combination of several artefact surveys, geophysical prospection and aerial photography, we
      could identify here several buildings and amphora kilns, spreading out over an area of more than
      one hectare. An unusual high density of Roman amphora fragments (30-50/m²) – many of them
      showing signs of overfiring – and the recovery of an oven wall fragment assure the identification
      of the site as an amphora workshop (Fig. 98). These observations are in line with our 2007/2008
      excavations of another site within the territory of Potentia, near the locality of Acquabona (site
      111). Here too, kiln debris and late Greco-Italic/early Lamboglia 2 wasters hint at the production
      of wine amphorae from the mid-2nd century BC onwards (Vermeulen et al. 2006, pp. 190-191;
      Vermeulen et al. 2009; Carre et al. 2014).
        A temporal and functional connection of Colle Burchio with the nearby site of Montarice villa
      (site 101) might be suggested based on the material recovered during a grid survey in 2012. Indeed,
      this survey revealed a large concentration of building materials – mainly roof tiles and bricks –
      together with almost 80 opus spicatum tiles and a large amount of dolia and amphora fragments,
      with many of the latter displaying an identical fabric than the Colle Burchio Greco-Italics (Fig. 99).
      The absence of fine wares and the presence of a small limestone basin further stress the artisanal and
      productive character of the site. In fact, we have already suggested that the totality of the evidence
      might indicate the presence here of an agricultural production space, perhaps for the processing of
      grapes and/or olives (Vermeulen et al. 2013, p. 608). The material collected in 2012 point towards
      a phase of major activity in the Late Republican period, around the same time of the dominant
      phase of amphora production at Colle Burchio. It is thus possible that the unfolding of the colony of
      Potentia in the coastal plain in the 2nd century BC was accompanied by the installation of a series
      of production loci in its hinterland.

      2013, pp. 606-608).

                            This link between the first phases of Roman colonisation and urbanisation in the 3rd-2nd century
                         BC and the installation of wine producing facilities in the Potenza valley finds increasing support in
                         the wider central Adriatic area. At Ariminum (Rimini), for example, recent excavations have shown
                         how Greco-Italic imports were gradually replaced by local variants from the suburban workshop of
                         Cattolica during the 3rd century BC (Stoppioni 2011; 2013). Another example is the colony of Sena
                         Gallica (Senigallia), where a grape processing plant was installed around the time of its foundation
                         at the beginning of the 3rd century BC, while archaeometric evidence supports the local production
                         of Greco-Italics and Lamboglia 2 wine amphorae (Lepore et al. 2012; Van Limbergen 2014). A
                         similar scenario may be proposed for the southern colony of Hatria, where the attested production
                         of Greco-Italics and the installation of a wine estate in its inland territory (Tortoreto Muracche) in
                         the 3rd-2nd century BC can be related to the foundation of the colony around 290 BC-286 BC (Fig.
                         97) (Staffa 2002, p. 58; Van Limbergen 2011, pp. 81-82).
                            In the middle valley, the typical dispersed landscape of farmsteads and villages in the Late
                         Republican period developed mainly in function of the newly created road axes. The PVS survey
                         transect is here crossed by the important Flaminia ad Urbe per Picenum Anconam, a major sideway of
                         the Via Flaminia that connected the Apennine settlement of Prolaqueum in the upper Potenza valley
                         with the coastal town of Ancona, crossing on its way the road stations and later towns of Septempeda
                         and Trea (see chapter 4). Furthermore, aerial photography and artefact survey work has indicated
                         the existence of another artery along the Potenza river – probably linking Septempeda with Ricina
                         and Potentia – with an important junction and vicus to the north of the Monte Franco (Fig. 100) (see
                         supra). Just as in the upper valley, the materialisation of this important W-E communication hub in
                         the 2nd century BC – which was probably a continuation of a pre-existing protohistoric network –
                         offered obvious opportunities for the exploitation of the landscape (Verreyke, Vermeulen 2009, p.
                         104; Vermeulen 2012, p. 52).
                            Around the Early Imperial period, rural settlement occupation reached its most intense and
                         widespread form in all survey transects. It seems no coincidence that this is also a phase of intense
                         veteran resettlement and land distribution in the central Adriatic area (see supra). Furthermore,
                         our intra-site artefact and geophysical surveys at Potentia, Ricina and Trea have shown that the
                         Augustan-Tiberian period marked the beginning of a comprehensive urban building program in the
                         Potenza valley; and that all three towns experienced their heyday – at least structurally, but probably
                         also demographically – in the 1st and 2nd centuries AD (Vermeulen et al. 2009; Vermeulen 2014;
5. Rural settlement dynamics in the Potenza corridor between 900 BC and AD 600                                                                   153




Van Limbergen, Monsieur, Vermeulen 2017) (see chapter 4). A recent overview of all town data
in the area has shown how this evolution was not confined to the Potenza valley, but was rather a
common feature of the Early and High Imperial urban landscape in central Adriatic Italy.63
   Some comments on the type of tendril arrangement that possibly organised this dense pattern of
smaller and larger farms and estates in our study area are at hand here. Traditionally, the 200-year
period following the end of the Second Punic War has been defined by scholars as a transition from
small farms focused on self-sufficiency to large ‘villa’ estates producing for the market. A concomitant
phenomenon of this rise in villa agriculture was the introduction of the so-called slave-mode of
production. This phenomenon manifested itself on the land through the gradual displacement of
free peasants by imported slaves that were now concentrated on the estate itself (Applebaum 1963).
Consequently, this process allegedly led to the decline of small farms and independent farmers
(Carandini 1988; Morley 2001).
   However, regional field surveys in Italy have attested the different evolutions of the countryside
and it seems that the dispersed nucleated pattern manifested itself most strongly in central Tyrrhenian
Italy, but that in many other regions of Roman Italy, the rise of villa agriculture did not undermine
the survival of small farms (Patterson 2006; Marzano 2007). On the contrary, it probably even
encouraged cooperation between these different sizes of agricultural units (Marzano 2009).
However, it could have meant the decline of independent farms. In fact, regional surveys have made
it clear that in most regions both ‘villas’ and farms seem to follow the same chronological patterns
between Late Republican and Early Imperial times, suggesting that they were rather mutually
inclusive than exclusive. It has been argued that this connection between the two was of a dispersed
peripheral type; that is, organised according to larger farms that were surrounded by a cluster of
smaller peripheral farmsteads occupied by tenants (Launaro 2011, pp. 166-168, 170-177).64

                                                                                                                                  Roman vicus at
                                                                                                                                  Passo di Treia as
                                                                                                                                  revealed by the
                                                                                                                                  surveys.

63
   This is the general impression of a recent review of all textual and archaeological data for the Marche and northern Abruzzo
(Van Limbergen, Vermeulen 2017, pp. 165-203).
64
   For tenants as a source of agricultural labor in Italy, Greece, Egypt and North Africa, see Foxhall 1990.
154                                                                             Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




         The results of the PVS surveys seem to point in the direction of precisely such a trend in the
      Potenza valley. First, up until the Early and Mid-Imperial period, there are no signs in our data
      to suggest that small and medium farms ‘suffered’ from the presence of larger farms and villas.
      We should however stress that larger domains were always more pronounced in the upper valley,
      possibly as a result of the lesser availability of good land, and thus the need to exploit larger tracts
      of it in order to obtain satisfying results. Second, when rural settlement starts to decline significantly
      after the 3rd century AD, smaller sites decline more than larger ones, possibly reflecting an increasing
      concentration of land in the hands of fewer owners, with their tenants now living in archaeologically
      poorly detectable dwellings or in the main villae (Tables 12-17) (Launaro 2011, p. 171). As we shall
      see further in this section, this evolution should not necessarily be interpreted in terms of decline
      and crisis, but rather as the reorganisation of the rural landscape under different socio-economic
      conditions (Banaji 2002, p. 16).
         In terms of location in the landscape, the distribution of Roman sites in all three valley transects
      – especially in the Early Imperial period, but also in the preceding and following phases up to
      Late Antiquity – reflects a total use of the available land. In the lower valley, sites may be broadly
      subdivided into four groups. A first group is concentrated near the low beach ridges that separated
      the lower valley bottom from the sea. These sites are all located along the important coastal N-S
      road and some of them seem to have been involved in the production of amphorae (Van Limbergen,
      Monsieur, Vermeulen 2017). A second concentration is found in the immediate surroundings of
      Potentia, in the alluvial plain, mostly along the road that connects the town with the interior. A third
      group is located along the lower and higher parts of the hilly ridges that boarder the Potenza plain.
      These sites were often ideally positioned on favourable points in the landscape, such as optimal
      viewpoints with great visual control over the plain, or sun-exposed ridges and slopes that were best
      suited for intensive vine and olive growing. A good example is San Girio (site 124), a large and rich
      terraced villa situated below a good water source on a gentle plateau along a north oriented slope of
      the southern ridge of the Potenza plain (Fig. 102). Geomagnetic prospections revealed here in 2013
      the plan of a large rectangular building that was continuously occupied from Late Republican until
      Late Antique times (Vermeulen et al. 2013, pp. 611-612).
         Finally, a few sites were also identified deeper inland in the valley bottom. The well-investigated
      settlement of Casa Apis (site 121) is an interesting illustration of the situation in this part of the
      landscape. Through a combination of gridded artefact survey, aerial photography and geomorphologic
      augerings in 2012, we could identify this site as a large and rich longue durée farm. Its construction
      on a slightly higher position compared to the surrounding flat terrain because of differential
      compaction of riverbed gravel versus finer alluvial material assured protection against the recurring
      risk of inundation in the alluvial plain in Roman times (Fig. 101). Furthermore, its position on a
      former river bed made that water was available on a depth of only 4 m. These conditions were surely
      favourable then for optimally exploiting one of the most fertile areas in the lower valley, especially
      for cereal culture (Vermeulen et al. 2013, pp. 609-611).
         As was mentioned during our discussion of the Iron Age finds, the currently low number of Roman
      sites in the coastal plain is probably in part to be explained by sediment accumulation in antique and
      post-antique times (see supra). Some indications have come from a random dig near a modern farm
      house in the coastal plain that delivered a large amount of Roman Age artefacts at a depth of one to
      two m. No remains were visible on the surface (site 218). We therefore need to consider that we are
      possibly missing many sites in this area, in particular within that part of the valley floor where the
      Romans developed the 20x20 actus centuriation of Potentia (Corsi 2008; Corsi, Vermeulen 2010).
      In the middle and upper valley, the rural settlements are mostly concentrated near contact zones of
      different landscape types, such as the border zones between the valley bottom and the hill slopes,
      or other topographical features like easy slopes and plateaus with good views towards the river. To
      some extent, this is to be expected, as the rather narrow alluvial plain of the Potenza forms a less
5. Rural settlement dynamics in the Potenza corridor between 900 BC and AD 600                                              155

                                                                                                             of the Roman farm
                                                                                                             at Casa Apis (121)
                                                                                                             in the lower valley

                                                                                                             by aerial survey
                                                                                                             and gridded artefact
                                                                                                             survey.


pronounced part of the landscape here compared to the lower valley. Selection criteria seem to have
been the presence of good arable land, the availability of natural water sources and/or the vicinity
of the main road arteries (Figs. 88-89) (Vermeulen, Boullart 2001, p. 13; Vermeulen, Monsieur,
Boullart 2002, p. 58; Verreyke, Vermeulen 2009).
  The still difficult to characterise centuries covering the Mid-Imperial and Late Antique occupation
phases in the Potenza valley were marked by new socio-economic conditions in town and country.
Two trends in our rural survey evidence should be stressed: first, the apparent overall decline in
occupation density; and second, the increasing role of favourably positioned larger farms and villas
as foci of attention, especially from the 4th century AD onwards. This nucleation of settlement into
larger estates is a pattern comparable to the developments in other parts of the Italian peninsula in
this period, even if it is unlikely that the central Adriatic area ever saw the rise of proper latifundiae
156                                                                                              Dimitri Van Limbergen et al.




  Fig. 102 The site

                        such as in southern Italy (Vera 2001, p. 627; Verreyke, Vermeulen 2009, p. 114). Furthermore,
                        comparative data from both within and outside our wider study area suggest that we should see these
                        4th-6th century estates not as rich and luxurious residencies, but rather as more modest dwellings
                        that were mainly used for agricultural activities and/or industrial production (Barker 1995, p. 225).
                        We have already mentioned the example of the inland villa of Pollenza Santa Lucia (see supra),
                        but the process of giving up former living quarters for rooms with a more utilitarian character is
                        a trend noticeable on other sites in the region too (Pupilli 1996, pp. 59-60). In some cases, these
                        developments were accompanied by additional functional changes, such as the placement of burials
                        within parts of the farm or the installation of a rural church within its premises from the 5th century
                        AD onwards (Guarnieri 2004, pp. 25-26; Augenti et al. 2005, p. 19; Lewit 2005, pp. 251-254).
                        In fact, such an evolution may be supposed for the large villa of San Girio in the lower Potenza
                        valley (see supra), where the nearby church contains many late antique and early Christian spolia
                        (Vermeulen et al.; 2013, pp. 611-612).
                          Still, one should be wary not to underestimate the agro-economic vitality of this 3rd-5th century AD
                        phase in central Adriatic Italy, especially in the coastal zones. The contemporary literary evidence for
                        agricultural production and trade in the region shows the ongoing popularity and fame of Picenian
5. Rural settlement dynamics in the Potenza corridor between 900 BC and AD 600                               157




wines between the 3rd and the 7th century AD, while the export of Picenian olives is attested up
until the 3rd century AD. Recent work on press installations in the region has also demonstrated how
some of the Late Republican and Early Imperial production plants continued their activities on a
smaller scale.65 Another trend is the placing of press installations within, or in the immediate vicinity
of, urban central sectors. One example of this practice in the Potenza valley may be the Late Antique
press bed found by the PVS in the middle valley town of Trea, located close to the Early Imperial
monumental sector of the settlement (Van Limbergen 2014). The late antique quadrangular press
block recently found at the intersection of two streets during the excavations of the monumental
sector of the town of Forum Sempronii (Fossombrone) seems to be another one (Luni 2007; Luni,
Mei 2012). In fact, these installations are part of a wider phenomenon that has become known as
the ‘re-ruralisation’ of urban life in Late Antiquity; that is, the gradual reconversion of many public
sectors into artisanal areas (e.g. Augenti 2006). Based on what we know from our urban survey
work and older excavation data, similar processes may have touched the towns in the Potenza valley,
especially following the ceasing of new (monumental) building activities after the 3rd century AD
(see supra, chapter 4). Slowly but steadily, as the line between working, living and dying faded in
the countryside, urban and rural worlds merged into a landscape of transition that eventually would
offer little resilience against the far-reaching events of the 6th and 7th century AD.

   For examples and references, see Van Limbergen 2011; 2018 (forthcoming) and Van Limbergen, Taelman 2017
(forthcoming).
158                                                                                                          Patrick Monsieur




      6. The changing material culture of a valley


           6.1. Introduction
           Patrick Monsieur


           D  ue to the enormous quantities of artefacts, mostly pottery, recovered during the survey, different
              approaches in the processing of the archaeological material were followed. A database was
      set up with a basic form for the quantitative data and a detailed form for the registration of selected
      individual artefacts. This information was integrated in the main GIS-based PVS-database. Besides
      the processing and first examination of the finds during each field campaign, specific campaigns for
      the study of finds were set up to refine the classification and to work out the study of the typology
      and the fabric groups more thoroughly. At first the study of the ceramic material served to assign the
      chronology of the different occupation phases of the sites. To this purpose the chronology retrieved
      from excavation material and well-known imported pottery categories found in the region provided a
      basic background. This basic work forms the backbone of the selected diagnostic material presented
      in the finds catalogue (see chapter 8).66
        In a next phase of the project the study of the locally or regionally produced and imported pottery
      categories in the dataset was tackled. This work is still unfinished and needs further confrontation
      with pottery groups from well dated contexts in the central-Adriatic region. At the present state
      of pottery research in the Potenza valley some important results have been achieved with locally
      produced and imported amphorae, and with groups of Late Imperial fine and common wares. The
      processing and basic study of the lamps has also progressed towards a first synthesis, but their study
      needs to be further refined. This also applies to the study of black gloss material, which was initiated
      but not finished. This group of fine pottery, very important for the dating of earlier Roman contexts
      in the valley needs further analysis (including archaeometric analysis), before a first synthesis for
      the valley can be presented. A first approach of the terra sigillata and its epigraphy is presented
      here, while also the study of protohistoric materials and the Greek imports in the valley could
      recently be initiated (in particular from some central settlement sites), giving way to the first general
      observations presented in this volume. Finally, the decorative stone materials were classified and
      analysed, providing a first overview of their use in architecture in the valley, especially concerning
      the Roman urban contexts. Archaeometric studies were carried out on the amphorae (petrography
      and chemical analysis), the late imperial common wares (petrography) and the decorative stone
      materials. It is the aim to continue and publish more comprehensively these specialised studies
      and to enlarge the research on other material categories in the future in order to provide an overall
      picture of the material culture of the Potenza valley. At present, the quality of information these
      surface materials provide us about the material culture in the valley during the studied periods is still
      somewhat limited, in particular because of the dire need of good stratigraphic contexts. Therefore,
      we prefer to present here first and foremost the catalogue giving an overview of the most typical,
      diagnostic or peculiar materials that were recovered during the survey, and to bring only a synthesis
      of the aforementioned selected material categories.67
        Artefact surveys do not yield the same quality and variety of materials as do excavations. Both
      categories of finds are complementary in order to give a complete picture of the material culture
      66
         In the course of the PVS project material studies on all survey finds were undertaken at different locations in the upper
      Potenza valley (Fiuminata), the middle valley (Treia) and the lower valley (Porto Recanati) , where the finds were temporarily
      stored in deposits of the Soprintendenza of Marche. At the time of writing all finds are still stored at our main work space in
      Porto Recanati from where they will be transferred to the storage facilities of the Soprintendenza at Ancona.
      67
         The invaluable research on Late Imperial pottery in the Potenza valley by Hélène Verreyke, especially in her PhD
      dissertation (Verreyke 2007), was revised and enhanced by Francesca Carboni.
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                                          159




of a region. The identification and chronometry of the survey materials also depend on the study
of published and unpublished finds from dated contexts of excavations in the region. Of equal
importance is the contribution of specialised regional and interregional material studies.68 The
Potenza valley knew since the sixties of the last century different excavations, mostly carried out
by the Soprintendenza delle Marche. The publication of the earlier excavations under the direction
of L. Mercando, especially in Ricina and Potentia, are not always useful from a stratigraphical
point of view, but the selected material is invariably well presented and discussed (Mercando 1971;
Mercando 1974a; Mercando 1979). On the other hand the publication of the northern necropolis
of Potentia (La Pineta, Porto Recanati) is important for the chronology and identification of many
artefacts, since many tombs contained helpful dating clues (Mercando 1974b). Of crucial importance
are the more recent excavations of the Soprintendenza delle Marche and the Belgian mission. They
were undertaken in the city centre, the northern necropolis and the west gate sector of Potentia, and
on the amphora atelier of Casa Valentini/Acquabona (site 111) (Carre et al. 2014; Percossi 1990;
Percossi 1995; Percossi 2001; Percossi 2007; Percossi 2012; Vermeulen et al. 2009; Vermeulen et
al. 2011; Vermeulen & Monsieur 2012; Monsieur 2009). These excavations produced lots of finds
from dated contexts that were relevant for the comparison and identification of the (Roman) survey
material. The materials of the excavations have not been published and most are still under study.69
The use and study of the excavations reports and their related material were in particular fruitful for
the chronology and a better understanding of the early Roman colonisation in the Potenza valley.
The discovery of the city wall of Potentia, which can be dated by the ancient sources around 174
BC, was a major progress because the stratigraphical contexts that were connected to it yielded an
appreciable variety of material categories of fine wares, plain and coarse wares, unguentaria, loom-
weights and amphorae. This precious comparison material lead to a reliable chronology of a series
of contexts and their associated finds in the first half of the 2nd century BC, which could be applied
to the finds on different surveyed sites, especially in the lower valley, were the recovered material
shows in some cases a marked homogeneity (e.g. sites 101, 105, 108 and 119). The same applies to
two Augustan-early Tiberian contexts discovered near the west gate of Potentia and in a dwelling
associated with the amphora workshop of Casa Valentini/Acquabona. These contexts yielded an
interesting assemblage of plain and coarse wares, finely dated by association with terra sigillata,
ceramic epigraphy and some coins. The discovery of the same types of plain and coarse wares on
several other surveyed sites (e.g. 129 and 218) again showed a consistent congruity. The information
gathered from the publication of excavations and material studies in the neighbouring valleys were
of equal importance for the identification and the dating of the Potenza survey material. In particular
the publication of the research carried out at Ancona, Jesi, Monte Torto di Osimo, Suasa, Urbisaglia
and the Tenna valley were consulted and used for comparative study.70 Finally the Ghent team could
rely on the expertise of several members from the University of Bologna working currently on the
site of Suasa and its well-excavated stratigraphic contexts.71

160                                                                                            Wieke de Neef




        6.2. Protohistoric periods


         6.2.1. Bronze Age
         Wieke de Neef

           For the present study, material was studied from three surface sites detected in the 2001 and
      2002 surveys: Montarice, Monte dei Priori, and Monte Franco. These sites provide well-preserved
      fragments of handmade impasto pottery which allow making observations on the changing material
      culture and settlement patterns of Metal Age inhabitants in the valley. Most datable fragments found
      at the three sampled sites can be placed in the Bronze and Iron Ages, but there are indications of
      earlier activity. At Montarice, a single fragment with typical ‘scaled’ surface can be dated to the
      Chalcolithic, and a flint arrow head at the same site can be dated to the Neolithic-Bronze Age. The
      Iron Age material is discussed in the next session; here a brief overview of the Bronze Age finds will
      be presented.
           Montarice and Monte dei Priori are counterparts on both sides of the Potenza Valley, located on
      subcoastal hills overlooking both the coastline and the valley. The Monte dei Priori site consists of
      several diffuse concentrations of impasto pottery on the slopes of the hill; the hilltop itself is partly
      overbuilt and partly heavily eroded. The flat plateau of Montarice, on the other hand, is reasonably
      well preserved despite agricultural practice, and the quality of the protohistoric pottery collected
      here in 2002 is remarkable. The many datable impasto fragments indicate a gradual expansion of
      occupied space during the Bronze and Iron Age, with a strong focus on the westernmost part in the
      MBA and moving towards the east in later phases. The third studied site, Monte Franco, is situated
      further inland, at the foot of a hill overlooking river Potenza. Like Monte dei Priori, it consists of
      several low-density impasto clusters as part of a multi-period occupation.
           Of the three studied sites, Montarice has the longest stable occupation, from the Middle Bronze
      Age (MBA, c. 1700-1350 BC) to the Late Iron Age. Monte dei Priori was in use only during the
      short period of the Recent Bronze Age (RBA, the first phase of the Late Bronze Age, c. 1350-1100
      BC). Monte Franco has some decorated MBA fine ware, but most material dates to the Iron Age. In
      all three sites, the transition from Recent to Final Bronze Age (the last phase of the Late Bronze Age,
      c. 1100-950 BC) marks a significant change in the protohistoric settlement system: in Montarice
      only few pottery fragments were identified which can be dated to the FBA, while Monte dei Priori
      was abandoned altogether. There is also minimal evidence for FBA activity at Monte Franco (cat.
      1.9). Like Montarice, this site flourishes again in the subsequent Iron Age.
           Most datable artefacts from all three sites belong to table wares. At Monte dei Priori, a fragment
      of a black drinking cup with a curved upstanding grip can be dated to the RBA (cat. 1.4). Further finds
      from this site include a rim fragment of a burnished cup and part of a horned handle (ansa cornuta) of
      a cup in dark grey burnished impasto, but also coarser wares. At Monte Franco, two thin incised wall
      fragments can be dated to MBA3 (01WK60-113) and FBA (01WK60-42; both in Boullart 2006).
      The large Montarice assemblage offers the best options to study Bronze Age material culture and
      links to stylistic material groups elsewhere in the Italian peninsula. Like in the other two studied
      sites, table wares form the majority of datable material, but there are also larger shapes for cooking
      and storage. The table wares consist of drinking cups, wide bowls with shallow bases (scodelle),
      covers, and small closed shapes. Larger forms include open and closed shapes in coarse fabrics.
      Many fine ware fragments are decorated in typical ‘Apennine’ style dating to the last phase of the
      MBA (MBA3, c. 1500-1350 BC).
           The first signs for stable occupation of the Montarice hill date to the early phases of the Middle
      Bronze Age (c. 1700-1500 BC). Datable pottery fragments from MBA1-2 are found all over the
      plateau, albeit in small densities. These include fragments of large storage vessels in coarse impasto
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                161




pottery, wall fragments of coarse impasto with incised decorations, two grip fragments with ‘ears’
and fragments of fine impasto ware. The fine ware includes a hemispherical bowl (olla a calotta),
a cup with accentuated shoulders (carena) and incised decoration, and an incised, out turning
rim of a small cup. Most material datable to the advanced phase of the MBA was collected in the
NW-corner of the plateau in units K140 and K144, but fragments also occur in the central part of
the hill and on the southern slope. Most of these sherds are wall and rim fragments with typical
incised ‘appenninico’ decorations (cat. 1.6), some of them with remains of crushed bone filling.
Remarkable are the large handle fragments with elaborate decorations; these include the parts of
two upstanding handgrips with parallels in excavated contexts in the Adriatic inland, including S.
Paolina di Filottrano, Monte Ignino and Moscosi di Cingoli (cat. 1.13; Percossi et al. 2006, p. 122,
fig. 77). Most MBA3 decorated wall fragments show parallels with the inland site of La Starza
and the ‘Medio Adriatico’ cultural group (Cocchi Genick et al. 1995, p. 90; Macchiarola 1987),
but there are also curvilinear motifs which can be associated with the Terramare cultural groups in
northern Italy (cat. 1.8; Bernabò Brea et al. 1997).
    The NW corner of Montarice is also a focus during the subsequent Recent Bronze Age (c. 1350-
1100 BC), but elevated densities of finds from this period are also recorded in the South-central
part of the plateau. Datable fragments include typical wide bowls with triangular grips (Damiani’s
pottery family 1, type 1; 2010, pp. 127-130; cat. 1.12), horned grips with pointed and ‘snail’-type
(a lumaca; cat. 1.11) ends, broad flat band handles (Damiani 2010, p. 101) and upstanding handles.
Three fragmentary sherds may represent typical bird-shaped handles. An impasto wall fragment of
a sieve spout with seven perforations (published in Percossi et al. 2006, p. 123, fig. 79) has a RBA
parallel in Etruria (Zanini 1997, p. 74, fig. 32). Associations with northern Italian cultural groups can
be seen in the curved plastic decorations on a fragment of a globular vessel (cat. 1.5).
    The Final Bronze Age sees a marked decline in diagnostic pottery. Most fragments were collected
in the central-eastern part of the plateau, which indicates the abandonment of the NW-corner as the
favoured settlement focus. The FBA pottery includes two wall fragments with ‘solcature’ groove
decorations with straight and triangular motifs, one cup with accentuated shoulder and straight
grooves, a decorated flattened rim and a bowl with an accentuated in-standing lip (with a parallel at
Torre Mordillo; Vagnetti & Trucco 2001, p. 91, type 20).
    Special finds which are not attributable to specific Bronze Age sub-phases include a bone disc
bead with five circle decorations (cat. 1.29). Two elaborately decorated handle fragments probably
date to the Late Bronze Age (cat. 1.14). So far, no Bronze Age Aegean imports or Italo-Mycenaean
wares have been identified in the Montarice assemblage.
    The three studied sites demonstrate different aspects of Bronze Age settlement in the Potenza
Valley. Monte dei Priori and Monte Franco yielded evidence of small-scale occupation, while
Montarice is a large central settlement in a notable location, and occupied over a long period. The
surface finds at the latter site suggest a firm embedding in subsequent Central-Adriatic material
culture groups, supplemented with occasional traces of contacts with areas further away. Around
1100 BC, a profound settlement change occurred – at least for the duration of the FBA. Future study
of other Bronze Age sites in the Potenza Valley may show if this caesura is typical for the whole
research area, and shed more light on the impact of this event on regional land use patterns.
162                                                                                                                  Sara Morsiani




           6.2.2. Iron Age: Picene culture
           Sara Morsiani

          La cultura materiale picena è stata oggetto di ritrovamenti fin dal primo anno di ricognizioni
      nella valle del Potenza.72 Sono due i siti principali che hanno restituito oggetti circoscrivibili a un
      orizzonte cronologico di fine VII-V sec. a.C.; si tratta di Monte Franco, situato su un promontorio
      nella media valle73, e di Montarice, nella bassa valle74, interpretati entrambi come contesti abitativi.
          I reperti riferibili all’età del Ferro comprendono essenzialmente vasellame in impasto
      buccheroide e ceramica d’impasto. Il primo elemento che va evidenziato è la mancanza totale di vere
      e proprie importazioni di oggetti dall’Etruria, sostituiti nella valle del Potenza da prodotti locali, che
      richiamano nelle forme e nelle caratteristiche tecniche il bucchero etrusco. Tale dato parrebbe essere
      indice della presenza di comunità locali particolarmente sviluppate, sia dal punto di vista artigianale,
      poiché in grado di recepire modelli altrui, selezionarli e renderli propri mediante il proprio sapere
      tecnologico, sia soprattutto dal punto di vista culturale. L’adozione, infatti, di forme allogene legate
      al rituale del banchetto e a quello funerario implica la scelta di aderire a determinati comportamenti
      e standard sociali, denotando un livello di complessità notevole per queste comunità picene.
          Le argille con cui vengono realizzati i recipienti in impasto buccheroide sono di colore grigio molto
      scuro, a volte tendente al marrone, spesso ricche di inclusi micacei, dure e con la superficie esterna
      lisciata. Le forme maggiormente attestate sono gli attingitoi a pancia ovoide con orlo svasato assottigliato
      e fondo a disco con fascia esterna rigonfia, assimilabili al tipo Rasmussen 1b75 (cat. 1.23) la cui cronologia
      si colloca tra l’ultimo quarto del VII e il terzo quarto del VI sec. a.C. Vi sono anche alcuni esempi di forme
      aperte, come ciotole con orlo ingrossato all’esterno76 (cat. 1.21), piatti con orlo indistinto77 e tazze con orlo
      svasato a corpo carenato, con decorazione a piccole tacche parallele78 (cat. 1.20). Non mancano, infine,
      esemplari che conservano una decorazione impressa a cerchi concentrici (cat. 1.19; cat. 1.22); nel primo
      caso è possibile identificare la forma come un calice su alto piede a stelo riferibile al tipo Rasmussen 2d.79
          Il panorama che emerge dallo studio della ceramica d’impasto è ancora più variegato e
      complesso. Sono chiaramente ben rappresentati i pocula e le olle con orlo rientrante e quattro
      piccole prese applicate poco sotto l’imboccatura (cat. 1.15), recipienti tipici della cultura picena,
      abbondantemente documentati peraltro in tutta la regione. I frammenti trovati nella valle del Potenza,
      infatti, si confrontano specificatamente con materiali di Numana, in tombe attribuite al Piceno IV
      B80 e al Piceno V81, di Sirolo82, di Serra S. Quirico83, di Camerano84, di Ripatransone85 e di Ascoli
      72

      Vermeulen e Patrick Monsieur, per aver offerto la possibilità di studiare una parte dei materiali dell’età del Ferro, provenienti
      dai siti di Montarice e Monte Franco.

6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                                    163




Piceno86. Tutte queste attestazioni concordano per una datazione tra il Piceno IV e l’inizio del Piceno
V, secondo la periodizzazione di Lollini, ovvero tra il VI sec. a.C. e la metà del successivo.
    A una fase leggermente precedente, dalla seconda metà del VII sec. a.C., si datano invece alcune
forme sempre prodotte in ceramica d’impasto ma su modelli di derivazione tirrenica, come le olle ovoidi
con orlo svasato a profilo arrotondato (cat. 1.17), che trovano confronti a Tarquinia87 e a Gravisca88, ma
che sono già documentate in area marchigiana, ad esempio a Suasa, nei livelli preromani89, e ad Ascoli
Piceno, in riporti eterogenei di fine IV-III sec. a.C.90, oltre che nella necropoli picena di Campovalano.91
Di derivazione da modelli laziali di VII sec. a.C., sono poi le olle con orlo verticale appiattito
superiormente92 (cat. 1.18) e le ciotole emisferiche con orlo verticale o leggermente rientrante93 (cat.
1.16), quest’ultime dal VI sec. a.C. documentate anche nella necropoli picena di Fossa.94
    Una categoria a parte è rappresentata dai dolia, non particolarmente numerosi (ne sono
stati rinvenuti alcuni frammenti, tutti a Montarice) ma altamente indicativi. Essi, attestandosi
unicamente in contesti di abitato, possono innanzitutto confermare l’interpretazione del sito in
questione. Inoltre, è indubbio il loro contributo alla definizione cronologica in quanto possono
uniformemente essere datati nella prima metà del VI sec. a.C. A Montarice ne sono testimoniate
due varianti: una con orlo a tesa pendente ed estremità assottigliata o ingrossata95 (cat. 1.25) e
l’altra con orlo a tesa rialzata appiattita internamente, tipica dell’età arcaica e attestata simile ad
esempio a Tarquinia96 (cat. 1.26).
    Da Montarice provengono anche due frammenti di rocchetti da telaio, a sezione cilindrica, che
conservano la parte terminale ingrossata con l’estremità ripiegata verso il basso (cat. 1.24). Questi
reperti vanno riferiti al tipo 3 di Ripatransone, datato sempre nell’ambito del VI sec. a.C.97 e trovano
confronti con materiale del Colle dei Cappuccini di Ancona.98
    Completano il quadro dei rinvenimenti piceni alcuni laterizi ascrivibili con certezza all’età
preromana. Il criterio distintivo si basa sull’argilla, che si presenta ricorrentemente di colore arancio-
rosso o meno di frequente arancio-marrone, con il nucleo grigio più o meno scuro e più o meno
poroso; l’impasto è ricco di inclusi quarzosi e calcarei di varie dimensioni.99 Il caratteristico doppio
colore è il risultato della tecnica di cottura, che non consentiva un’uniforme ossidazione della matrice
argillosa. Nella valle del Potenza si verifica la stessa situazione già evidenziata per il territorio
fermano, con una sproporzione tra coppi e tegole, che, a differenza di quanto accade in Etruria, sono
attestate in numero molto inferiore. Questo dato è stato spiegato come una conseguenza del peso
e delle dimensioni dei laterizi piceni: i coppi risultano, infatti, molto più grandi e pesanti rispetto
alle tegole (peraltro meno riconoscibili in mancanza dell’aletta), riuscendo quindi a sopravvivere
maggiormente alle condizioni atmosferiche e all’azione dell’uomo.100
    I coppi della valle del Potenza sono tutti a profilo poco convesso, con estremità appiattita o
ingrossata; le argille sono sempre di colore marrone-arancio all’esterno e grigio scuro all’interno,

      con superficie non ingubbiata e corpo ceramico poroso (cat. 1.28). Secondo la recente tipologia
      elaborata da Ciuccarelli sulla base dei materiali, anch’essi da ricognizione, dell’ager Firmanus,
      rientrano nel gruppo B2 - II1.101 Gli spessori variano tra 1,8 e 2,5 cm, inserendo i nostri coppi tra
      quelli di modulo medio e grande.102 Le tegole, invece, rientrano nella tipologia ad angolo retto, con
      aletta alta tra i 4,2 e i 4,7 cm (cat. 127). La datazione per i laterizi piceni è fissata a partire almeno dal
      tardo VI sec. a.C. fino a tutto il V e forse il IV sec. a.C.103, sulla base dei pochi contesti di abitato editi
      con edilizia non deperibile. In ambito regionale, sono istituibili confronti con Moscosi di Cingoli104,
      Matelica105 e Pesaro106, oltre che con il già citato territorio di Fermo.107
          Questa, pur sommaria, disamina dei materiali piceni rinvenuti nella valle del Potenza lascia
      supporre la presenza, perlomeno a Montarice e a Monte Franco, di comunità piuttosto complesse
      e vivaci culturalmente, interessate a importare nel proprio territorio non solo manufatti ma anche
      forme e modelli, che furono alla base di un florido artigianato locale tra la fine del VII e il V sec.
      a.C. Si tratta di un arco temporale che vide proprio il proliferare di insediamenti nell’intera vallata,
      indice di un’importante esplosione demografica che interessò il popolo piceno specialmente nel
      VI sec. a.C.108 Solo il proseguo degli studi porterà al riconoscimento e all’analisi dettagliata di un
      numero sempre maggiore di manufatti ceramici dell’età del Ferro in tutta la regione, il che consentirà
      finalmente di ampliare le conoscenze sulla cultura materiale picena, nelle sue varie sfaccettature.


            6.2.3. Greek imports
            Anna Gamberini

          Nei rapporti preliminari sul Potenza Valley Project è stata rilevata più volte la presenza di
      frammenti ceramici che sono stati identificati come prodotti greci o di tradizione greca, rinvenuti in
      particolare a Monte Franco e a Montarice.
          Nel sito di Monte Franco, dal quale provengono anche pochi frammenti a vernice nera assegnati
      ad una non meglio definibile produzione greca, sono stati rinvenuti in particolare alcuni frammenti
      in ceramica suddipinta con motivi geometrici, comprendenti anche un’ansa di oinochoe di probabile
      origine sud italica riferita al VII-metà VI secolo a.C.109
          Più numerosi sono i rinvenimenti da Montarice, alcuni dei quali ho avuto l’opportunità di
      visionare in occasione della revisione generale funzionale alla presente pubblicazione110. Si tratta
      principalmente di frammenti di vasi figurati e a vernice nera, alcuni dei quali sono compresi nei
      rapporti preliminari menzionati.
          Relativamente ai vasi figurati, sembra assegnabile alla produzione laconica (senza però poterne
      escludere la provenienza attica) un frammento di kylix che conserva una piccola porzione del tratto
      inferiore della parete, decorata con linee concentriche rosse e nere e tratti radiali, databile nell’ambito
      della seconda metà del VI secolo (cat. 1.36) 111 e inquadrabile dunque come una delle più antiche

attestazioni di ceramica greca nella vallata e nel Piceno in generale.112 Altri tre frammenti figurati sono
invece caratterizzati da peculiarità tecniche compatibili con quelle della produzione attica, per quanto il
loro stato di conservazione non ne consenta l’esatta identificazione. Nel primo caso (cat. 1.41) si tratta di
un frammento di ansa di cratere a colonnette che conserva la sommità, decorata da una doppia fila di punti
separati da una linea retta parallela al bordo, e parte della superficie verticale esterna, decorata da quattro
linee verticali parallele. Trattandosi di decorazioni secondarie, non è possibile proporre un’attribuzione
più precisa dell’esemplare in questione, pur segnalando la presenza di crateri a colonnette datati fra il
secondo e il terzo quarto del V secolo a.C. in diversi siti marchigiani113. Un secondo esemplare (cat.
1.40) è relativo ad un orlo a tesa inclinata decorato superiormente da una fila di ovoli inquadrata da
linee rette. Dal momento tuttavia che tale motivo è ampiamente utilizzato nella decorazione dei bordi di
diverse forme vascolari e che le dimensioni del pezzo non consentono di determinarne il diametro, non
è possibile proporne un’identificazione morfologica. A puro titolo esemplificativo si segnala la presenza
di vasi a figure rosse con bordi così decorati a Sirolo (dinos e kalpis)114, Numana (kalpis)115, Moscano di
Fabriano (cratere a calice).116 Del terzo esemplare, infine, è possibile solamente indicare la pertinenza a
un vaso di forma chiusa realizzato con la tecnica a figure rosse.
    I vasi a vernice nera, sebbene anch’essi limitati a pochi esemplari prevalentemente riferibili
a skyphoi, sono comunque più numerosi di quelli figurati e si datano nell’ambito del V-IV secolo
a.C. È probabilmente da riferire al V secolo un frammento di fondo bifido, rigonfio nella sua parte
superiore e con base risparmiata, la cui foggia particolare è compatibile con quella del piede di una
Acrocup117. Nell’ambito dello stesso secolo si data anche un frammento di orlo arrotondato rientrante,
compatibile con quello di uno skyphos di tipo B o di tipo Corinzio.118 A quest’ultimo tipo si riferisce
senz’altro anche un frammento di fondo, mancante del piede, morfologicamente avvicinabile ad
esemplari ateniesi della seconda metà del IV secolo.119 Sono invece riferibili allo skyphos attico tipo
A diversi frammenti, comprendenti almeno un fondo e due orli. Il fondo (cat. 1.38)120, caratterizzato
da un piede ad anello di foggia comune nel tipo121, è decorato nella sua superficie esterna, risparmiata,
da un punto e un cerchio suddipinti al centro e da un altro cerchio a lambire il piede stesso. Tanto la
forma quanto la decorazione, anch’essa comune, rimandano ad esemplari ateniesi dei primi decenni
del V secolo.122 Può forse riferirsi allo stesso tipo anche un altro fondo (cat. 1.39), caratterizzato
anch’esso da un motivo suddipinto nella sua superficie esterna (due cerchi concentrici attorno al
centro, mancante) ma privo del cerchio in prossimità del piede che due possibili confronti dall’agorà
ateniese daterebbero nell’ambito del IV secolo.123 Gli orli, rappresentati da frammenti molto piccoli
(cat. 1.37), sono entrambi leggermente svasati e avvicinabili ad esemplari dall’agorà di Atene
datati agli inizi-primo quarto del IV secolo a.C.124 Si segnala da ultimo un’ansa verticale a sezione

          Meritano poi di essere menzionati alcuni frammenti non propriamente di produzione greca
      ma ispirati a tale tradizione. Sono stati riferiti alla produzione alto-adriatica alcuni frammenti
      con impasto chiaro e caratterizzati da suddipinture rosse o brune, già editi in passato.125 Ad essi
      può essere aggiunto almeno un fondo di skyphos morfologicamente affine a quelli attici tipo A
      ma caratterizzato da un impasto chiaro e poroso e da una vernice bruna diluita, con una fascia
      risparmiata al di sopra del piede. È proprio questa caratteristica a suggerirne la provenienza alto-
      adriatica, e la datazione nell’ambito della metà-seconda metà del IV secolo sulla base del confronto
      con esemplari analoghi da Numana-Sirolo.126 Si segnala infine un frammento di vaso di forma
      chiusa caratterizzato da una suddipintura bruna (una linea orizzontale continua e una spezzata,
      parallele), per la quale non è stato individuato tuttavia un confronto ma che pare con certezza da
      interpretare come importazione.127
          Si segnalano infine alcuni esemplari caratterizzati da impasto chiaro, acromi o con decorazione
      suddipinta. Si tratta di hydriae (o comunque vasi per liquidi: cat. 1.30, cat. 1.31, cat. 1.32) e stamnoi
      (cat. 1.33) per i quali, in assenza di confronti puntuali con esemplari da altri siti, resta incerta la
      provenienza (magno greca? locale/regionale?), così come la datazione.
          Questa veloce panoramica dei rinvenimenti da Montarice e Monte Franco, situati rispettivamente
      nella bassa e nella media valle, conferma dunque i ben noti contatti della vallata stessa con il mondo
      greco fra la fine del VI e il IV secolo a.C.128


            6.3. Roman period: Republican, Imperial, Late Imperial

             6.3.1. Fine wares

              6.3.1.1. Italian terra sigillata
              Federico Biondani

           Fra le sigillate prodotte nella prima età imperiale, quella che di gran lunga predomina fra i
      materiali trovati nelle ricognizione della valle del Potenza è la sigillata italica. Poche e concentrate
      nell’area di Potentia sono le sigillate orientali A e B129.
           Nell’ambito della sigillata italica prevale nettamente la produzione centro-italica, in particolare
      quella aretina, mentre molto limitata pare la presenza di quella nord-italica. Sulla base dell’esame
      macroscopico sembra invece da escludere la presenza di prodotti di fabbricazione centro-meridionale,
      la cui presenza nel Picenum è stata invece accertata fra i materiali trovati in recenti ricerche di
      superficie condotte nell’ager Firmanus130.
      125
          Vermeulen et al. 2005, Figg. 31-32, p. 52, con bibliografia.
      126
          Esemplari a figure rosse da Numana-Sirolo, in contesto funerario (Berti, Bonomi, Landolfi 1996 nr. 07.01, t. 24, p. 99:
      metà IV sec. a.C.) e da Numana (Ibid. nr. 11.00, p. 103: seconda metà IV sec. a.C.).

          Le analisi archeometriche compiute sulla sigillata raccolta nelle ricognizioni dell’Università di Pisa hanno evidenziato

      esemplari con corpi ceramici vulcanici, prodotti in Italia centro-meridionale, in particolare in area campana (Menchelli,
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                                      167




Rimane poi incerto se qualcuno dei prodotti genericamente centro-italici possa essere di origine
marchigiana. Al momento mancano indagini archeometriche che provino con certezza una
produzione regionale, così come mancano dati certi sulla presenza di fornaci. In proposito va
ricordato il ritrovamento di “un sigillo a forma di planta pedis” presso Recanati (Percossi Serenelli
1985, p. 125; p. 133: sito n. 19) e il ritrovamento, nel suburbio di Septempeda, di un complesso
artigianale “con una serie di fornaci per la cottura di vasi in terra sigillata”131.
      Il repertorio morfologico, quale è ricavabile da un centinaio di frammenti diagnostici,
comprende un discreto numero di tipi (piatti, coppe e qualche calice), che si collocano in un arco
cronologico che va dalle prime fasi della produzione aretina sino a fine I-inizi II secolo d.C.132
      Un arrivo precoce della sigillata centro-italica è testimoniato dalla presenza di alcune tra le
forme più antiche di questa produzione, come il piatto con parete convessa e orlo distinto Conspectus
5 e la coppa troncoconica Conspectus 8 (cat. 2.44), e inoltre dalla presenza di tre bolli radiali che,
rispetto al totale dei bolli ritrovati (in tutto 10), costituiscono una percentuale cospicua: due sono
attribuibili ai ceramisti aretini C. V( ) (40-20 a.C.) (cat. 2.48) e L. Tettius Samia (probabilmente di
poco successivo)(cat. 2.46); del terzo si conserva solo il bordo (cat. 2.51).
      Molto più numerosa e varia morfologicamente è la documentazione che si colloca fra la piena
età augustea e l’età tiberiana. Tra i piatti, sono presenti con poche unità i tipi Conspectus 4 (cat. 2.16)
e Conspectus 21 (prodotto, quest’ultimo, fino alla metà del I sec. d.C.)(cat. 2.26, cat. 2.27); più diffusi
invece sono i piatti Conspectus 12 (cat. 2.17, cat. 2.18, cat. 2.37) e Conspectus 18 (cat. 2.19, cat. 2.20,
cat. 2.21). Le coppe sono documentate da pochi frammenti attribuibili ai tipi Conspectus 13 (cat. 2.36),
Conspectus 14, forse Conspectus 9 e Conspectus 22 (tipo che perdura per tutto il I sec.)(cat. 2.34).
      In questo periodo si collocano anche quattro bolli entro cartiglio rettangolare posti al centro del
fondo: due appartengono ai noti ceramisti aretini A. Vibius Scrofula (schiavo Diomedes) attivo fra
il 20 e il 5 a.C. (cat. 2.49) e C. Umbricius Philologus, la cui produzione si colloca posteriormente
al 15 a.C. (cat. 2.52); un altro, che conserva le lettere L•M[?], potrebbe riferirsi a varie officine non
localizzabili o centro-italiche(cat. 2.45); del quarto, infine, si conserva solo il bordo (cat. 2.50).
      I bolli e l’esame ottico dei reperti fanno ritenere che in questo periodo continui a predominare
la sigillata centro-italica; tuttavia fa la sua comparsa anche quella nord-italica: dall’area padana
arrivano prodotti decorati come il vaso a forma di calice Mazzeo 15D/Magdalensberg 3 (età
augustea) (cat. 2.42) e coppe tipo Sarius (cat. 2.38, cat. 2.41, cat. 2.55). Queste ultime, prodotte
anche posteriormente all’età augustea fino ad epoca flavia, sono documentate da un discreto numero
di frammenti (orli, anse e soprattutto pareti decorate). Probabilmente arriva anche vasellame liscio:
nord-italici sembrano infatti alcuni impasti di piatti Conspectus 18.
      Sono infine documenti alcuni tipi che si diffondono fra la piena età augustea e l’età tiberiana
e che perdurano fino alla metà o anche per tutto il I sec. d.C. (se non oltre): fra i piatti il più
documentato è il tipo Conspectus 20 (cat. 2.24, cat. 2.43); meno testimoniati sono il piatto carenato
Conspectus 3.1 ed il piatto Conspectus 6.2. Meglio documentate sono le coppe, rappresentate dai
tipi carenati Conspectus 26 e forse 27, dal tipo emisferico Conspectus 36.4 (cat. 2.32) e soprattutto
dal tipo emisferico con orlo distinto Conspectus 37 e da quello con orlo a listello Conspectus 34
(cat. 2.30). Quest’ultimo, che è il tipo in assoluto più rappresentato, è attestato anche da esemplari
che mostrano caratteristiche tecniche (argilla beige-arancione con inclusi micacei e calcitici; vernice
di qualità scadente arancione o rossastra) che si avvicinano alla cosiddetta sigillata medioadriatica,
produzione caratteristica della media e tarda età imperiale, segno che il tipo continua ad essere
prodotto, presumibilmente in officine regionali, anche oltre il I sec. d.C.

Cerbone 2012, p. 10; Menchelli 2012, p. 78).
