             Fra i prodotti decorati va ricordato un frammento di parete attribuibile presumibilmente
      al calice Conspectus R9 probabilmente aretino (cat. 2.35), la cui produzione ha inizio in
      età tiberiana.
             In questa fase ancora predominanti sono i prodotti centro-italici, tuttavia paiono aumentare
      le importazioni dal Nord Italia. Oltre a qualche piatto Conspectus 20 (cat. 2.43) con impasti che
      si ritengono nord-italici, sembra significativo che dei tre bolli in planta pedis post-augustei trovati
      nelle ricognizioni due appartengano a fabbricanti nord-italici: si tratta di Albanus (1^ metà I sec.
      d.C.)(cat. 2.53) e di T. Turius (30-50 d.C.)(cat. 2.47), i cui marchi sono diffusi soprattutto nell’Italia
      nord-orientale. Al ceramista aretino C. Amurius (o Camurius), attivo fra il 30 e il 70 d.C. (cat. 2.54),
      appartiene invece il terzo bollo.
             Un aumento delle importazioni dall’area padana nel corso del I sec. d.C. è anche quanto
      suggerisce la documentazione epigrafica già nota nella valle del Potenza (Mercando 1971, p. 411;
      Mercando 1974a, passim; Mercando 1979, passim; Giuliodori 1984; Giuliodori 1986; Landolfi
      1987, p. 408; Vermeulen et al. 2011, p. 201). Pare anche significativo che tutti i bolli nord-italici
      provengano dalla zona costiera, segno della scarsa penetrazione dei prodotti padani nelle aree interne,
      dove comunque arrivano, come testimonia la presenza di vasellame decorato a Villa Potenza/Helvia
      Ricina (cfr. infra).
             Pochi sono gli esemplari decorati, ad appliques o a matrice. Fra le appliques, oltre a
      decorazioni molto comuni come maschere (cat. 2.28, cat. 2.29), palmette (cat. 2.25) e doppia
      spirale (cat. 2.26, cat. 2.23, cat. 2.31, cat. 2.33), è testimoniato un motivo meno diffuso costituito
      da un cordoncino unito con doppie volute (per la frattura non è chiara la sua lunghezza) su coppa
      Conspectus 36.4 (cat. 2.32): si tratta di una decorazione ben documentata nella produzione di
      Scoppieto su coppe Conspectus 36.4a-b (Bergamini 2016, pp. 307-308, motivo O7: in particolare
      cfr. O7d); sempre su coppe Conspectus 36.4 lo si trova anche ad Aquino (Bellardi 2006, p. 46;
      p. 63, tipo 35; p. 105, n.1; tav. I, 1; tav. XI, 1Goud. 21bf) e sul Monte Iato (Hedinger 1999, pp.
      102-103, A2; Taf. 131, A2).
             Fra i pochi esemplari con decorazione a matrice sono testimoniati sia esemplari aretini (o
      centro-italici) sia esemplari nord-italici. Fra i primi, oltre a frammenti molto piccoli con decorazioni
      vegetali (Fig. 26), si segnalano:
      - il già citato esemplare attribuibile probabilmente al calice Conspectus R9, di cui si conservano una
      fascia decorata a rotella ed un fregio di ovuli (cat. 2.35);
      - un frammento di parete che conserva due rosette con molti petali (paiono 14 in quella conservata
      per intero) e bottoncino centrale, le quali probabilmente facevano parte di un fregio (cat. 2.59).
      Una decorazione simile è documentata in una coppa attribuita dubitativamente a Rasinius trovata
      ad Urbisaglia (Fabrini 2000, p. 127; Giuliodori et al. 2007, p. 442, fig. 14). Rosette analoghe
      tuttavia compaiono anche nel repertorio decorativo delle officine degli Anni (Dragendorff,
      Watzinger 1948, p. 154, Abb. 23, 2-3; Taf. 29, 468; Taf. 30, 463 ecc.; Brown 1968, pl. XVII, 68)
      o dei Perenni (Dragendorff, Watzinger 1948, Taf. 10, 87; Chase 1975, pl. IV-V, n. 2; pl. VI, n.
      3; p. XVI, 67);
      - una fila di foglie a punta stilizzate (al di sopra si intravede un’altra fila analoga) (cat. 2.40): un
      motivo simile compare fra i prodotti decorati dell’officina di Rasinius (cfr. Dragendorff, Watzinger
      1948, p. 217, n. 430; Taf. 29, 430).
             Rispetto ai frammenti decorati centro-italici ben rappresentati percentualmente sono quelli
      riconducibili alla produzione nord-italica, in particolare alle coppe Sarius. Fra i motivi documentati,
      oltre a fregi di ovuli (cat. 2.56) o di fogliette (cat. 2.39) e ad elementi vegetali (fra cui una foglia
      d’acanto)(cat. 2.55), si segnala:
      - un frammento di parete attribuibile ad una coppa Sarius (cat. 2.41) in cui, al di sotto di una linea
      orizzontale in rilievo, si riconoscono una capsula sferoidale costolata frammentaria con occhielli
      sommitali (il motivo è definito anche “capsula di papavero”, “melograno” o “grosso bocciolo
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                                        169




rigonfio”)133 ed un fascio di tre linee parallele a rilievo. Il motivo della capsula è documentato
soprattutto in area altoadriatica; si è ipotizzato possa essere attribuito all’officina di Sipa (Negrelli
1998, p. 228), in quanto l’unico esemplare marcato con capsula, trovato ad Aquileia, porta il nome
di questo figulo (Scotti Maselli 1972, c. 8, fig. 3). In area marchigiana è documentato a Pesaro nello
scavo dell’ex Farmacia Boscia (Bartolini 2008, p. 125, fig. 9, 66).
      In conclusione i dati che emergono dalle testimonianze emerse nelle recenti ricognizioni di
superficie della valle del Potenza sono largamente in linea con la documentazione già nota della
maggior parte dei centri delle Marche.134
      Anzitutto, come in tutta la regione, risulta confermata la precoce presenza di prodotti centro-
italici (in particolare aretini); in proposito si ricorda che con il territorio toscano le Marche sono
strettamente legate anche in epoca tardorepubblicana, come testimoniano, per esempio, le molte
importazioni di ceramica a vernice nera (Mazzeo Saracino 1991, p. 55).
      Da Arezzo e in generale dall’area centro-italica tirrenica i materiali arrivavano in regione
utilizzando la Flaminia e i diverticoli che da questa conducevano nelle varie valli. La valle del
Potenza era collogata alla strada consolare da una via che si staccava dalla Flaminia all’altezza di
Nocera Umbra.
Si nota poi una grande concentrazione di attestazioni fra la piena età augustea e l’età tiberiana,
come si riscontra in generale nel Picenum, in centri come Urbs Salvia, Cupra Maritima ed Asculum
(Cicala 2008, p. 83: ivi bibl.).
      La netta prevalenza del vasellame liscio (talvolta decorato con appliques) rispetto al vasellame
decorato a matrice è un altro dato che accomuna la documentazione presa in esame in questo
contributo con quella di tutto il territorio marchigiano (Mazzeo Saracino 1991, pp. 55, 59-60;
Mazzeo Saracino 1992, p. 71).
      Altro elemento che caratterizza la valle del Potenza, così come tutte le Marche, è la larga
prevalenza della sigillata di produzione centro-italica rispetto a quella di produzione nord-italica,
presente quest’ultima soprattutto con materiale decorato. In area marchigiana tale prevalenza è netta
nelle zone interne, mentre invece maggiori presenze di sigillata padana si riscontrano lungo la fascia
costiera (Mazzeo Saracino 1991, pp. 59-60; Mazzeo Saracino 1992, p. 72: ivi bibl.), dove pure
la documentazione appare piuttosto variegata: se nello scavo dell’ex Farmacia Boscia a Pesaro la
sigillata italica è per la massima parte di produzione aretina (con forme anche precoci) (Bartolini
2008, pp. 96-97), invece a Cupra Maritima, fra i bolli trovati durante ricognizioni di superficie
degli anni Novanta del secolo scorso, prevalgono quelli nord-italici 135. Anche nella necropoli di
Portorecanati i bolli di cui sia possibile accertare con sicurezza l’origine sono tutti nord-italici. Si
tratta però di solo tre esemplari: quello più antico è fra i materiali sporadici, mentre gli altri due
provengono da tombe che si datano alla fine del I sec. d.C. (Mercando 1974a, passim), epoca in
cui anche fra il materiale qui preso in esame si riscontra un aumento delle presenze nord-italiche.
All’interno la sigillata italica è meno numerosa, ma non assente come testimonia la presenza di
coppe Sarius e bicchieri Aco a Villa Potenza (Mercando 1971, p. 410, fig. 12, 30; Mercando 1972,
pp. 205-206, nn. 5a-5b). A proposito del materiale di provenienza padana, va infine sottolineato
che sia per i bolli sia per le decorazioni si conferma lo stretto legame con l’Italia nord-orientale,
testimoniato non solo dai ritrovamenti di sigillata ma anche da altre classi di materiali come i bolli
laterizi (Fortini 1990, p. 65).

    Cfr. Schindler-Kaudelka 1980, p. 198, 57/19 e p. 204, 59/134; Taf. 57/109 e 59/134; Taf. 92, Punzen T23 e T25: frammenti

            6.3.1.2. African Red Slip ware
            Francesca Carboni

            The types of African Slip ware found in the Potenza valley span the full chronological range
      from the 2nd to the 7th century AD, being the most widely represented class within the late antique
      fine table wares collected in all the surveyed sites (Fig. 103). To identify the different types we used
      the essential volumes of J.W. Hayes (Hayes 1972) and M. Bonifay (Bonifay 2004).
            The framework of imports of African slip wares, valid for the Potenza Valley, concurs with
      what is currently known for the middle Adriatic area on the whole. If we consider in fact finds
      from sites and areas as Forum Sempronii136, Suasa137, Monte Torto138, Urbs Salvia139 and the Ager
      Firmanus140, we see that the largest flux of imports occurred in the 4th and 5th centuries AD.
            It is significant that the earliest examples of African red slip ware attested in the valley, and
      dated from the 2nd century AD, have only been collected in the area of the coastal colony Potentia
      and to a lesser extent also at the inland towns of Trea and Ricina. They consist of Hayes type
      8A and 9A bowls in production A1141, followed, from the first and second half of the 3rd century,

                         late variant within the production A. It is compelling that these early productions are still completely
                         absent on the rural sites in the valley.
                               From the 3rd century dates the beginning of imports in production C. They consist of the bowls
                         Hayes forms 45, 45B, 46, 53 and the dishes Hayes 49 and 57, that are also only attested in Potentia,
                         and in the widely diffused Hayes 50A type, the earlier examples of which were produced in C2 and
                         C3 fabrics.
                               Within the Potenza valley, types in production D are predominant (Fig. 104), which illustrates
                         that the climax of African red slip imports occurred in the 4th and first half of the 5th century AD.
                         During the apex of African imports in the Adriatic, the African red slip types were also present in
                         the rural areas at the foot of the Apennine hills, although in very limited quantities.142 Production D
                         fragments indeed account for over 80% of the African red slip ware finds on the rural sites. However,
                         the number of imports declines drastically towards the inland zones of the valley. Of all African red
                         slip pottery from rural sites in the Potenza valley, 65.31% was collected in the lower valley, while
                         respectively 22.45% and 12.24% were found in the middle and upper valleys.

                               After this period, the African imports decreased considerably. This trend, generally attested
                         in regional surveys throughout the Italian Peninsula, is confirmed in the Potenza valley, where the
                         African imports post-dating the mid-5th century are only 1/5 of the finds prior to AD 450 and these
                         late imports are fully restricted to the lower valley. The latest type of African red slip attested by our
                         surveys in the Potenza valley is represented by fragments of Hayes 91D and Hayes 105, collected at
                         Potentia and dated to around the first half of the 7th century. Apart from these ones, only at the town
                         of Trea previous excavations discovered a fragment of Hayes 105 (Fabrini 1990).
                               Within the Potenza valley, imitations of African red slip ware were rather limited in numbers.
                         The types found were crude imitations of Hayes 61A and B, both in a red-slipped and in a common
                         version. These types of bowls, loosely inspired by African red slip imports, seem to have been the
                         most common within the central Adriatic.143 On the basis of this exiguous quantity of fragments
                         collected, we assume that in the Potenza valley, the imitations were a marginal phenomenon.
                         Moreover, the imitated wares did not occur more abundant in the inland zones. In general, it seems
                         characteristic rouletted decoration and the grooves on the interior have a lower position. The earlier variant was fairly
                         frequent in the Potenza valley, where it was attested at the excavations of the north-eastern corner of Potentia (Mercando
                         1979, p. 271, n. 1, fig. 180f) and at the intra-site surveys of Ricina and Trea.

that there was no large scale production of imitations of imported wares in central Adriatic Italy
following the decline of imported table wares after the middle of the 5th century AD.
      We consider it useful also to point out that the imports of African cooking ware are extremely
scarce, in the face of the large number of African slip wares. This trend, which seems steady within the
central Adriatic area, can be ascribed to the high availability of local and eastern cooking wares144 in
this market, or to the fact that these vessels from North Africa ceased to be manufactured between the
end of the 4th and the 5th century AD, just when African slip ware started to be imported abundantly.


      6.3.1.3. Other Late Imperial fine wares
      Francesca Carboni

      ‘Terra sigillata medio adriatica’
      We use this denomination to define the fine red-slipped table ware with relief or painted
decoration, consisting of a wide range of fabrics and forms, which was probably produced in
Romagna and northern Marche from the 2nd to the 5th centuries AD.145
      Within the scarce number of samples (14 fragments) from the Potenza Valley Survey we
distinguished two different fabrics. The first of them (TSMA1), consisting of an orange purified clay
with a very fine red to brown glossy slip, was related to the cup Brecciaroli Taborelli 4146 (cat. 2.60),
dating from the mid-2nd to the mid-3rd century AD, but attested frequently in later contexts (Biondani
2014, pp. 257-258), and the bowl Brecciaroli Taborelli 5 (cat. 2.61), present at Suasa in levels from the
4th to the 6th century AD (Biondani 2014, p. 262). The second fabric (TSMA 2), consisting of a beige
purified clay with a fine red slip, was related to the plates Brecciaroli Taborelli 19−22 (cat.2.62), which
were often decorated with dark brown slipped motifs, mainly concentric circles, sometimes connected
by radial flame patterns.147 This type occurred between the 3rd and the 5th, probably also the 6th century
AD, as proven by dated contexts from sites where it was found (Biondani 2014, pp. 271-276). The same
fabric was associated with two fragments that seem similar to a variant of the form Brecciaroli Taborelli
10−17 (cat. 2.63 and 2.64), found in Suasa at the Domus dei Coiedii (Biondani 2014, p. 270, fig. 9, n. 6).

     Late Roman C ware
     The 55 finds of Phocaean pottery (late Roman C ware)148 collected by our surveys in the
Potenza valley round off the ceramic imports from Asia Minor to the western Adriatic shore. This
kind of pottery is thoroughly widespread within the Marche region, even if it is attested in little
quantity. We find it in urban centres, as well as in the rural sites and along the coast, as well as in the
hinterland.149 Even if the form types dated in the first half of the 5th century AD (Hayes 1, 2 and 4)
are very sparsely distributed on the Italian Peninsula, the Late Roman C fragments collected by our
surveys throughout the Potenza valley refer only to forms dated from the mid of the 5th century AD
onwards.150 Within our territory, indeed, the Phocaean red slip, dated from the mid-5th to mid-6th

   6.3.2. Lamps
   Laurent Wilmet, Francesca Carboni

    A substantial part of the lamps collected during the survey could not be identified precisely due
to their fragmentary state. Despite the identification of some rare types for the region, the typological
variety is also poor, and not really representative when compared with the finds from the different
excavations carried out in the Potenza valley, especially in and near the ancient city of Potentia. The
bulk of the material recovered belongs to the large group of the Firmalampen. Late Imperial North
African lamps are also present, as well as probably regionally made imitations.
176                                                                              Laurent Wilmet, Francesca Carboni




            6.3.2.1. Republican Lamps

           Three Republican lamp types could be recognized. The cylindrical lamp cat. 3.1 is a local
      production, manufactured in a red brown light coarse fabric with white inclusions, typical for the
      semi coarse and coarse wares in the Potenza Valley and in the region. From a typological point of
      view, it is similar to the lamps type Ricci H (Ricci 1973, pp. 223-226). These lamp types, found
      during the excavations at the pottery workshop of Aesis (Jesi), are dated between 150 and 50/40
      BC (Brecciaroli Taborelli 1996-1997, p. 226). These dates are confirmed by a similar fragment
      from our own excavations at the amphora workshop of Casa Valentini/Acquabona, (unpublished,
      cf. Monsieur 2009). The rim fragment cat. 3.2 decorated with small globules in relief belongs to
      the type Warzenlampe/Dressel 3, dated between 70 and 15 BC (Pavolini 1981, p. 162). This type
      is not very frequent in the region (cf. also Biondani 2014, p. 349). The nozzle fragment cat. 3.3
      cannot be ascribed to a specific type but it testifies the presence of black gloss lamps on rural sites
      in the Potenza valley. Apart from another wall fragment, no other examples were found during
      the survey.151


            6.3.2.2. Imperial and Late Imperial lamps

            The fragment cat. 3.4 refers to the lamp type Deneauve Vg, probably produced in central
      Tyrrhenian Italy, between the second quarter of the 1st and the early 2nd century BC. These lamps
      were widely distributed throughout the Mediterranean. In the Marche only few examples were
      found, especially at Urbino (Mercando 1985, pp. 355-356). Together with the Loeschcke VIII,
      of which no examples where recognized between the materials of the survey, the type Deneauve
      Vg seems rather anecdotal. The transverse handle cat. 3.6 can be attributed to a local imitation of
      Wall Lamps or Vogelkopflampe, which is confirmed by the finds of some examples in the northern
      necropolis of Potentia (Mercando 1974, tombs 8, 10, 24, 211; Ramadori 2001, pp. 131-133). Only
      four fragments of Volute Lamps (1st-2nd century AD) were identified in the survey (sites 124 and
      140; not in catalogue), although they are not rare in the excavations. The largest group consists
      undoubtedly of the family of the Firmalampen, the lamps with a manufacture stamp on the base,
      which were probably produced in the Po region from the 1st century AD onwards.152 Whereas
      cat. 3.5 seems to be an import piece, most of the fragments recovered show a light coarse orange
      brown fabric that could point to local productions (cat 3.7-3.9). The evidence of the Potenza valley
      confirms the popularity of this lamp type that is also much occurring in the northern necropolis
      of Potentia and in the Marche in general. On the bottom of cat. 3.10 similar to type Buchi X-b,
      there is a stamp with the name of the figulus that is hard to read or even illegible, more because
      of the bad quality of the impression than of its fragmentary state. In comparison with an example
      found in Suasa one can maybe read [FORT]VNI? (Biondani 2003, pp. 119-120; Biondani 2014, p.
      369). However, the fine fabric and the traces of a red brown slip probably lead us to associate this
      lamp to the later examples found in the Marche. Indeed, during Late Antiquity locally produced
      ‘degenerated’ imitations of this lamp type (cat. 3.11) appeared in the north and central Adriatic
      Italy, in many contexts dated from the end of the 3rd century to the 6th century AD.153 A workshop
      with moulds was discovered at the rural site of Monte Torto di Osimo (Pignocchi 2001, pp. 139-
      147; Biondani 2014, p. 362).
      151
          Some black gloss lamps were found in the Italian and Belgian excavations of Potentia (unpublished). For an overview of
      the finds in the region, see Biondani 2014, pp. 346-349.
      152
          For a general framework, besides the pioneering study of Loeschcke 1919, see Buchi 1975, Bailey 1980, Harris 1980
      and Gualandi Gemito 1986.
      153
          Similar examples of late Firmalampen were found at Castelfidardo (Mercando 1979, p. 163), Potentia (Mercando 1979,
      pp. 242 and 263) and at the Villa di Ripe near Ancona (Andrenacci 2010, p. 94).
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                                    177




      Some imported African slip ware lamps turned up in the Potenza Valley Survey. They can be
identified as belonging to the earlier Atlante VIII/Hayes I type (cat. 3.14), present on the Italian
Peninsula from the third quarter of the 4th century AD onwards, and the Atlante X/ Hayes II type (cat.
3.12-3.13), the most documented African lamp in the Marche region, which occurs from the mid-5th
century AD and dominated the market from the mid-6th century AD onwards. Like the African red
slip and African cooking ware, the African lamps were mostly collected in the lower valley along
the Adriatic coast, and to a lesser extent in the middle Potenza valley. Only two fragments belonging
to imitations of African lamps, possibly regionally manufactured in a hard red clay (2.5YR) with
some mica inclusions and with unslipped surface, were picked up during the grid survey in Potentia
(cat. 3.15).


      6.3.3. Late Imperial common wares
      Francesca Carboni

    The identification of late Roman common wares has often proven to be problematic, and
it would seem almost impossible when dealing with artefacts picked-up by way of surface
surveys. Nevertheless, we could reconstruct the pottery assemblages diffused within the
Potenza valley from the 3rd to the 6th century AD, after comparing them with the finds
from well dated contexts of the costal colony Potentia. For this, it was crucial to review the
documentation concerning the excavations at the northern necropolis and at the north-eastern
insula of this town, carried out in the 1970s and 1980s (Mercando 1974 and 1979) and by the
study of the pottery, unpublished up to date, brought to light during the campaigns conducted
by the Soprintendenza per i Beni Archeologici delle Marche in the central area of the town
from 1982 and 2005.154 These last investigations allowed us to figure out the nature and typo-
chronological features of a substantial and stratigraphically reliable amount of pottery, which
provides valuable information on the Mediterranean products that circulated in central Adriatic
Italy in the periods concerned.
    It is still quite problematic to distinguish fabric groups within the group of plain wares, as
the fabric is always purified. Moreover, the fragmentary state of the pottery makes it often very
difficult to distinguish plain wares from the flat-based amphorae, which were typical for the
central Adriatic region from the 1st to the 4th and possibly also the 5th century AD. For the
coarse ware instead, clay analysis of the pottery was combined with petrographic analysis of the
fabric groups.155 The archaeometric data revealed that a good percentage of the coarse common
wares were imported (from northern Italy, north Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean) and were
thus not locally made. Of particular interest is the presence of common wares from the Italian
Peninsula that are testimony to the interregional exchange of pottery for utilitarian use in the late
Roman period.

the excavations at the central area of Potentia. The study of the material from excavations was possible thanks to the
courtesy of E. Percossi Serenelli, responsible of the Soprintendenza per i Beni archeologici delle Marche and scientific

            6.3.3.1. Late plain common wares

            Within the group of late plain common wares, it has been possible to isolate two pottery
      categories that can be considered as benchmark indicators of Late Antiquity in the central Adriatic
      area: the colour-coated ware (ceramica a rivestimento rosso) and the red or brown painted ware
      (ceramica dipinta a bande). The difference between colour-coated ware and Red painted ware lays
      in the application of the red slip. The colour-coated wares consist of pottery completely or partly
      covered with a red slip, like the imitations of African red slip, while the term Red painted ware is
      used for plain ware decorated with geometrical motifs in red slip.
            Within the Potenza valley, the colour-coated wares are the most common, often combined with
      incised or cogged decoration, consisting of rouletted rectangular or triangular impressions. The most
      typical form seems to be the jug with neck collar (cat. 4.23 and 4.24), decorated on the shoulder. It
      finds many comparisons in northern and central Adriatic contexts, dated from the middle of the 5th
      century to the first half of the 6th century AD (Negrelli 2007), demonstrating it to be a regionally
      produced ware from the area between eastern Emilia and the northern part of central Marche. The
      fragmentary state of the sherds makes the attribution of some of them uncertain: they might belong
      or to the closed vessels illustrated above or to globular and carinated jars with similar decorations.
      Examples of the latter pots are present in contexts of 4th-5th centuries from Urbs Salvia (Fabrini
      et al. 2006, p. 362), Monte Torto (Pignocchi 2001, p. 117), Castelfidardo (Mercando 1979, p. 151),
      and Pescara (Staffa 1991, p. 214).
            The Red painted wares decorated with geometrical patterns and randomly applied strokes
      were typical for southern Italy, where many production sites were located. Their distribution was
      widespread in Campania, Molise, Basilicata, Abruzzo, Puglia and Calabria, where an increase of
      the production occurred in the 6th century AD in the early Byzantine period. In Marche several
      examples of this pottery class, attested with various types of fabric and decoration, are present in
      different sites (Biondani 2015, pp. 270-271, TAB. 3). In absence of well-dated stratigraphic contexts
      it is difficult to refer to a punctual chronological range for the different forms, even if several clues
      lead to date them to the 5th century AD. During the excavations at the central area of Potentia, one
      wall fragment with a red painted geometrical pattern was found, which can be related to the southern
      Italian Red painted productions. Within the Potenza valley, the examples collected by surveys refer
      only to types where diluted red glaze, mostly applied in drops, create not-clearly defined decorations
      (cat. 4.25 and 4.26). For these vessels, similar to some Tuscany pots, dated to the 4th-5th centuries
      AD (Filippi 2007, pp. 19-25)156, also a local production cannot be excluded.


            6.3.3.2. Coarse ware from the central Adriatic

           The percentage of locally and regionally produced coarse ware is the most attested in the valley
      (61 %). The macroscopic properties of the pottery, exclusively fired in oxidized firing conditions
      (2.5 YR 5/8, light reddish brown), are the abundant presence of silex inclusions within the rather
      purified fabric. Also typical are very small river pebbles, often protruding the surface. The most
      common forms were the casseroles/bowls/lids with inclining rims (cat. 4.71-4.78). Within this
      category produced in the local fabric LRCW4, 6 and 12157, we distinguished several varieties, based
      on the size of the vessel and the form of the rim.
           Typical for the 3rd-4th centuries AD is the large and deep casserole/lid with soft inclination
      and plain, slightly thickened or almond-shaped rim, sometimes delineated on the interior with a
        The same type of painted wares have been found at the cryptoporticus of Urbs Salvia: Cingolani, Tubaldi 2015, pp. 305-306.

groove, and with a flat base. The diameter of these vessels ranges between 22 cm and 32 cm. In the
5th century AD, the body of the casserole tends to get heavier, with a thicker wall. Representative are
also the casseroles/lids with sharp inclining almond-shaped or triangular rim, sometimes creating
a ridge on the interior, with a flat base. This form, which was clearly used as a bowl and casserole,
and probably as lids, because the same types occur without fire traces, was widely attested in the
central Adriatic.158 Finally, few examples of clibani were found (cat. 4.80). This form, consisting
of a dome-shaped lid with a handle, often with a ventilation hole in the wall was used as a mobile
oven, in which food could be cooked in an open fire. Although it goes back to prehistoric times and
was marginally present during the Republican and early Imperial period, this vessel re-emerged to
the foreground during the 3rd century AD and remained very popular into the Early Middle Ages.


      6.3.3.3. Coarse ware from Northern Italy

       Besides the local coarse wares, we detected within the Potenza valley several imports from
the northern part of the Italian peninsula. Especially the trade routes along the river Po and the via
Aemilia seem to have been very closely connected to the central Adriatic.
       The ‘Olle ad orlo sagomato e decorazione incisa’ (cat. 4.81) is a type of cooking jar with profiled
rim, sometimes decorated with impressed patterns on the shoulder. According to archaeometric
analysis it was produced with clay coming from the Alpine region (Corti et al. 2002; Bertolani et al.
2002), and reached the coastal colony of Potentia. Here fragments of this ware were found during our
artefact surveys as well as by excavations, in contexts dating from the 1st to the 4th century AD.159
Another type of cooking vessel imported from northern Italy is a burnished coarse ware, which was
first identified in wells in the area surrounding the Roman town of Mutina (Gelichi, Giordani 1994).
It has been assumed that this pottery was locally manufactured in several workshops in the eastern
Po area (Corti et al. 2004). The Modena coarse wares were at first presumed to be distributed only
regionally via the river Po. Afterwards, they were found in several sites of Northern Italy, located
between Luni and the Venetian lagoon. In Potentia, which up to date represents their most southern
destination, they were present in contexts dated to the 3rd-5th centuries AD (cat. 4.82).
       In fact, petrographic analysis conducted by the team of the PVS project (Verreyke, De Paepe
2009) confirmed the origin of these pots, consisting of cooking jars with everted rim.160 From a
macroscopic point of view, the fabric (LRCW 5) is extremely coarse, with a very dark grey exterior
and interior surface (10 YR 3/1) and an oxidized orange-red core (2.5 YR 4/6). The pottery shows
many voids on the surface, and it is common to observe large white semi-translucent angular
inclusions. They also abound in the fabric, in combination with some silver-coloured ones. Irregular
polishing marks and occasional impressed decorations occur on this burnished cooking ware. The
uneven thickness of the walls and the asymmetric shapes led to hypothesize that these vessels were
made on a slow turning wheel (Brogiolo, Gelichi, 1998, p. 214). Lately, researchers suggest that
the vessels were all manufactured on a fast turning wheel, after which the polish marks were applied
by hand, thus causing irregularities of the form. This characteristic feature, in combination with the
abundant presence of calcite, which testifies that the vessels were fired in a reduced atmosphere with
a low firing temperature (<850°C), induced scholars to attribute this pottery to a household industry
or to individual workshops (Corti et al. 2004, p. 165 and 169).
158
    Osimo (Monte Torto): Pignocchi 2001a, p. 125, n. 297-301, p. 128, n. 311-314. Suasa: Mazzeo Saracino 1991a. Matrice

            Closely related to the Modena burnished ware are the cooking globular pots ‘tipo Classe’,
      which were present in Potentia and on some coastal rural sites in the Potenza valley around the
      mid of the 6th century AD (cat. 4.83).161 The archaeometric analysis of the PVS project revealed
      that the fabrics of Modena and the Classe ware (LRCW 10) are practically identical, thus testifying
      to the same geographical origin. This pottery was first identified at the podere Chiavichetta site in
      Classe (Gelichi 1998), in the surrounding area of which its production workshops must be localized.
      The vessels consist of coarse ware cooking jars with a flat base and everted rim and a wavy line
      decoration on the shoulder.


            6.3.3.4. Coarse ware from the eastern Mediterranean

           The Aegean production of corrugated cooking jars and frying-pans is well represented in
      Potentia and the Potenza valley. Most of the collected finds seem to date to the late 4th-5th centuries
      AD and can be easily compared with the contemporary vessels from the domestic waste discovered
      in the cistern at the villa rustica di San Giacomo degli Schiavoni (Biferno valley, Molise). The
      vessels from the Potenza valley consist namely of the forms Albarella 1-3 (cat. 4.84 and 4.85),
      individualised there (Albarella et al. 1993).
           The Potenza Valley Survey project recognised 5 different fabrics related to the Aegean
      cooking ware:
      - LRCW2a: reduced (5YR 5/2, reddish grey) sandy fabric with regular break and some void in the
      matrix. Polished surface, on which silver-coloured shimmer is often visible.
      - LRCW2b: reduced (10YR 5/2, greyish brown), compact sandy fabric with regular break. Abundant
      silver-coloured shimmer on the exterior surface.
      - LRCW2c: reduced (10YR 5/1, grey) compact fabric with a hackly break.
      - LRCW2d: reduced (2.5YR N3/, very dark grey) compact fabric with a hackly, laminated break.
      The exterior surface shows polish marks.
      - LRCW2e: a reddish brown (2.5YR 5/4) core and perfectly evenly reduced exterior and interior
      margins (5YR 2/1, black). Very shiny, good quality surface with a silver-coloured shimmer.


            6.3.3.5. Reduced cooking ware

           A ware fired in reduced atmosphere was identified, consisting of burnished cooking jars
      with everted rim and a flat base and casseroles/lids with an inclining rim, first at Potentia162 and
      then in the Potenza valley sites (cat. 4.86 and 4.87). This group (LRCW 7) strongly resembles
      the imported Aegean grey cooking ware, if we consider both the forms of the vessels and the
      similarities in the fabric when examined macroscopically. However, the presence of casseroles
      with inclining rim in the same fabric suggested that it was in fact a separate pottery class, as
      confirmed by archaeometric analysis. The exterior surface of the vessels is always reduced (10YR
      4/1, dark grey). The fabric can show three different conditions: a) grey core/interior margin
      with a red exterior margin and a black surface; b) red core/internal margin with a grey external
      margin, and c) completely dark grey. While the inclusions in LRCW 2 pointed to a production
      area with ophiolitic rock fragments, coinciding with the Aegean geological characteristics, the
      reduced ware contains metamorphic lithic fragments, possibly coinciding with a production area

in southern Italy. Waiting for further data and future possible comparisons, these artefacts can

      6.3.3.6. African cooking ware

     Besides coarse wares from the Italian Peninsula and the eastern Mediterranean, African cooking
wares were also imported to the Potenza valley. The collected fragments of African cooking ware
refer to types dated from the 2nd-3rd centuries to the mid-late 4th century AD, consisting of the
casserole Hayes 23, the lids 181 and 182, the dishes/lids 196 and 197 (cat. 4.88-4.93).164 The number
and location of our finds seem to confirm the general distribution trend of this kitchen pottery
throughout the Marche region and along the Adriatic seashore of the Italian Peninsula, where these
imports were less abundant and often limited to the coastal region. The lower frequency of African
cooking wares in this area could also be the result of the fact that African red slip only got into its
stride from the 4th and 5th centuries AD onwards, when the production and export of kitchen ware
was already diminishing due to the high costs of the transport. Similarly, the sparse distribution of
African cooking ware in the hinterland could also be the result of the high transport costs over land
(Reynolds 1995, p. 102).




                                                                                                                            Fig. 116 LRCW10:
                                                                                                                            thin section in plane
                                                                                                                            polarized light.
                                                                                                                            Alkali feldspar
                                                                                                                            (AF), microcline
                                                                                                                            (Mi), calcite (C)
                                                                                                                            and quartz (Q).


      The information from the late coarse wares collected through the surveys of the PVS project
produces new evidence on the circulations of imports along the Italian Adriatic coast and on the
insertion of the Roman coastal colony of Potentia within the Mediterranean routes, in the period
preceding the Lombard conquest. With regard to the first point, it emerges, in particular, that the
trade, distributed via short - or medium - distance ‘cabotage’, of coarse ware produced by household
industries, is differently dispersed within the Italian peninsula. Regarding the imports of cooking
ware from Northern Africa and the Eastern Mediterranean, we note that, starting from the 5th century
AD, there is a drop of the African goods against an increase of Aegean pottery and Italic productions
that show similar technological and morphological features.

et al. 2010.

             6.3.4. Amphorae
             Patrick Monsieur, Francesca Carboni

          The typological variety of the amphorae found in the Potenza valley is impressive. The survey
      finds revealed at least 32 types.165 The three oldest fragments belong to Greek style wine amphorae
      of the 4th or 3rd century BC, but it remains hard to identify them as imports or as regional imitations.
      Remarkable is that among the later amphorae the Central Adriatic productions represent the most
      important group with 5 main types. The discovery of several amphora production centres in the
      lower Potenza valley proves that a substantial part of the survey finds, if not most of the occurring
      Central Adriatic types, were produced locally. Their production initiated in the first quarter of the
      2nd century BC and continued until the middle or even the second half of the 1st century AD. Most
      types were manufactured for the export of wine, but in the Early Imperial period olive-oil became
      also important. These amphora productions were followed by flat-bottomed types, mainly made in
      the lower Po valley, but probably also in workshops in the Central Adriatic. From the Tyrrhenian
      coast, most probably Campania, there was some import of wine amphorae as several, mostly
      undiagnostic sherds testify. One or two fragments can be identified as Punic types, which were
      designed for the export of fish-based products. A 2nd-century AD amphora type with a conspicuous
      funnel rim was almost certainly designed for the transport of olives. This type must have been
      manufactured somewhere between North and Central Italy. The presence of Greek imports is also
      striking. Hellenistic Rhodian wine amphorae are represented with some regularity and such imports
      from Rhodes were resumed from the Augustan age onwards. In the 1st and 2nd centuries AD other
      Greek wine amphorae were introduced from Kos, Asia Minor and Crete. This Eastern import route
      continued to flourish in the Late Imperial period with the introduction of new Aegean and Asia
      Minor types for the transport of olive-oil and wine. Iberian imports are extremely rare but present.
      North African types pop up from the 3rd century AD onwards carrying - like the Iberian amphorae
      - olive-oil as well as fish-based products. Finally, there is a fragment of a Late Imperial Palestinian
      amphora witnessing wine import from that region.


               6.3.4.1. Late classical Greek-style and Hellenistic amphorae

            The oldest examples of amphorae revealed by the Potenza valley survey are imported Greek or
      Greek-style productions from the 4th and 3rd centuries BC. Only three examples were recognized and
      all of them were found on early sites. The fabrics give no conclusions as to their origin, but if they are
      regional productions they are clearly inspired by Greek models. A pointed base from Monte dei Priori
      (cat. 5.55, site 122) reminds us of Corinthian B types manufactured in Corinth or Corcyra (Koehler
      1982; Whitbread 1995). Two rim fragments from Montarice (cat. 5.56, site 24) and Casa Cingolani
      (not in catalogue, site 119) could point as well to Corinthian B, as to regional parallels from Adria
      which are at any rate inspired by the Corinthian parallels (Toniolo 2000, 13-31). These Greek-style
      amphorae from Adria can be considered as the first generation of Adriatic Greco-Italic amphorae.
      Corinthian B and early Greco-Italic wine amphorae belong evidently to the sphere of Greek commerce
      and civilization which had a large impact in the Adriatic and especially on Picene culture.
          In the course of the first quarter of the 2nd-century BC Rhodian Hellenistic wine amphorae were
      introduced in the Potenza Valley. The amphorae of Rhodes are the largest known and most diffused
      production in the Hellenistic period, especially in the period 250-50 BC (Grace & Savvatianou-
      Petropoulakou 1970, 289-316; Empereur & Hesnard 1987, 18-20; Withbread 1995, 53-67). The
      fine fabric and finishing of this top shaped amphora with its typical elegant rounded handles and peg
      toe is rather easy recognizable even in small fragments. Both handles were systematically stamped
      165
            See also the excellent overview of the amphora finds in the domus of the Coiedii at Suasa: Gamberini 2014.
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                              187




with the respective names of the eponym officials and the producers, providing invaluable dating
clues (Grace 1953; Finkielsztejn 2001). The chronology of the stamps also revealed that the export
of Rhodian amphorae peaked in the 2nd century BC. A substantial part of the Rhodian stamps,
maybe even half them, found on different sites of the Central and Northern Adriatic coasts precisely
date from the first half of 2nd century BC (Marengo, Paci 2008). The oldest stamps come from
excavations in Ancona and Potentia. One example of Potentia reveals the name of the eponym
official Philodamos (dated c. 183 BC), which is quite near to the foundation date of the city in 184
BC (Monsieur, Percossi, forthcoming). Even if unfortunately no stamps were discovered during
the surveys, fragments of Rhodian amphorae (cat. 5.57-5.60) were regularly recovered during
fieldwalking on different early colonial sites in the lower Potenza valley (especially sites 101,
108, 119 and 134). The common presence on these sites of Rhodian amphora fragments and of
locally produced Greco-Italic amphorae, which can be dated in the same period (see below on the
workshop of Colle Burchio, site 105, and its chronology), makes the Rhodian chronology of stamps
indirectly relevant for the survey finds. We must note that the regular presence of Rhodian amphorae
on these early colonial sites should be interpreted with care, and not solely be linked to the colonial
foundation moment, as since they do not all date from this period. Indeed an important part of the
stamped handles found in the Adriatic region dates from the second half of the 2nd and the beginning
of the 1st century BC. Finally we note that even if most Hellenistic Rhodian amphora finds were
recovered in the lower Potenza valley, they also sparsely occur inland, as far as the town sites of
Ricina and Trea (not in catalogue).


   6.3.4.2. Republican and Imperial Adriatic amphorae

    The Central Adriatic amphorae represent the largest group among the amphora finds of the
survey. There used to be a lot of confusion concerning these long-distance transport containers.
The Lamboglia 2 type is the most representative type and this vessel was long considered as an oil
container of Apulian origin (Tchernia 1986, pp. 53-56 and pp.68-74). In fact different production
zones in relation to contents and chronology must be distinguished for the Late Republican and
Early Imperial period. Although Apulia really produced wine amphorae, the mass of the production
concerned rounded and bag shaped oil containers, generally defined as the Brindisi type, since the
bulk of them seemed to be manufactured in that region. Brindisian amphorae were largely exported
to the Greek East and their production must have lasted until the last quarter of the 1st century BC
(Will 1989; Cipriano and Carre 1989, pp. 68-74; Manacorda 1990; Nonnis 2001, pp. 467-480;
Palazzo 2003). Nevertheless further research has established now that the most important region
producing amphorae was definitely the Central Adriatic coast, mostly for the export of wine, but in
the last phase also for olive oil. The export was mostly directed to North Italy, the Dalmatian coast
and the Eastern Mediterranean (Tchernia 1986, pp. 68-74; Cipriano & Carre 1989; Will 1989;
Bezeczky 1994; Bruno 1995; Nonnis 2001; Carre et al. 2014). From the Augustan era onwards
production in the Po Valley and Istria would gradually eclipse the Central Adriatic region with their
Dressel 6A, Dressel 2-4 and Dressel 6B types. In the course of the 1st century AD Istria and the north
Dalmatian coast dominated the oil production, transported in a later Dressel 6B type, whereas from
the Flavian age North and Central Italy started to produce flat-bottomed wine amphorae, probably
emulating South-Gaulish types. Especially the Southern Po valley successfully exported from then
onwards its Forlimpopoli type (Aldini 1978; Panella 1989; Tassaux 2001).
    The production sites of Central Adriatic amphorae are mostly to be situated along the coastline,
approximately between Rimini and Pescara, i.e. in the Ager Gallicus and Picenum, although some
ateliers are also known in the Po valley (Mercando 1979, pp. 110-131; Cipriano and Carre 1989;
Brecciaroli Taborelli 1984; Staffa; Carre et al. 2014). Late Republican Central Adriatic amphorae
188                                                                                  Patrick Monsieur, Francesca Carboni




      (Carre 1985; Cipriano and Carre 1989; Pesavento Mattioli 1992; Bruno 1995; Toniolo 2000;
      Starac 2001) can be classified in 5 typological groups: Greco-Italic (cat. 5.1-5.11), Lamboglia 2
      (cat. 5.12-5.20), Dressel 6A (cat. 5.28-5.31), Dressel 2-4 (cat. 5.41-5.46) and Dressel 6B (cat. 5.32-
      5.40).166 Within these main types there is a wide range of subtypes and types hesitating between one or
      another main type. When the amphorae are in a fragmentary state it even becomes more problematic
      to make typological distinctions. These subtypes could reflect as well local traditions and origins
      of workshops, as transition periods from one type to another due to technological improvements
      and fashions (imitations). The different types evolved one from the other in a chronological line.
      Most of them are wine-containers, only the Dressel 6B types carried olive oil (and maybe olives).
      This type is still much confused with the resembling examples produced in Istria, and it is true
      that sometimes they are hard to be distinguished. Otherwise it seems also that oil-amphorae from
      the Brindisi region, probably rather incidentally, reached the Central Adriatic (cat. 5.25-5.27). The
      association of typological and fabric studies of Central Adriatic amphorae is important to make
      distinctions between workshops and amphora groups, but up till now archaeometric research is
      rather limited (Bezeczky 1994, pp. 143-195; Bruno 1995, pp. 82-92; Mange and Bezeczky 2006;
      Menchelli et al. 2008; Braekmans et al., forthcoming). The amphorae don’t show much variety in
      their fabrics. They are mostly characterized by pale, little sandy but fine and well levigated fabrics.
      These amphorae are hard-fired and the exterior colour goes from red brown over yellowish beige
      to greenish. Apart from the typical dark red brown clay nodules, little inclusions are to be seen. In
      some cases the density of these red brown nodules can give a clue to distinguish a workshop, as is
      the case with the Colle Burchio site (site 105, see below; cat. 5.4-5.5, 5.7, 5.9-5.11) where they are
      very abundant in the fabrics. Otherwise the typological variety of subclasses within the main types
      is so disconcerting that they are of little help to distinguish workshops. With some rare exceptions
      on Greco-Italic examples, only a certain amount of Lamboglia 2 and Dressel 6A amphorae seem to
      bear name stamps, but not so regularly as the mass of published examples would suggest. In fact it
      remains unknown what the percentage of stamped and unstamped Central Adriatic amphorae would
      have been. All stamps are related to the property and the internal organization of the workshops and
      have no commercial aims, which are normally expressed by tituli picti. Unfortunately, whatever
      the reasons might be, painted inscriptions occur very rarely on Central Adriatic amphorae (stamps:
      Pesavento Mattioli 1992; Bruno 1995, pp. 119-291; Toniolo 1991, pp. 163-195; tituli picti: CIL
      XV, 2; Romano 1994). Amphora stamps are invaluable tools for chronology and tracing trade routes
      to consumption sites. Unfortunately all the examples from the Potenza Valley Survey are illegible.
      Another peculiar element of Adriatic amphorae is the presence of ceramic stoppers. The older
      examples have a specific pointed hat-like profile and belong to Greco-Italic and early Lamboglia
      2 types. Since lots of misfired examples where found in the excavation of a kiln in the workshop
      of Acquabona (Casa Valentini: site 111, see below) they can at least partly be dated in the last
      quarter of the 2nd century and the first quarter of the 1st century BC. Examples with flat profile and
      central knob belonged to Lamboglia 2 examples, whereas later types with decoration and (mostly
      undeciphered) inscriptions must have been used to close Dressel 6A and B amphorae.
            The chronological framework of the typology is still floating but can be sketched in its main
      lines. The dating is above all provided by shipwrecks, destruction sites and foundation dates (Carre
      1985; Cipriano, Carre 1989; Bruno 1995, pp. 27-46). The period of production of Late Republican
      and Imperial Central Adriatic amphorae ranges from 200/175 BC to AD 75/100, i.e. a period of some
      250 years or even more. First the late Greco-Italic type appeared. As will be discussed below, the
      Belgian excavations of the west gate of Potentia provided a new dating clue for this production that
      166
          It is true that there exists a proto-type of the Dressel 6B type, in imitation of Bridisian types but the definition of its form
      and variants as well as the chronology remain unclear. In the literature this imitation is called ‘affine alle brindisine’,‘ante
      Dressel 6B’, ‘anfore con orlo ad anello’ or ‘anfore ovoidale’: Cipriano, Carre 1989, pp. 68-74; Toniolo 1991, pp. 17-19;
      Pesavento Mattioli 1992, pp. 44-45; Bruno 1995, p. 22.
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                189




started c. 200-175 BC. In the course of the 2nd century BC this slender type became sturdier and
merged around 100 BC into the classic Lamboglia 2 type. In the Augustan age, c. 30/20 BC, this type
evolved into a larger and more massive type called the Dressel 6A. Around the turn of the century,
probably parallel with the continuing production of Dressel 6A, the new Dressel 2-4 type with its
typical double-barrelled handles was developed. Between 40 and AD 75/100 this type was probably
one of the last representatives of Central Adriatic amphora production, apart from the production of
flat-bottomed types which are still ill-attested. It is worth insisting that Central Adriatic Dressel 2-4
amphorae are rarely recognized, let alone considered. In fact, because of their fine fabric, they tend
to be confused with the Greek originals from Kos and their imitations from the coasts of Asia Minor
that they took as model. Their omnipresence in the whole of the valley of the River Potenza proves
the importance of this amphora type and its great relevance for the knowledge of wine production
during the 1st century AD in the Central Adriatic.
      The great age of the export of Central Adriatic wine is to be situated in the period 125-50 BC,
about the lifetime of the Lamboglia 2 type as is shown by its mass presence on consumption sites in
Dalmatia and in the East, especially in Athens, the Cyclades and Alexandria. There was a decrease
with the later exports of Dressel 6A up to the late Augustan era, but they still occur in appreciable
quantities on sites in both East and West. The situation of their followers, the Dressel 2-4, is
interesting. Even when considering the difficulties to identify them on consumption sites, they are
not abundant outside Italy. In the Potenza valley on the contrary, they seem well represented, even
if their identification relies on diagnostic fragments (typical rim and toe, double-barrelled handles,
incision or sharp angle between shoulder and neck). This is also confirmed by the urban survey of
inland Ricina, which revealed a large number of locally produced Dressel 2-4 amphorae, but not
so many older types. This could point to a wine production that became more regionally oriented
in the 1st century AD, whereas the older Central Adriatic wine amphorae were destined for long-
distance export.
      The export of olive-oil in Dressel 6B is a late and short-lived phenomenon. It started possibly
around 50 BC with a prototype that imitated the types of the Brindisi region. It is possible that our
excavation of the Casa Valentini/Acquabona workshop (site 111) has revealed some fragments, but
further study is needed to confirm this. At any rate, gradually the Central Adriatic production took
over from Brindisi and by 25 BC the massive Dressel 6B type with its large curved rim, rounded
handles and heavy knob emerged. The export reached its peak in the Augustan and early Tiberian
ages and vanished around 40 AD, overshadowed by the Istrian olive-oil production bringing their
own version of Dressel 6B on the market.
      Thanks to the surveys of the Potenza and Tenna Valleys the picture of the amphora workshops
along the Central Adriatic coasts recently became more refined (Pasquinucci & Menchelli 2002;
Pasquinucci et al. 2006; Menchelli & Ciuccarelli 2009; Menchelli 2012, pp. 67-71). In fact
archive research, study of literature, intensive survey and excavation revealed 7 different amphora
production centres in the Lower Potenza Valley, the ancient ager Potentinus. This is a considerable
contribution to the comprehension of pre-industrial production and export of wine and olive-oil
in this region. The excavations of the west gate sector in Potentia and of the amphora workshop
site of Acquabona (site 111) near Potenza Picena provided dating clues to the typo-chronology,
and confirmed local production of transport amphorae of different types. Especially the find in
2010 of the city wall dated by written sources around 175-174 BC had consequences as to the
Republican chronology of the earliest amphora production in the Potenza valley. Indeed, in the city
wall contexts the same types of amphorae turned up as at the Colle Burchio amphora workshop (site
105). On the other hand the chronology and the occurrence of different amphora types recovered
from the amphora workshops in the Lower Potenza Valley are very variable. Most types occur in the
workshop of Acquabona revealing a continuity of production on the same site. Otherwise, the survey
seems to confirm that the site of Colle Burchio produced the oldest type (Greco-Italic) and only that
190                                                                         Patrick Monsieur, Francesca Carboni




      one, revealing not only its short existence, but also that this workshop is a material reflection of the
      early Romanization of the region, since it was created shortly after the foundation of the colony of
      Potentia in 184 BC. A third workshop, at Casa Giampaoli (site 129), revealed the manufacture of
      Dressel 6A and B. Finally, a fourth workshop partly covering the southern fringe of the northern
      necropolis (cf. La Pineta, site 129) and the north part of the north east quarter of Potentia was
      identified by the study of archives and literature and by the recent excavations of the Soprintendenza
      of Ancona (Monsieur et al. 2003, 2006; Vermeulen et al. 2005, 2009, 2011; Monsieur 2007, 2009;
      Vermeulen & Monsieur 2012; Braekmans et al. forthcoming, Van Limbergen et al., forthcoming;
      Italian excavations: Mercando 1979, pp. 184-186 and pp. 281-293; Percossi Serenelli 2007). This
      workshop produced Dressel 6A and B, and probably also Dressel 2-4 types.167 However, the study
      of the amphora workshops of the ager Potentinus remains puzzling because of a lack of essential
      data. Only the site of Acquabona is known to have had a long production, from 125/100-75 BC to
      AD 25/50, i.e. around 125 years. In contrast, the site of Colle Burchio merely produced the Greco-
      Italic types during the first half of the 2nd century BC, which proves that some workshops tended
      to remain, others to shift to a different place, or to disappear, whatever the reasons might have been.
      Concerning the other workshop sites of the lower Potenza valley, there is only proof of production in
      the Augustan and Tiberian periods, but of course they can hide older phases in the deeper layers, as
      the excavation at the site of Acquabona demonstrated. Even embarrassing is the lack of substantial
      information on the relation of these amphora production sites with wine or olive-oil producing
      estates. Were they separated, independent workshops? Did some of them depend from the city, as the
      site of La Pineta near the north necropolis of Potentia might suggest? Did they belong to the same
      estate where a favourable situation for both agriculture and the manufacture of pottery was present?
      Or did such complex relationships exist all at once?


           6.3.4.3. Imported Italian Imperial amphorae

            With the exception of some Campanian amphorae, no Tyrrhenian amphorae types were
      recognized. Although never in great quantities, the fabrics point to a volcanic environment, almost
      certainly Campania that was known as major amphora production region (Hesnard et al. 1989; Ricq
      de Boüard et al. 1989; Panella & Fano 1989). The fragments are mostly wall fragments which
      hampers the identification and the date of the amphora type (Greco-Italic? Dressel 1A? Dressel 1B?
      Dressel 2-4) so no imported Italian Republican amphorae could be recognized. One double-barrelled
      handle fragment from the Casa Moretti site in the Upper Potenza Valley leaves no doubt about its
      identification as an imperial Campanian Dressel 2-4 (cat. 5.47, site 49). In the second half of the
      1st century AD the Central Adriatic Dressel 2-4 were gradually replaced by flat-bottomed amphora
      types. Probably a substantial amount came from Northern Italy, Forlimpopoli and the region of
      Rimini, but with the workshop of Spello also Central-Adriatic workshops can be supposed (Aldini
      1978; Panella 1989). When only fragments are recovered it is in many cases nearly impossible to
      distinguish them from large jugs and basins, especially when it concerns smaller survey material
      (cat. 5.49-5.54). On the other hand, the discovery by excavations of 12 complete flat-bottomed
      amphorae in the northern necropolis of Potentia demonstrates a wide variety of subtypes that cannot
      be reduced to the mere Forlimpopoli types (Mercando 1974; Monsieur 2007). Only two fragments
      of a Funnel Rim type amphora (tipo collo ad imbuto) were recognized among the material of the
      survey (cat. 5.48). They used to be misleadingly coined as the ‘Portorecanatian type’ since 6 rather
      well preserved examples were discovered in the northern necropolis of Potentia, near present-day
      Porto Recanati. The origin of this amphora type is unclear but fabric studies point to Northern Italy.
      167
          The three other amphora workshop sites identified through study of literature and archive work are not taken into
      consideration here, since they do not belong to the surveyed zone.
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                               191




The content of this amphora type is not ascertained, but when comparing the same sort of funnel
rim with Baetican and South Gaulish examples the transport of olives in defrutum can be reasonably
surmised. The Baetican and Gaulish evidence is supported by tituli picti and by the find of olive
stones. It is likely that the peculiar rim of the amphora was designed for the filling and pouring of
olives (Bezeczky 1987, pp. 34-36; Monsieur 2001; Mazzocchin 2009).


     6.3.4.4. Greek Imperial amphorae

      The production of Rhodian Hellenistic amphorae seriously declined or was even interrupted
for some time after the destruction of Rhodes by Cassius in 43 BC (Finkielsztejn 2001, 199). At
an unknown moment in the Augustan period production was resumed but without the habit of
official stamping. The typology changed to more slender and smaller forms with typical horned
handles. There is a wide variety of subtypes, capacities and fabrics pointing to different workshops
on Rhodes and its territory on the coasts of Asia Minor, so far that these amphora groups are
regularly named as ‘Rhodian tradition’ (cat. 5.61-5.64). The two most occurring fabrics are the fine
red brown group resembling the Hellenistic predecessors and a more sandy red brown fabric with
large white inclusions (Peacock 1977, pp. 266-270 Hesnard 1986; Empereur & Picon 1986, pp.
116-117; Empereur & Picon 1989, pp. 224-225; Gamberini 2014, pp. 562-564). Another important
group consists of wine amphorae of Kos and Asia Minor imitations, recognizable through their fine
finishing and double-barrelled handles. During the 1st century AD the Koan amphora type became
one of the most imitated wine containers in the Empire, as we have seen already for the Campanian
and Central Adriatic Dressel 2-4 types. The Koan amphorae of the 1st century AD are also classified
as Dressel 5, a more cylindrical form than its Hellenistic predecessors. In fragments it is not possible
to distinguish the Hellenistic from the Imperial types and the fine, slightly sandy light brown to pink
fabric is in both cases the same (cat. 5.67-5.69). Since the Hellenistic type does not occur much in
the Adriatic we suspect the fragments of the survey to be mostly Imperial (Hesnard 1986; Empereur
& Picon 1986, pp. 109-112; Empereur & Picon 1989, pp. 225-226; Empereur & Hesnard 1987, p.
13; Monsieur & De Paepe 2002, pp. 163-166). In the 2nd century AD the Dressel 5 type evolves
in a more elongated form with a peculiar long convex or concave neck called Knossos 19 type,
which refers to the Villa Dionysos excavations in Knossos. The find of 5 examples in the northern
necropolis of Potentia confirms its 2nd-century date (Hayes 1991; Mercando 1974; Monsieur
2007). A substantial neck and shoulder fragment with broken-off handle was found at the temple site
of Cimarella near Ricina (cat. 5.70; site 98). Cretan Dressel 43/Crétoise 4 small wine amphorae are
also characterized by horned handles. Most of the handle and wall fragments recovered in the survey
show a sandy, sometimes coarse rather dark red brown fabric with limestone inclusions (cat. 5.65-
5.66). The contexts of the tombs of the north necropolis of Potentia confirm their date in the second
half of the 1st and first half of the 2nd century AD (Marangou-Lerat 1995, pp. 84-89; Mercando
1974; Monsieur 2007). Rhodian, Koan and Cretan amphorae are all widely diffused in the Empire,
not only in the Mediterranean but also in the northern provinces.


     6.3.4.5. Late Punic and Iberian amphorae

      The site of Casa Cingolani (site 119) revealed a profiled hollow toe fragment of a late Punic
so-called tubular amphora. It was found together with several fragments of Central Adriatic Greco-
Italic amphorae on this early colonial site. Most interesting is the reference that can be made with the
material found in Corinth among the destruction debris under the southeast building, at the east end
of the south stoa. There, seven complete Adriatic Greco-Italic and two late Punic so-called ‘tubular’
192                                                                            Patrick Monsieur, Francesca Carboni




      amphorae were discovered providing most probably a terminus ante quem of 146 BC (Romano
      1994, pp. 86-89). Punic amphorae are known to have carried fish-based products. A rim fragment
      found on the same site belongs maybe also to a late Punic amphora (cat. 5.78).
            Some rare fragments of Baetican amphorae were also recognized in the survey material. One wall
      fragment of a Baetican Dressel 20 olive-oil amphora comes from Casa Malati in the lower valley (site
      108), a second one not connected to a site from the upper valley near Pioraco (both not in catalogue)
      (Martin-Kilcher 1987). They must be situated in the 1st or 2nd century AD. In Trea (site 137), two
      Baetican Dressel 7-10 fish-sauce amphorae came to light during the survey (cat. 5.74-5.75). The coarse
      grey brown fabric consisting of many quartz inclusions is similar to that of the Dressel 20 of which
      the production centres are situated in the Lower Guadalquivir Valley. In Ricina (site 140) a resembling
      fabric can be associated to a rim with broken-off handle of an Almagro 50 Baetica/Keay 16A (cat. 5.76).
      The type is almost certainly copied from the Lusitanian Almagro 50 type (or was it vice versa?), which
      is a valuable argument to consider also this type as a container for fish-based products (Monsieur 2016).
      In Brondoleto (site 56), also in the upper valley, a wall fragment of a Beltrán IIA amphora for fish-
      based products turned up (not in catalogue). The greenish exterior and the light brown fine sandy clay
      leaves no doubt about its provenance in the Cadiz region (García Vargas 1998). The Dressel 7-10
      and the Beltrán IIA must be dated in the 1st and the first half of the 2nd century AD, the Almagro 50
      Baetica/Keay 16A probably in the 3rd century AD. With the exception of Casa Malati, it is striking
      that most of these Baetican amphorae were found far inland. The presence of the Baetican amphorae
      in the Potenza valley is probably related to the shipping routes used to provide the Roman military
      settlements in Noricum and Pannonia (Cambi 1983; Bezeczky 1987; Schindler-Kaudelka 2000).


            6.3.4.6. Late Imperial amphorae

            There is an appreciable variety of Late Imperial amphora types in the survey.168 Eastern
      amphorae form an important group. A ribbed neck fragment of a Kapitän II type was picked up at
      Casa Lassandari in the lower valley (site 104; not in catalogue). The precise origin of this typical
      Aegean 3rd century wine amphora is unknown, but the variability in fabrics points to different
      ateliers (Riley 1979, pp. 189-193). ). As the Greek Early Imperial amphorae this type is also widely
      diffused in the Empire. One of the most common types is the Late Roman 1 amphora from Cilicia
      (cat. 5.71; Pieri 2005; Reynolds 2005). Only one fragment of this wine container was found inland,
      on the site of Ricina (site 134), all the others were recovered in the lower Potenza valley, mostly on
      the site of Potentia (site 132). Two fragments were found on the large villa sites of Casa Lassandari
      (site 104) and Casa Birocini (site 125). The Late Roman 2 amphora is represented on almost the
      same sites as the Cilician Late Roman 1. This larger globular amphora with combed ribbings, most
      probably an olive-oil container possibly connected to the annona, was produced on different sites in
      Aegean Greece (cat. 5.72; Pieri 2000). One example of a Late Roman 3 (not in catalogue) was picked
      up in the survey of Potentia. The origin of this elegant dark red brown micaceous wine container is
      situated in the Ephesos region and in the Maeander valley (Pieri 2000). Finally, again on the urban
      site of Potentia, a shoulder fragment of a Late Roman 4 or Gaza amphora of Palestinian origin
      turned up (cat. 5.73; Pieri 2000). The presence in the 5th and the 6th century AD of these eastern
      amphorae, together with other ceramic categories such as Late Roman C or Aegean cooking ware,
      is related as well to the attraction of the new capital Ravenna as to the influence of Constantinople.
      North African amphorae are rather well represented and some were found inland, although the bulk
      is still coming from the lower valley and from the same sites as the Eastern amphorae, i.e. the
      large villa sites and the urban site of Potentia. Among the rare diagnostic fragments only four main
      168
          For an overview of the circulation of Late Imperial amphorae in the Marche region between the 3rd and the 7th century
      AD, see Gamberini 2015.
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                                 193




types were distinguished, but it is clear that the bulk of the recovered wall sherds can conceal a
far wider variety of types. Otherwise the fabric and the technique of African amphorae are rather
easy recognizable, especially their characteristic vertical marks of a spatula applied to smoothen
the surface. Two finds from the grid surveys in Trea and Potentia refer to the Africana I amphora,
a rather small amphora type produced in Byzacena and Tripolitania from the end of the 2nd and
the mid of the 3rd century AD (cat. 5.79; Keay 1984; Bonifay 2004). As for the Kapitän II and the
Almagro 50 Baetica/Keay 16A, the Africana I is a typical representative among the 3rd century AD
amphorae. The larger cylindrical Africana II amphora was defined amongst a few fragments of rims
and spikes (cat. 5.80). It covers partly the 3rd and the 4th century AD. The contents of the Africana
I and II are still a matter of debate, but as for the later types (see below), archaeometric research
carried out on these amphorae points – regardless of the type to the transport of different products,
oil as well as fish-based products (Piquès et al. 2008; Woodworth et al. 2015). However, the number
of finds referring to these ‘classical’ late North African amphorae is limited in comparison with the
later medium and small-sized cylindrical containers. These large cylindrical types are identified
as Africana III/ Keay 25 amphorae. Their diffusion in the lower Potenza valley is wider: besides
Potentia they also occur on sites 104, 106, 114, 116, 125, 126 and 134. Considering the diagnostic
sherds an attribution to all three main subtypes was possible: Keay 25.1/African IIIA, Keay 25.3/
African IIIB, and Keay 25.2/African IIIC (cat. 5.81-5.83; Keay 1984; Bonifay 2004). They are all
characterized by a pronounced everted rim.169 The amphora type Keay 26 with a small everted rim
and a peculiar long spike, better known as spatheion, can be considered as a small version of the
Keay 25 and is also frequent here (cat. 5.84).
      Amphorae from Italic origin are represented by the circulation of Keay 52 and related flat-
bottomed wine containers produced in Bruttium and Sicily from the middle of the 4th to the beginning
of the 6th century AD. Their diffusion is rather limited. Only two fragments from Casa Birocini (site
125) and Potentia (site 132) could be identified in the valley (not in catalogue; Pacetti 1998).
