            6.3.5. Marbles and other stones used as luxury material
            Devi Taelman


            6.3.5.1. Finds from the valley

            This study presents a short overview of the results of the characterisation and identification of
      the stone material used for decorative purposes in the valley of the River Potenza. The material has
      been collected during line and grid surveys carried out by the PVS project. Most material presented
      was found at the Roman towns of Ricina, Potentia and Trea, while a small number of fragments
      were found at rural sites. Material from the Potentia western gate excavations is not discussed.
            The study is part of a larger project that studies the trade, supply mechanisms, use and meaning
      of decorative stone in the wider region of central Adriatic Italy in Roman times.
      Central Adriatic Italy is geologically characterised by very few stone resources of sufficient quality
      for use as decorative stone and to merit being moved around even regionally (see chapter 3).
      Nevertheless, stone was used intensively for embellishing private and public monumental buildings
      at all major urban centres in the region. All these high-quality stones therefore had to be imported.
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                                195




     Table 20 Counts of decorative stone finds from the PVS surveys; veneer weight in gram is indicated between brackets.

                                           Ricina       Potentia Trea              Rural        Total
            Veneer                         94           68       530               46           738
                                           (9550 g)     (6801g)      (35062 g)     (8006 g)     (59419 g)
            Cornice                        2            5            12            2            21
            Column element                 3            0            3             0            6
            Architectural element          0            2            1             0            3
            Inscription                    0            2            1             0            3
            Sculptural element             1            3            2             1            7
            Other                          0            1            1             2            4
            Total                          100          81           551           49           782
            Number of varieties            9            12           17            9            21


      A total of 782 fragments of decorative stone have been collected during the PVS surveys. As
the table shows, most material comes from the surveys of the Roman towns of Ricina, Potentia and
Trea. Only 49 fragments were found at rural sites. Forty-four percent of the stone fragments are grey
marbles, 15 % are white marbles, and 40 % are polychrome stones. The remaining pieces are carved
from white or black limestones that were probably obtained locally or regionally (identification is
based on macroscopic properties). Grey-white, medium- to coarse-grained marble from Proconnesos
and grey, fine-grained bardiglio from the quarries near Luna could clearly be distinguished among
the grey marbles. Not all grey marbles could be identified macroscopically. The white marbles are
generally of high quality and of (almost) pure white, with fine- to coarse-grained crystal sizes.
      More prestigious polychrome lithologies, that were used exclusively for wall décor or
paving application (veneer or cornices), are mostly greco scritto (10 %170), breccia di sciro (8
%), giallo antico (4 %) and to a lesser extent also africano (3 %), fior di pesco (3 %), portasanta
(3 %) and verde antico (3 %). Other polychrome types are alabasters (< 1 %), breccia corallina
(2 %), cipollino verde (1 %), lumachello orientale (< 1 %), pavonazzetto (< 1 %), rosso antico
(2 %) and serpentino (< 1 %). One fragment could not be identified. Most stones were imported
from the coast of Asia Minor, Greece (mainland and Aegean islands) and North Africa (giallo
antico and lumachella orientale). Trea is the only site with some imports from the inland of Asia
Minor (breccia corallina and pavonazzetto). Notable absences are Egyptian imports, apart from
1 veneer fragment in possibly Egyptian alabaster from Trea. This relative absence is especially
remarkable for Trea where excavations have illustrated the presence of a Serapeum sanctuary,
with imports of Egyptian statuettes carved in greywacke from the Wadi Hammamat quarries in
the Eastern Desert.
      Special finds of imported stone include foremost a fragment of a large, white marble, Ionic
style cornice block found in the forum area of Ricina (cat. 8.4) that was part of the monumental
architectural decoration of the town’s main public complex. A white limestone, architectural element
was found just outside the north-western corner of the city walls of the Roman town of Trea (cat.
8.5). On one side, the block displays a sequence of carved, projecting mouldings on three sides,
suggesting a function as a cornice, perhaps part of a column pedestal belonging to a monumental
Roman building. Probably in the 3rd-4th century AD, the block has been reused as a press bed for a
direct screw press. Simpler and smaller cornices, as well as architectural elements, such as fragments
of column shafts (including a fluted column shaft from Trea), capitals, and bases are in white or grey
170
    For veneer, percentages are expressed as percentages of the total assemblage weight (grey marbles, white marbles,
polychrome stones and local/regional stones).
196                                                                                            Devi Taelman




      marble, with the exception of eight small rosso antico and one giallo antico cornices. The rosso
      antico cornices have a widespread distribution around the entire Mediterranean (Lazzarini 2007, p.
      72), and have, for example also been found on the nearby sites of Urbs Salvia (Urbisaglia), Urvinum
      Mataurense (Urbino) and Forum Sempronii (Fossombrone). Three fragments of inscriptions (cat.
      8.1, 8.2, 8.3) are in white marble (2) and giallo antico (1), all three inscribed in Roman capitals
      and probably datable in the 1st or 2nd century AD. Sculptural elements include seven fragments of
      statuary (part of a finger, limb or support, and drapery) and one fragment of a bas-relief with leaf
      decoration. Finally, among the utensils we can note a fragment of a cosmetic palette in fior di pesco
      from Trea, and a small fragment of a white marble lid decorated with a garland motive.
           Differences in the quantities and varieties of imported stone types (especially in veneer) can
      be observed between the three urban sites. While subtle variation in the composition of decorative
      stone assemblages of the three urban sites probably (partly) result from survey circumstances (e.g.
      survey time and number of surveys), obvious differences in the assemblages more likely reflect the
      socio-economic situation.
           The site of Trea by far accounts for the highest quantity and largest variety of stones, with a
      remarkable popularity of polychrome and grey marble veneer and the low amount of white marble
      veneer. Grey marble veneer (Proconnesian, bardiglio, and some greco scritto) collected in the area
      of the forum basilica certainly formed part of the building’s wall and pavement decoration. For
      Trea, the large percentage of breccia di sciro (10 %) is also noted, especially considering that this
      material is not found at Ricina and only in small amount at Potentia. Most fragments of decorative
      stone were found in the area of the basilica and the eastern part of the forum complex (main
      temple and portico). In the temple area, white marble, greco scritto, fior di pesco and breccia
      di sciro form the bulk of the decorative stone material. Grey marble, africano, giallo antico and
      portasanta are missing near the temple and have been found mainly around the basilica or at the
      forum portico.
           At Ricina a high percentage of greco scritto and low percentage of polychrome veneer is
      recorded. Most decorative stone material was collected near the forum baths (as identified by the
      remote sensing approaches), with especially abundant white marble, and greco scritto, giallo antico
      and verde antico for the polychrome stones. The forum and temple square was the area where
      most grey marbles was found, suggesting its use as most popular stone for decorating this public,
      monumental building complex.
           For Potentia, we can conclude that portasanta and giallo antico were widely used (10 % and 9
      % of the total). Most fragments were found just north and west of the forum square, in the area where
      geophysics located several important buildings (e.g. the probable main temple of the city). This is
      also the only zone in the town where all lithotypes have been collected.
           Even though the other material categories (essentially fine pottery) sometimes reveal rich villa
      complexes, the rural sites provided very little to no decorative stone material. The rural temple near
      Ricina, Villa Potenza – Cimarella 1 (S0098), delivered six fragments of veneer (white marble, grey
      marble and one fragment of breccia di sciro). At the villa of Casa Lassandari (S0104), 27 veneer
      fragments were found: 18 are greco scritto, 7 white marble and 2 grey marble. A fragment of cipollino
      verde from Mergnano S. Pietro 2 (S0034), a fragment of serpentino from Le Casette 4 (S0009) and
      a white marble basin from Montarice villa (S0101) are also worth mentioning. This absence of
      stone decoration can be due to a limited visibility of the material during the surveys (although this
      is unlikely given the distinct visual properties of the stones), the absence of stone decoration in rural
      central Adriatic Italy, or spoliation for reuse and/or lime production. The latter has, for example,
      been documented by late Roman and post-Roman lime kilns at several sites in the Marche region,
      including at Ricina and Potentia (Ullrich 2013; Vermeulen et al. 2011, pp. 203-205).
6. The changing material culture of a valley                                                                              197




                                                                                                            Fig. 117
                                                                                                            Provenance of the
                                                                                                            decorative stones
                                                                                                            found in the PVS
                                                                                                            surveys.


     The results of the decorative stone study of the PVS surveys show a strong integration of the
region in the Mediterranean marble trade with a wide array of stone types originating from Italy,
Greece (mainland and Aegean islands), Asia Minor and North Africa (Fig. 117). The most popular
polychrome lithotypes along the river Potenza are greco scritto, breccia di sciro and giallo antico.
Remarkable absences are stones from Egypt. With the exception of greco scritto being used more
frequently at the Potenza valley urban sites, the results of the stone study corresponds rather nicely
with the results reported for three other urban centres (Urbs Salvia/Urbisaglia just to the south, and
Forum Sempronii /Fossombrone and Suasa/Castelleone di Suasa in the northern Marche region)
for which stone provenance and characterisation studies have been carried out (Antonelli 2006,
2007; Antonelli et al. 2013; Antonelli, Lazzarini 2002, 2013b; Capedri et al. 2001). Compared
to nearby Urbs Salvia where cipollino mandolato from the Pyrenees was a very popular stone, the
absence of this stone type in the PVS surveys is surprising, even though its use is very rare outside
Gallia (Antonelli, Lazzarini 2013b). While polychrome stones were used almost exclusively for
veneering, the surveys have illustrated that imported stone (essentially white and grey marbles)
were used together with (local) limestones for producing architectural elements such as columns
and cornices.


     6.3.5.2 Wider context

     The earliest known marble imports in central Adriatic Italy are a group of funerary, Hellenistic
stelae bearing Greek inscriptions from one of the necropoleis of Ancona. The stelae can be dated
between the second half of the 2nd and the early decades of the 1st centuries BC. The archaeometric
analysis illustrated that 11 stelae were carved in Parian marble from the Lakkoi quarries (Paros-2(3)),
two are in Luna marble, 1 is in Proconnesian marble, and 1 is carved from the local white limestone
from the Monte Conero (Antonelli, Lazzarini 2013a). These results demonstrate a clear link
between central Adriatic Italy and the Greek world, in particular Delos, in the form of material and
craftsmanship imports. Moreover, the identification of Luna marble for the two stelae from Ancona
provides the first evidence that the distribution of this marble was not limited to central western Italy
in pre-Roman times.
198                                                                                           Devi Taelman




            A general remark for the Imperial period is that there exists a clear distinction between the
      import of architectural marbles and sculptural marbles. From the currently available dataset, it is
      clear that the sculptural imports have their peak in the Late Republican and Julio-Claudian period,
      while the late 1st century AD and the 2nd century AD is the most flourishing period for the use of
      marble as architectural decoration in central Adriatic Italy. Good examples are the Arch of Trajan in
      Ancona in Proconnesian marble (Attanasio et al. 2003) and the rebuilding and lavish polychrome
      marble decoration in the oecus of the house of the Coiedii in Suasa (Castelleone di Suasa), dated to
      the early 2nd century AD, with adjustment in the 3rd century AD (Capedri et al. 2001; Dall’Aglio
      et al. 2007, pp. 192-193, 200). It needs to be remarked that the import of Proconnesian marble for
      architectural purposes predates the use of this stone type in the western part of the Italic Peninsula.
      For example, in Rome, Proconnesian marble is attested in large quantities only from the early 2nd
      century AD onwards. In this perspective, the use of Proconnesian marble for the capitals of the
      temple of Salus Augustae in Urbs Salvia (Urbisaglia) (Antonelli, Lazzarini 2013b) and especially
      for the construction of the Arch of Trajan in Ancona are important testimonies of the early use
      of Proconnesian marble. Proconnesos appears to be the prime supplier of architectural marble for
      other large-scale building projects in central Adriatic Italy, as is further testified in the theatre of
      Urvinum Mataurense (Urbino) and at Forum Sempronii (Fossombrone) (unpublished results). This
      probably results from the ease by which this architectural marble par excellence could reach the
      region through the Adriatic, certainly in comparison with Luna marble that either had to be exported
      overland over the Apennines or contouring the entire Italian peninsula.

        Grey marbles and polychrome decorative stones

      8.6     Breccia di sciro
      Skyros, Greece. Breccia of with sheared white, pink or red fine-grained calcitic marble clasts and
      red hematitic calcite matrix.
8. Finds catalogue                                                                                            309




8.7      Cipollino verde
Euboea, Greece. Sheared and folded calcitic marble with chlorite-rich bands giving the marble it
green colour.

8.8      Fior di pesco
Eretria, Greece. Heavily crushed and sheared marble or breccia with brownish-red-purple matrix
and white marble clasts or white veins.

8.9        Portasanta
Chios, Greece. Fine-grained, brecciated limestone or low-grade marble, heavily fractured with white
calcite-filled fractures and abundant stylolites. Colours are predominantly dull red, salmon-pink and grey.

8.10   Rosso antico
Mani Peninsula, Greece. Fine-grained, impure calcitic marble coloured by hematite and traces of
manganese. Interspersed bands of white marble are frequent.

8.11    Serpentino
Levetsova, Greece. Porphyritic andesite or dolerite with epidote and chlorite silicate minerals.

8.12     Verde antico
Larisa, Greece. Breccia-conglomerate with clasts of serpentinite, schist and marble and serpentine
and calcite matrix.

8.13     Africano
Teos, Turkey. Limestone breccia or conglomerate with limestone multi-coloured limestone clasts
and a black, brown of greyish-green matrix. Fractures are infilled with white calcite or quartz.

8.14     Breccia coralline
Vezirhan, Turkey. Breccia with an orange, salmon-pink or brown hematitic matrix and (creamy-)
white limestone clasts.

8.15     Greco scritto
Probably Ephesos, Turkey. Medium-grained tectonised calcitic marble with fine- to medium-grained
grey-black graphite veins.

8.16     Pavonazzetto
Afyon, Turkey. Breccia with medium-grained white marble clasts with diffuse boundaries and violet
or bluish calcitic matrix.

8.17     Proconnesian marble
Marmara, Turkey, Asia Minor. Medium- to coarse-grained calcitic marble with grey bands of
graphite, magnetite and pyrite.

8.18     Bardiglio
Carrara, Italy. Fine-grained calcitic grey marble, coloured by disseminated graphite.

8.19     Giallo antico
Chemtou, Tunisia. Brecciated fine-grained calcareous limestone with white, yellow to orange-brown
clasts and orange-brown to red matrix. Monochrome examples occur.
Jurassic age.
310                                                                               Patrick Monsieur et al.




      8.20    Lumachella orientale
      Henchir Kasbat, Tunisia. Limestone with abundant fossils (mainly oyster, gastropod and
      foraminifera).

      8.21  Alabaster
      Unknown provenance. Yellow alabaster and banded travertine alabaster.

      8.22  Unidentified stone
      Unknown provenance. Igneous rock with red phenocrysts.

      9. General conclusions: the changing political, cultural and economic complexity of an
      Adriatic valley
      Frank Vermeulen



      T     he data presented in this book result from a quite intense and interdisciplinary team effort, spread
            over a 16 year time span, to reach some broad understanding of settlement dynamics in the crucial
      transition period between later Prehistory and the early historical phases of the formation of an Adriatic
      valley landscape. This research exercise has been based primarily on a wide range of non-invasive field
      operations, fully integrating the legacy data at our disposal and incorporating, since the conception of
      the project in 2000, a range of evolving technologies of archaeological fieldwork and desktop analysis.
      We are aware that the limitations and biases inherent to the chosen non-invasive approaches could
      only be partially overcome and that the inevitable sampling strategy to tackle a valley of more than 80
      kilometres long could only result in a patchy and incomplete picture of the past. In the course of the
      PVS project, which started as a prospection program mainly based on archaeological line walking and
      associated surface pottery analysis, enhanced by some aerial photography and broad geomorphological
      terrain observations, these limitations of the surface record were gradually dealt with. Recurrent site
      visits with higher resolution artefact collections on settlements found earlier by the team, very intense
      active aerial prospections as follow-up of the field surveys and also to detect many more ancient sites
      and landscape features, targeted geoarchaeological investigations with augerings and other detailed
      field observations, wide-scale and sometimes full coverage geophysical prospections on a broad
      range of sites, and finally some targeted excavations to obtain stratigraphic confirmations of surface
      phenomena and a more reliable reference collection for the regional artefact studies, are among the
      most important strategies employed to deal with traditional biases and the restricted view imposed by
      selection and sampling in a landscape. The harvest of all these integrated strategies is therefore not
      meagre and some real advances in the study of human occupation of this part of central Italy were
      obtained. Hereunder, we will briefly summarise some of the more important observations, with respect
      to the wider implications of settlement dynamics and moves towards settlement complexity in the
      region. Some of these observations were already developed in a recent synthesis on the development of
      settlement complexity and urbanism in the wider central Adriatic region (Vermeulen 2017), to which
      we refer for further bibliographic referencing.
        For the first part of the chosen timeline, the Iron Age, a certain breakthrough was obtained in
      characterising some of the settlement patterns that evolved between the beginnings of the last
      millennium before our era and the coming of Rome in the 3rd century. This long period of Italic pre- and
      protohistory was and is in the Marche region notoriously ill-documented for its settlement organisation
      and transformation, but the newly obtained information on site hierarchies and dispersion, and on
      the characterisation of some of the more focal settlements and aggregate sites, now allows to deepen
      the knowledge base and to point out useful directions for future field research, hopefully including
      well-targeted large scale excavations. The need for better and more detailed reference collections of
      datable pottery and other settlement artefacts remains however high, and a long way is still ahead
      before the study of Picene and eastern Umbrian settlement behaviour can attain levels of comparison
      with the information we already have on more advanced societies in the western parts of central Italy.
      Nevertheless, important observations can be made about the changing political, cultural and economic
      complexity of the societies inhabiting the valley throughout these centuries of increasing contact with
      higher societies on their fringes, such as those of Etruria and Lazio towards the west and of Greek and
      later Celtic cultures predominantly along the Adriatic.
        In the more mountainous and hilly eastern and central zones of the Potenza valley habitation long
      remained organised in small village-type settlements increasingly dominated by aristocratic segments
      of society. In the upper and middle valley sections the archaeological data point at a protohistoric
9. General conclusions: the changing political, cultural and economic complexity of an Adriatic valley        383




society that is but a weak reflection of what happens in the more central and western parts of adjoining
Umbria. There we see that out of a group of potentates, who based their power on a warrior ideology
and on strong individually organised ties with clientes, develops a new class-organised oligarchy that
finds its power base in the growing proto-urban settlements of the 7th/6th century BC and later. In this
new type of settlement, they find the social structure and institutional centralisation needed for keeping
cohesion among townsfolk and for controlling larger and more stable territories. The gradual growth
of such centralised settlement also allows the organisation of the first urban planning efforts, such as
the construction of common defences (e.g. Cesi, Spoleto) and the erection of monumental sanctuaries,
thanks also to the creation of more surplus which is now no longer frozen and locked up in tombs.
The greater impact on the territory can be seen by the creation of new satellite settlements located in
strategic positions for commerce and control, such as on riversides or river crossings. In the Apennine
parts of ancient Umbria (including the upper Potenza valley) and in the bordering Picene districts (such
as the middle Potenza valley) such developments can only be seen very gradually from the 4th century
onwards, particularly in parts of the valleys where control over exchanges between the Tyrrhenian and
Adriatic sides of Italy could best be organised. Nevertheless, in locations like Matelica and Camerino,
whose spheres of influence reached that part of the Potenza corridor where our upper valley field
transect is located, important moves towards such higher complexity can be detected from at least
the 7th century BC onwards. Especially the situation in Matelica is one of the best archaeologically
known of this Umbrian-Picene contact area. Here a strong demographic boom is visible from the 7th
century onwards with the development of a series of settlements and associated cemeteries located on
river terraces near the River Esino. These various ‘hamlets’ seem associated with a habitation centre
on the hilltop where the modern town is now situated. From the cemeteries with their rich 7th and 6th
century burials, some with tumuli and ditched circular enclosures, we see the will of certain individuals
or family groups to make their special position in society clearly visible. This includes the expression
of a series of late orientalising characteristics – two-wheeled chariots, certain weaponry and vessels for
symposium banqueting – connected with the adoption of a ‘Greek’ lifestyle by the elite, as it is well
dispersed among the aristocrats of the Tyrrhenian side of the Italian peninsula. The Etruscan origin of
some of the fine grave goods makes clear that these warriors of the highlands had strong economic,
social and cultural links with the southern Etruscan aristocracy, with whom they probably exchanged
gifts within a context of reciprocity. Apart from these imports, we also see in these rich cemeteries the
appearance of refined objects in bronze, bone and amber, the products of local workshops probably
producing for the small aristocracies of the area. A crucial import component at the site appears to
be technology that allowed the exploitation of new techniques and styles by local craftsmen. These
communities no doubt played a major role in the redistribution and organisation of exchange between
the central Marche and the other side of the Apennines and the Tiber valley. Contrary to what happens
on that other side, however, their 6th century BC schemes of settlement structure persist into the 4th
century. It is true that around 500 BC we see here, as elsewhere in Marche, the adoption of new
building techniques, with now fully rectangular houses instead of the large huts with rounded short
sides, and the adoption of stone bases for walls and of terracotta roof-tiles. But only a century or so
later, in the early 4th century BC, the old system of scattered settlement nuclei with their associated
cemeteries lying in peripheral areas is gradually abandoned, and a concentration of habitation is now
visible over the full river terrace. Here, on higher ground with good visibility and control potential, the
centralised settlement developed further, at a gradual pace, while only much later, in the 2nd century
BC, was it walled and condensed into the Roman-looking town Matilica.
  For Camerino a very similar scenario is likely. The position of this currently still inhabited central
site, located on a hilltop that visually dominates the north-south intra-montane basin and lying quite
centrally between the upper Chienti and Potenza valleys, was ideal for the formation of the already
described territorial nucleation. Finds in its wide settlement chamber – well defined north and south
by the two rivers, and east and west by mountain ridges – show that the protohistoric habitation centre
384                                                                                             Frank Vermeulen




      under the modern town was the focus of at present still poorly-datable dispersed farms, which took
      part in the exploitation of this agriculturally suitable territory. At the north-western mountainous
      edge of the territory, near the narrow gorge leading to the Potenza valley uplands, the landscape was
      visually dominated by the impressive hilltop site of Monte Primo, which we interpreted here as a
      periodical aggregation site for special activities (feasting, cattle mustering etc.) and as a communal
      sanctuary active since the Bronze Age. Such mountain-top sanctuaries, often connected with seasonal
      transhumance activities, are typical for the central Apennines, especially in Samnium and Umbria
      and they generally have a long duration of use. Our archaeological data are currently insufficient to
      understand how long Monte Primo was used as sacred site for the valley communities but the place
      was surely very suited for the ritual observation of the flight of birds and the auspicio, one of the
      crucial elements of ancient Umbrian religion, as is essentially known from the Iguvine Tables found
      at Gubbio. Furthermore, the climb to the top, with its elaborate system of causeways and enclosures,
      seems to have been part of the rituals performed at the sanctuary. Such ‘archaic’ and deep-rooted
      traditions probably only gradually declined in the period leading up to the Roman conquest, when
      the hilltop of modern Camerino becomes the new focus for the proto-urbanisation (e.g. with Etrusco-
      Faliscan style temples) taking place there in the course of the 4th century BC. The presence here,
      much earlier than elsewhere in Apennine Umbria, of such architectural schemes of monumentalisation,
      possibly pre-dating the Roman incorporation of the area in 310 BC (Liv. IX, 36), might also be the
      result of the economic success of this community. Since the mid-4th -century shift in the commercial
      flows within the Umbrian region, thanks to the opening up of Gallic markets and the development of
      Ancona after the coming of Syracusians, this site might have profited much more than before from its
      controlling position. The location at the exact crossroads of the EW oriented Potenza valley, linking
      northern Latium and southern Etruria to the Adriatic, and the north-south oriented intra-montane basin,
      connecting eastern Umbria with the Celtic north, could explain this apparent boom.
        In the middle Potenza valley other potent Iron Age centres developed, north of the river on top of
      Monte Pitino and directly south of the stream on Monte Franco. The first was between the 7th and the
      3rd centuries the central place of a vast territory, visually well controlled from its impressive hilltop.
      The permanent nucleated settlement was surrounded by a series of cemeteries, which yielded some
      very wealthy graves, demonstrating that the settlement of Monte Pitino had a pivotal role in the control
      and organisation of transport, through the strategic corridor of the Potenza valley, of luxury goods to
      and from Etruria and Latium, as well as from Greece and the eastern Mediterranean. At least from the
      6th to the 4th century the central place was also surrounded by a high number of more or less isolated
      farms and loosely dispersed rural hamlets. It is not unlikely that, within this fertile and environmentally
      diversified ‘settlement chamber’ of the middle Potenza valley and adjoining hills, the emphasis of
      power and economic activities shifted throughout the 4th and 3rd centuries BC from the hilltop centre
      to lower locations near the Potenza river. Here, at the edge of its territory, already existing satellite sites
      – some with a prominent past going back to later Bronze Age – were now expanding. The strategic
      area of Passo di Treia, some 7 km east of Monte Pitino, where the Potenza corridor narrows and control
      of transported goods, as well as easy river crossing, were facilitated, is the best proof of this. Here, on
      the opposite side of the river, was located the small Bronze Age and Iron Age hilltop site. This central
      settlement is since long known for its rich Picene cemetery of Moie di Pollenza and now, thanks to
      the new surveys, also for substantial Late Iron Age and Roman occupation, in its later phase perhaps
      aggregated as a ‘village’, that flourished thanks to the river crossing in the shadow of Monte Franco.
      It is likely that this area depended at some time on the ruling communities living on the Monte Pitino,
      but that it gradually acquired more importance due to its position on the route through the valley, a
      topographic asset that would be much enhanced after the Roman conquest. Such enhancement also
      occurred at other possible satellites of Monte Pitino. One of these is the Picene predecessor of the later
      Roman town of Septempeda, which possibly evolved out of a healing water sanctuary on the spring-
      line at the foot of a small promontory overlooking the valley floor. A second is the Picene predecessor
9. General conclusions: the changing political, cultural and economic complexity of an Adriatic valley        385




of later Roman Trea, located on a hilly plateau and along an ancient route linking the middle Potenza
valley with the commercially blooming area of the Monte Conero, south of Ancona.
  The Iron Age development of the lower Potenza valley and especially of the coastal zone was
much influenced by the more rapid transformations the whole of the Adriatic area went through. The
surroundings of the limestone massive of Monte Conero, with its natural incisions offered stable and
safe harbours, which at least since the time of Mycenaean commercial visits to the Adriatic attracted
many long-distance ships and their goods. The well sheltered northern and southern shores especially,
where the permanent grouped settlements of Ancona and Numana developed into maritime emporia,
linked the merchants visiting the Conero to the Umbrian, Picene and later Celtic communities of these
surroundings. Especially the openness to Greek culture and international contacts, starting from the
6th century BC and continuing thereafter, not only directly touched these two main harbour sites or
their immediate hinterland, but also the nearby areas along the Adriatic coastline. The evidence from
many finds of Greek imports in Picenum, especially along its northern valleys, allows us to enlarge
the mercantile reasons of these Aegeans, such as the acquisition of metal from Etruria and finished
products from the Etruscan towns near the Po plain and certain centres of Picenum, and the massive
export of Greek wine to central and northern Italy and transalpine Europe. The Greek imports in central
Adriatic Italy comprised, apart from amphorae with wine, above all fine pottery, especially symposium
vessels; but also Greek bronzes, refined furniture, artistic objects (e.g. marble statuary and stelae),
jewellery and ivory goods, were deposited in the rich tombs of Numana and of the central settlements
along the valleys of the interior, which channelled many of these goods to the Tyrrhenian side and in
particular to the Tiber basin. Certain categories of goods, especially Attic pottery, have also been well
attested in connected settlement contexts, and there is no doubt that other goods commercialised by
the Greeks, such as probably honey, textiles and oil, also found buyers among the local populations.
The autochthonous Italic involvement in these floods of goods to the inland is particularly evident near
the mouths of larger rivers like the Cesano, Misa and Potenza, where simultaneous control of, and
participation in, maritime transactions could well be combined with deep inland transport along the
valley corridors. This is certainly the case for the situation near the mouth of the river Potenza, where
about one kilometre north of the presumed Iron Age river bed protohistoric people made good use of a
geomorphologic unit consisting of a flat elliptical hilltop plateau with some 7 hectares living space and
steep sides: the site of Montarice. Our intensive survey demonstrated the presence here of habitation
since the Middle Bronze Age and the increasing size of the settlement possibly points at almost
uninterrupted occupation until at least Late Republican times. Most important was the pre-Roman
occupation from the 7th/6th century BC onwards, revealed by local pottery but also by Attic and other
imports, attested on the plateau itself, as well as on the southern terraces towards the river plain. The
plateau settlement was, during its largest extension surrounded by imposing defences, presumably a
combination of walls and ditches. Inside the enclosed settlement remote sensing operations revealed
plans of large rectangular houses, some seemingly with sunken floor and some with wall alignments
made of posts, and a discrete regular organisation can be supposed. Settlement activities were also
present in the nearby valley floor and especially on the dryer beach rich just north of the river mouth,
where possibly a satellite of the defended village of Montarice existed. This could have functioned
as a landing-stage near the Potenza estuary channelling intense Greek commercial contacts and
imports from the area around Monte Conero into the valley corridor during the 5th/4th century BC.
The place marks the same spot where the Roman colony of Potentia was ultimately laid out during
the early 2nd century BC, without proof for a direct link between this Picene occupation and the
later Roman developments.
  As was expected, much more reliable and high resolution data were gathered for the Roman period
in the valley, which was from the start also the central focus in the research strategy of the project. The
observed densities of rural settlements obtained thanks to the successful combination of fieldwalking,
aerial survey and geophysical prospections, the high resolution mapping of the four Roman towns
386                                                                                           Frank Vermeulen




      resulting from the integrated urban surveys, and the study of the much more diagnostic artefacts
      picked up during the field prospections, all contribute to a quite intense view on settlement dynamics,
      changing material culture and enhanced complexity of society. The project was able to shed light on the
      initially slow and only from the 2nd century onwards much intensified colonisation process organised
      by Roman power in this part of Adriatic Italy. From the surface record in this valley it looks as if the
      military annexation by Rome of the majority of central Adriatic Italy during the first part of the 3rd
      century BC had no drastic impact on the populations living in the area. The apparent rapid colonisation
      of a series of new town centres on or near the Adriatic coast during the 3rd century BC (e.g. Ariminum,
      Sena Gallica, Firmum) is paralleled by a comparably slow change in the organisation and occupation
      of the valleys which were not affected by this initial military organisation. Despite persistent change
      in the agricultural territories of the Latin and Roman colonies near the coast, and more gradually
      in the wider development of a new road infrastructure for the organisation of the conquered lands
      between the Apennines and the coast, life in the Potenza valley did not seem to have undergone fast
      transformations. Nevertheless the creation of a road system linking the colonies to Rome, and the
      organisation of collection points for taxes, markets and harbours, where more standardised modes
      of payment and measures or weights are gradually introduced, will have had a more indirect impact
      on settlement dynamics in the interior of the region, and to a certain extent also on the evolution of
      allied population centres and their territories. This was further enhanced when a process started of
      populating the wider conquered central Adriatic territories directly possessed by Rome with a massive
      influx of viritane colonists, soon after the vote in 232 BC in the Roman senate of the lex de agro
      Gallico et Piceno viritim dividundo. Together with the opening of the Via Flaminia in 220 BC, and
      thereafter the development of important diverticula from this road, such as the one through the Potenza
      valley, this created the definitive conditions for full rural infill by newcomers in this region. The latter
      would eventually also lead to a gradual and semi-organic process of roadside village formation in the
      inner valley and to the creation by Rome, after the passage of Hannibal, of a colony for Roman citizens
      on the mouth of the river Potenza.
       The survey data, combined with what is known from stratigraphic interventions in the coastal area,
      show that here the impact of Roman intervention, and settling of immigrant populations, is much
      more tangible than in the inland parts of the valley. The foundation conditions and main topographic
      details of the colonial city of Potentia are now fairly well documented archaeologically, and need not
      be summarised here. The initial colony of 184 BC was soon, within the span of a decade, organised
      as a fully equipped city, with a circuit wall and gates, a regular and well equipped street system, a
      temple, a forum, and a series of commercial buildings. The town must almost from the start have had
      a very commercial and maritime vocation. The many imports dated to the 2nd century BC, such as
      Rhodian amphorae and other Aegean goods found at Potentia, have sufficiently demonstrated that the
      settlement was well integrated in Mediterranean commercial circuits, and it may even be assumed
      that it had some harbour facilities from the foundation of the colony onwards. But also the immediate
      hinterland was well organised. Apart from some hamlets located close to the colony, including the
      (now reorganised ?) village of Montarice, the suburbium of the new town soon saw an expanding rural
      population, inhabiting the valley floor with its regular centuriated landscape as well as the hillslopes
      and crests bordering the Potenza fertile valley corridor. Apart from organising their houses inside the
      town, a large proportion of the Roman colonists and their families – possibly between 60 and 80 %
      of them – must have immediately taken up residence in and around their allotted plots of land in the
      adjoining territory. Here the settlers no doubt immediately started to drain the surrounding plain and
      marshy lands by digging ditches, while perhaps being supported through the first seasons by way of
      some state provisioning of food. Palynological evidence collected during our excavations at the west
      gate of Potentia, also suggests that very soon after their arrival large tracts of the remaining woodland
      were cut in the lower Potenza plain. This was not only needed for town building, but also to prepare
      the new territory for an agricultural exploitation which increased in intensity throughout the whole
9. General conclusions: the changing political, cultural and economic complexity of an Adriatic valley          387




late Republic and early Imperial times. Some of the fourteen Late Republican farms discovered near
Potentia via our prospections, and all built within a radius of 6 km outside the town limits, have indeed
procured good dating evidence of a start in the first half of the 2nd century BC, within the timeframe
of the colonial foundation. Most of these farms were found on plots quite near to the valley floor, but
outside the centuriation grid and in the fertile zones of moderate hilly land that was very suitable for wine
cultivation. In fact, the surface materials on several of them provide good evidence for wine amphora
production associated with farms of the first generation. Kiln residuals of locally produced Greco-Italic
amphorae are found here in surface concentrations together with other diagnostic pottery, such as black
gloss, cooking ware from Campania, and Rhodian amphorae, demonstrating at the same time the good
integration of these communities in the Roman commercial system. The artefact studies of material
from Roman farm sites and the development known from town contexts and architecture in Potentia
show well that this coastal environment remained a relatively flourishing one until Mid-Imperial times,
despite the normal ups and downs of political and economic developments on the larger scale of the
Empire. As in many other Italian areas a particular boost was given during the rule of Augustus and
the settling of additional veteran colonists in the coastal town no doubt amplified the potential for
economic prosperity in the lower valley area. As overseas markets were gradually taken over by wine
exports from the lower Po valley we see however a downward trend from Augustan times onwards
in certain segments of the agricultural prosperity. This resembles what happened with the main wine
producers on the Tyrrhenian side, which probably means that the central Adriatic wine industry had to
shift from long-distance trade towards regional provisioning, and investments were spread over other
productions and means of commerce. This must surely have affected a coastal town like Potentia, but
according to the field survey data in the eastern part of its territory the rural settlement pattern did not
fundamentally change during the first two centuries of the Principate. The town remained surrounded
by a series of single farmsteads, particularly clustered on the hillslopes and near the edge of the valley
plain. In the valley bottom of the Potenza River, where regular land division prevailed, lesser densities
were observed, but this might be due to a geomorphological bias in the surface record. At the end of
the Republic and during the 1st century AD, settlement and peripheral structures still intensified in the
suburban area of Potentia. The first kilometre or so outside the city walls remained mostly free of rural
habitation and seems to have hosted a series of peripheral facilities and functions for the town. The
three outgoing roads here were increasingly lined with funerary monuments and the corresponding
cemeteries expanded now further away from these transport axes, that also served a series of industrial
activities, such as amphora workshops, and grassland areas for grazing livestock and orchards for the
town supply in fruits and vegetables. Outside this first peripheral town zone the wider agricultural
land stretched over the valley bottom and the hills. An even denser network of Roman farms and some
larger villae rusticae than in the previous period has been revealed here. Many farms and villas were
typically located on well positioned hill ridges and slopes near the edge of the valley plain, where
wine and olive cultivation on terraced slopes was favourable. According to the dates from the surface
survey the maximum occupation density of rural settlements in this suburbium was in the 1st century
AD when a full range of sites, from small house units to larger villae, populated the landscape. During
the 2nd century BC there was no further growth in settlement numbers, but only a limited decrease.
 As stated above, change in the middle and upper valleys as a result of the Roman takeover of the Picene
and Umbrian areas was a more gradual process. In most of the Potenza valley distinct 3rd century sites
with datable Roman presence are still quite rare, but this observation is also associated with the still
more difficult archaeological visibility of the surface contexts. Although it does not immediately look
as if this part of north Picenum was much affected by the early viritane assignations of 232 BC, things
would soon start to change thereafter. Initially the first settlers seem to cluster in certain nodal sites in
the river valleys and on the developing road network. Here, the possibly mixed villages of colonists
and local populations, would, during the 2nd and 1st centuries BC, transform into real agglomerations,
and eventually towns. The road organisation, together with choices made by the Roman state and
388                                                                                         Frank Vermeulen




      the new migrant communities to establish and develop small centres away from the colonial towns,
      was crucial for the fate of existing indigenous centres, like Monte Pitino and Monte Franco, or their
      satellites. Some gradually disappeared, while others took in the new influences, often at different speed,
      according to their relationship with the conquering forces or their particular position in the landscape
      or district. Especially those that hosted praefecturae, the headquarters of those outlying offices (e.g.
      the tribunal) that were indispensable to civilian life far from Rome, had the best chances for survival
      and development. These praefecturae were obviously located where the population was densest and
      consequently became an incentive for the growth of such settlements. It is possible that the later towns
      of Ricina, Trea and Septempeda had such an official origin, but there is no textual proof for this so far.
      But even if we now know much more of the topography and organisation of these new ‘roadside’ cities,
      which only received municipal status around the middle of the 1st century BC, they are no doubt rooted
      in a complex synergy of clustering settlers from Tyrrhenian Italy and their descendants on one side,
      and displaced or locally moved indigenous population groups on the other. Indeed, especially after the
      mid-2nd century do we see that – in certain former indigenous centres and small new agglomerations
      alike – the roots were present for a deeper urbanism mirroring the socio-economic urban models of
      Rome and of the nearby colonies such as Potentia, Auximum and Pollentia/Urbs Salvia. This model
      was applied in the centres of the region in a flexible way, and not always on the same scale or with the
      full range of options. The materialisation of a change in mentality, but most of all in the demographic
      composition of the centralised communities, can be seen via the absorption and application of urban
      features such as: stone town walls in opus reticulatum (at Trea) or opus quadratum (at Septempeda)
      technique, the regular structuring of houses and plots on the main road axis (well demonstrated at
      Ricina), more comfortable and better built housing, ‘Latin’ types of temples inside the agglomeration
      or town, etc. Some of these material developments were of course made possible due to the expansion
      of Roman power to the East supplying the wealth to finance such initiatives to Italy, but also by the
      increasing efficiency of Late Republican agricultural and commercial practice in the region. Especially
      since in the course of the 1st century BC this gradual process of economic integration must have
      accelerated and intensified when the many new municipia developed into well-structured market
      centres, and new waves of veteran colonists joined them. The role as economic foci for markets and
      trade fully defines these Roman towns now as places within the local economy, and as centres which
      became the engine for the better integration of the region in the international flows of goods. At the
      same time the countryside densified and flourished, and the rural infill observed in the survey record
      illustrates this well, deep into Imperial times. It must be accepted that the majority of products handled
      through the towns of the valley were essentially of local or regional origin, in particular the mass
      of food products obtained from agricultural activity in the farms and villas of the countryside, from
      profitable exploitation of the nearby sea, and from intensified activity in the higher mountain areas,
      where the collective use of the ager publicus was indispensable for the practice of pastoral activities.
      While for instance in Etruria and Campania, towns and territories were greatly dependent on the
      Late Republican wine business, with huge investments by the Roman elite, and with a remarkable
      decline visible from the mid-1st century AD onwards as a result of international competition and
      shifting foci, this part of the central Adriatic region probably suffered less these major fluctuations.
      Here the more mixed economy was smaller in scale and less unpredictable, and thus less affected by
      changes in modes of exploitation. This helps to explain the quite stable continuity of rather dense
      rural and, therefore, also urban settlement throughout the general period of 90 BC-AD 200, even if
      some change in exploitation (e.g. shifts towards larger farms and villas) is clearly perceptible in the
      archaeological record.
        Developments in the Potenza valley during Late Antiquity, up to the 6th century AD, are again
      somewhat less well documented. The reasons for this are multiple, but next to the gradual decrease in
      archaeological visibility most explanations are related to broader developments in the Roman Empire.
      As elsewhere in the region and in Italy a crucial factor in the transformation of the Potenza valley
9. General conclusions: the changing political, cultural and economic complexity of an Adriatic valley         389




towns had been the involvements of the elite, who through great deeds of euergetism took their chances
for political promotion. The size of the four identified urban centres in the valley was relatively small
(between 12 and 22 ha), but the creation of urbanitas was in the coastal colony and in the three inland
municipia a rapid and quite impressive affair. Their capacity to apply models from outside and adapt
them to the exigencies of their cityscape typifies the new towns of the region. The identified fora,
temples, theatres, amphitheatres, macella, baths, gate buildings, etc. show that these towns were all
well provided with urban infrastructures, which in many cases must have impressed the visitor. But
ultimately, in the course of the 2nd century AD, the system of large scale sponsoring of architecture
and infrastructural needs in the towns changed, and the overall level of building activity declined.
Inscriptions from the region, such as from Ricina, show that especially inland towns and probably their
associated rural areas already suffered during different phases of the 2nd century AD, and that as in
many other parts of Italy, these Adriatic towns were not able to fully uphold their decorum and keep up
their infrastructure. Private euergetism connected with public architecture and infrastructure of towns
became quite limited in the 2nd century and this was only partly remedied by direct Imperial intervention
and donations, mostly for restoration works on important buildings or essential infrastructure, such as
streets, aqueducts, baths and temples, and rarely if ever on new architecture. It is likely that the Potenza
valley inland towns, where the public infrastructure was somewhat disproportional compared to their
real importance as population centres and markets for a wider territory, did not do well once the system
of euergetism weakened and elite spending partly shifted to villas and family tombs in the countryside.
The best example in the valley for understanding the progressive decline from the 3rd century onwards
in public and private expenditure on urban monuments, and the steady erosion of civic amenities and
infrastructure, is Potentia. Firstly it must be observed that almost no late Roman settlement material
was recovered in the immediate extramural areas, where, by contrast, early Roman finds are frequently
found. This is a clear indication of a shrinking city that also transformed much in its intra-mural areas.
Here it seems that during the 4th and the first half of the 5th centuries, the bulk of the walled area was
still occupied. From the mid-5th century onwards, however, occupation probably contracted, since
material becomes restricted to the northern area of the city, the central area around the forum and
the south-central zone to its south. During the first half of the 6th century AD, the occupation areas
contracted further as shown by the limited and sparse distribution of finds, demonstrating a process of
gradual abandonment which would ultimately lead to the complete shift of nucleated settlement to new
places on the nearby hilltops. Even if no such detailed data were collected for the other towns of the
valley, similar general patterns of transformation could be noted there.
 In the later Roman countryside we observed that in the 3rd century, a clear decline both of the existing
settlements and in the creation of new rural units occurred. Although the 4th century seems to document
a weak economic recovery, with a possible reoccupation of previous rural sites and a new intensive
exploitation of the agricultural resources of the landscape, dense frequentation is only well attested
until the second half of the 5th century, at least on the basis on the basis of the latest imported ceramic
evidence. In the upper Potenza valley the more positive fate of the settlements in the countryside reflects
the two nearby Roman towns of Matilica and Camerinum, which even within the changed economic
and historical circumstances remain tightly linked to the substantial persistence of the Roman road
network and local power bases. This area is in some contrast with the situation in the middle and lower
Potenza valley where the towns on the coast (Potentia) and in the valley floor (Ricina, Septempeda)
whose specific topographic position played a role in their decay and eventual total annihilation during
the Middle ages. A combination of a poor strategic position on the coastal plain or in the narrow and
erosion sensitive valley floor, and an altering situation in the soil hydrology, must have been among
the most important causes of the diminishing population of these central Adriatic towns and their
connected rural settlement network in favor of surrounding hilltop sites. On some of these strategic
hilltop sites a new Medieval settlement organisation would develop, with clear reminiscence of the
heyday of Picene culture and former landscape control.

RCrust   Roman crustae
RD       Roman dolium
RG       Roman glass
RhA      Rhodian amphora
RL       Roman lamp
RMo      Roman mosaic
RMort    Roman mortarium
RP       Roman plain ware
RPa      Roman painted ware
RRG      Roman red glaze ware
RS       Roman stucco
Rung     Roman unguentaria
SBM      stone building material (conglomerate, limestone, sandstone)
SGTS     South Gaulish terra sigillata (wheel-turned fine Imperial pottery with a red-
         coloured slip originating from southern Gaul, produced from the Augustan
         period until the end of the 2nd century AD, peaks between c. AD 40 and 100)
T        teeth
TD       terracotta decoration
TW       thin-walled pottery (wheel-turned, fine and sometimes slightly coarse, very
         delicate and thin-walled Roman pottery)
TS       terra sigillata (wheel-turned fine Imperial pottery with a (dark) red-coloured
         slip, typically of Italian origin, produced between c. 30 BC and the end of
         the 1st century AD)
UCo      Undefined coin
UG       Undefined glass
UP       Undefined pottery
